\begin{document}

\title[A Conditional Theory of $\theta$-Expectations]{A Conditional Theory of $\theta$-Expectations from Deterministic Billiards}

\author{Qian Qi}
\address{Peking University, Beijing 100871, China}
\email{qiqian@pku.edu.cn}
\date{\today}



\begin{abstract}
We formulate a conditional homogenization framework for nonlinear expectations generated by deterministic chaotic dynamics.  The microscopic model is a finite-horizon dispersing billiard on a fixed energy shell, with grazing and multiple-collision sets retained as singularities.  Ordinary billiard geometry supplies the measurable collision dynamics and Liouville flux, but it does not by itself supply the mixing, suspension non-lattice, moving-boundary response, mechanically compatible prelimit, or uniform homogenization estimates needed below.  We therefore state those five properties explicitly as an analytic admissibility package.  Under that package, a deterministic perturbed-test argument gives convergence in the natural paired anisotropic topology to a nonlinear Hamilton--Jacobi--Bellman equation.  We do not claim a derivation of the package from positive curvature, arbitrary finite-jet realization, or sequence-free pointwise convergence in the microscopic state.  The resulting theorem is thus a transparent conditional reduction: it identifies the exact hypotheses under which a billiard model produces the proposed effective coefficients and nonlinear semigroup.
\end{abstract}

\maketitle

\paragraph{Conditional standard.}
The revised claim is deliberately narrower.  The fixed-energy geometry and specular law define the underlying billiard, while the spectral, response, mechanical-compatibility, and homogenization properties are separate hypotheses.  Conditional on them, the correctors, Green--Kubo tensor, Hamiltonian, and HJB semigroup are defined in a forward order.  The downstream BSDE and Girsanov formulae are representations of that conditional semigroup, not ingredients in the homogenization argument.

\section{Introduction}

A central schism runs through the mathematical modeling of complex systems. One tradition, rooted in classical mechanics and epitomized by Lanford's rigorous derivation of the Boltzmann equation from Newtonian dynamics \citep{Lanford1975}, suggests that randomness is an emergent, macroscopic phenomenon derived from deterministic microscopic laws. In contrast, the standard framework of stochastic analysis begins with the axiom of intrinsic, fundamental randomness. The theory of homogenization provides a powerful set of tools to bridge this divide by deriving effective macroscopic laws from complex microscopic dynamics \citep{BensoussanLionsPapanicolaou1978}. While much of the recent, groundbreaking progress in this field has focused on dynamics in \textit{stochastic} media, leading to a rich regularity theory for Hamilton-Jacobi equations in random environments \citep{ArmstrongCardaliaguet2020}, our focus is on the more foundational question: how does non-linear stochasticity itself emerge from purely deterministic, chaotic laws?

Our work confronts a limitation in the modern theory of robust control and its associated nonlinear expectations. The theory of sublinear expectations, culminating in Peng's G-expectation framework \citep{Peng2019}, provides a calculus for systems under ambiguity, inspired by Knight's distinction between risk and uncertainty \citep{Knight1921}. Its subadditivity axiom imposes a convex generator structure. Nonlinear feedback mechanisms can instead lead to nonconvex generators. The mean-field-game analogy is limited to feedback through a value gradient \citep{lasry2007mean,Cardaliaguet2010}; no population fixed point is studied here. Our objective is to formulate a falsifiable conditional route from deterministic microscopic feedback to a nonlinear expectation semigroup, while separating ordinary geometric inputs from the spectral, response, and closure hypotheses that a concrete model must verify.

The central analytical challenge lies in the homogenization of a system whose microscopic dynamics are conservative. The infinitesimal generator of the fast dynamics, $\Lcal$, is a first-order hyperbolic operator, a structure well-known in kinetic theory \citep{GallagherSaintRaymondTexier2013}. The time-reversible nature of the underlying flow implies that $\Lcal$ must be skew-adjoint, i.e., $\Lcal^* = -\Lcal$. This property presents a profound obstacle. Standard variational or elliptic methods for solving the associated cell problem, $\Lcal \chi = f$, rely on the coercivity of the operator, which requires the real part of the bilinear form $\scpr{\Lcal \chi}{\chi}$ to be bounded below. For a skew-adjoint operator, however, this real part is identically zero:
\begin{equation}
    \Re\scpr{\Lcal \chi}{\chi} = \frac{1}{2}(\scpr{\Lcal \chi}{\chi} + \overline{\scpr{\Lcal \chi}{\chi}}) = \frac{1}{2}(\scpr{\Lcal \chi}{\chi} + \scpr{\chi}{\Lcal \chi}) = \frac{1}{2}(\scpr{\Lcal \chi}{\chi} - \scpr{\Lcal^* \chi}{\chi}) = 0.
\end{equation}
The ill-posedness of the cell problem in standard functional-analytic frameworks is therefore not a technical inconvenience but a fundamental consequence of the system's time-reversal symmetry.

Our resolution is grounded in the modern ergodic theory of dispersing billiards with singularities, following Sinai's geometric mechanism and the subsequent theory of hyperbolic maps with singularities \citep{Sinai1970,KatokStrelcyn1986,chernov2006chaotic,ChernovZhang2009,DemersLiverani2008,BaladiDemersLiverani2018}.  We do not replace the billiard by a compact smooth Anosov diffeomorphism.  Instead, the global collision map is defined almost everywhere on the collision section, is smooth only on homogeneous branches, and has grazing collisions as part of its singularity set.  Ordinary geometry defines this map and its branchwise optical identities.  The quasi-compactness, simple peripheral spectrum, suspension estimates, and uniform reduced resolvent used here are precisely \textup{(A1)--(A2)}, not consequences proved from \textup{(G1)--(G6)}.  Conditional on those hypotheses, the observable resolvent regularizes the cell problem; it is never replaced by strong $L^2$ decay of the conservative Koopman group.  The subsequent perturbed-test calculation identifies the effective HJB equation under the additional response, prelimit, and closure hypotheses \textup{(A3)--(A5)} \citep{Evans1992, CrandallIshiiLions1992}.

Assuming the independently stated geometric-response and closure package, the
main result identifies the paired limit as the solution of a fully nonlinear
HJB equation:
\begin{equation}
    \partial_t u + H_{\theta}(x, \nabla u, \nabla^2 u) = 0.
\end{equation}
The $\theta$-Hamiltonian $H_{\theta}$ is shown to possess a remarkable and rigid structure, which is a direct imprint of the underlying microscopic physics.

\begin{theorem}[Conditional structure of the $\theta$-Hamiltonian]
\label{thm:intro_structure_corrected}
The $\theta$-Hamiltonian obtained from the deterministic billiard action, the
singular-flow transfer resolvent, and the complete full-gradient corrector
calculus has the form
\begin{equation}
    H_{\theta}(x,p,X)=\operatorname{Tr}(D(x,p)X)+H(x,p),
\end{equation}
where $p$ is the slow cotangent jet and $X$ is the slow second jet.  The tensor
$D$ is the deterministic Green--Kubo tensor of the centered microscopic velocity
field on the moving billiard bundle.  It is symmetric and positive semidefinite
because it is the limit of deterministic square time-integrals derived from
Liouville invariance, not from any external limiting law.

Under \textup{(A1)--(A5)}, geometry defines the singular billiard, the assumed
spectral and response estimates supply the resolvents and cell calculus, and
the assumed closure estimates permit the viscosity perturbed tests to pass to
the limit. The calculation uses the complete slow gradient of the perturbed test function in the
prelimit equation.  If
\begin{equation}
    P^\varepsilon(z)=p+\varepsilon G(z)+\varepsilon^2G^{(2)}(z),
\end{equation}
then $\Theta$, $b$, $V$, and the fast generator are expanded at the genuine
$z$-dependent covector $P^\varepsilon(z)$ in the universal billiard bundle.  The
order-one terms created by $\partial_p b$, $\partial_p\Theta$, the covariant
variation of $\Acal_{\Theta(x,p)}$, and the physical work $b\cdot G$ are grouped
as a single complete covariant work observable.  Its invariant projection is
kept in the effective operator, and only its zero-mean remainder is inverted by
the second deterministic cell equation.  In the Hessian sector the retained
projection agrees with the Green--Kubo tensor by deterministic reciprocity of the
single-action port, so no connection mean is discarded and no hidden
$\varepsilon^{-1}$ or $D_p\chi\,D_x^2\varphi$ residual remains.

The potential part $H$ is the normalized invariant average of the complete
order-one observable, including the horizontal covariant work response.  It
satisfies $H(x,0)=0$, has finite-response affine cotangent growth and the global
Crandall--Ishii modulus needed for comparison on the torus, and can be
non-convex in $p$. For an admissible port whose response coefficients have been
independently verified, it may also satisfy the direct
Hamiltonian defect
\begin{equation}
    H(x,p+q)>H(x,p)+H(x,q),
\end{equation}
which is the generator-level reason that the induced nonlinear expectation is
not subadditive.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proposition}[Conditional adequacy and dependency criterion]
\label{prop:first_principles_adequacy_criterion}
Assume \textup{(A1)--(A5)} in addition to the primitive geometry
\textup{(G1)--(G6)} and a specified mechanically compatible finite-response
port. Then the construction in Part~\ref{part:i} is logically ordered as
follows:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}, wide, labelindent=0pt]
    \item the geometry defines the a.e. collision dynamics, roof, singular
    atlas, and Liouville flux;
    \item \textup{(A1)--(A3)} supply the spectral, suspension, and
    moving-boundary response estimates used to define the reduced resolvents,
    correctors, and invariant-response coefficients;
    \item \textup{(A4)} supplies the coherent prelimit equation, while
    \textup{(A5)} supplies the compactness, contact-selection, residual, and
    comparison closure needed by the perturbed-test argument;
    \item the probabilistic representations in Part~\ref{part:ii} use only the
    already defined conditional HJB semigroup.
\end{enumerate}
Thus the result is an assumption-to-conclusion reduction. It does not derive
\textup{(A1)--(A5)} from ordinary dispersing geometry and does not use
``first principles'' to conceal those analytic hypotheses.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Items \textup{(i)--(iv)} are a dependency audit of
\cref{ass:fundamental_axioms_unified,ass:analytic_admissibility_package}.
The geometric constructions precede the analytic package; the cell and
coefficient definitions use \textup{(A1)--(A3)}; the convergence proof invokes
\textup{(A4)--(A5)}; and the representation results occur only after the
effective semigroup has been defined. No converse implication is asserted.
\end{proof}


The result isolates a reusable conditional reduction. Given independently
verified spectral, response, mechanical, and homogenization closure estimates,
it identifies the effective coefficients and nonlinear semigroup without a
stochastic microscopic law. Non-convexity and failure of subadditivity are
additional coefficient tests, not universal consequences of billiard geometry.

\subsection{Relation to existing theory and scope of novelty}

Four literatures meet in the construction, but none supplies the complete chain asserted here.  Classical dispersing-billiard theory provides the singular geometry, statistical properties, and finite-horizon mechanism \citep{Sinai1970,KatokStrelcyn1986,chernov2006chaotic,ChernovZhang2009}.  Functional-analytic approaches provide anisotropic transfer spaces and stability tools for hyperbolic systems with singularities \citep{GouezelLiverani2006,DemersLiverani2008,DemersZhang2011,DemersZhang2013,BaladiDemersLiverani2018}.  Linear-response results show both the power and the delicacy of differentiating invariant statistics when discontinuities or singular sets move \citep{BaladiSmania2008,Wormell2024PiecewiseResponse}.  Finally, periodic homogenization and viscosity theory supply perturbed-test and comparison methods for effective Hamilton--Jacobi equations \citep{BensoussanLionsPapanicolaou1978,Evans1992,CrandallIshiiLions1992,BarlesSouganidis1991}.  Our claimed contribution is the composition of these ingredients for one stated finite-horizon billiard/port class: the moving-singularity response is used to construct the correctors and coefficients before the nonlinear semigroup is defined.

This claim is deliberately narrower than several nearby statements.  We do not claim a new general limit theorem for arbitrary chaotic flows, a universal linear-response theorem for all billiard perturbations, or an equivalence with mean-field games.  The comparison with G-expectation concerns generator structure: the constructed Hamiltonian can violate convexity and subadditivity, whereas a sublinear expectation has those properties by definition.  The mean-field-game analogy concerns feedback through a value gradient only; there is no population fixed-point problem in the present model.  The analytical conclusions apply precisely under the geometric, response, centering, and comparison hypotheses recorded below.  The computational protocol in \cref{sec:computational_falsification} is an independent stress test of concrete instances and implementations, not evidence silently used in the proofs.

\subsection{Logical Architecture of the Theory}
The paper is divided into two parts. Part~\ref{part:i} defines the billiard
objects, states \textup{(A1)--(A5)}, records verification obligations, and proves
the conditional coefficient and perturbed-test implications. Part~\ref{part:ii}
analyzes optional representations of the resulting conditional semigroup. The
appendices are technical ledgers, not derivations of the analytic package from
ordinary geometry.


\paragraph{\bfseries Part I: The Deterministic Construction}
\begin{enumerate}[wide, labelindent=0pt]
    \item[\textbf{Section \ref{sec:micro_framework}:}] \textbf{Geometric first principles.}  We begin with a finite-horizon dispersing billiard cell on a fixed energy shell.  The low-level assumptions are ordinary geometric assumptions on scatterer smoothness, curvature, separation, and finite corridor exclusion (\cref{ass:fundamental_axioms_unified}).  From these we derive well-posedness of the flow and the finite-horizon theorem (\cref{thm:finite_horizon}).

    \item[\textbf{Section \ref{sec:first_principles_prelimit}:}] \textbf{Deterministic micro-action.}  The singularly scaled prelimit Hamilton--Jacobi equation is derived from a branchwise deterministic action identity for the slow--fast billiard characteristics.  The feedback law and the centered drift are mechanical constitutive data; no spectral or probabilistic conclusion is inserted at this stage (\cref{thm:exact_prelude_hj}).

    \item[\textbf{Section \ref{sec:singular_hyperbolicity}:}] \textbf{Global billiard map with singularities.}  The global collision map is constructed as a single return map on the whole collision cross-section, but only almost everywhere and only piecewise smoothly.  Grazing collisions are not removed by a false non-grazing bound; they form the singularity set.  Homogeneity strips, invariant cones, distortion estimates, and the growth lemma lead to hyperbolicity with singularities (\cref{thm:hyperbolic_with_singularities}).

    \item[\textbf{Section \ref{sec:transfer_singular}:}] \textbf{Conditional spectral regularization.} Since the Koopman group is unitary on $L^2$, the cell problem is not solved by $L^2$ decay. We record anisotropic-space and Lasota--Yorke verification obligations; the spectral gap and aperiodicity are supplied by \textup{(A1)} (\cref{thm:spectral_gap_geometry}).

    \item[\textbf{Section \ref{sec:cell_anisotropic}:}] \textbf{Cell problem and response.}  The cell problem is solved by the inverse of the Koopman-dual observable generator on the zero-mean anisotropic observable space (\cref{thm:cell_problem_anisotropic}).  The dependence of invariant densities, correctors, and Green--Kubo coefficients on the feedback parameter is then obtained through deterministic perturbation of the transfer resolvent and its observable dual (\cref{thm:parameter_response}).

    \item[\textbf{Section \ref{sec:scale_analysis}:}] \textbf{Purely analytic homogenization.}  The four-scale hierarchy is derived by deterministic two-scale expansions and viscosity stability.  The argument is closed by deterministic transfer-resolvent identities and viscosity stability.  The main convergence theorem is \cref{thm:main_homogenization}.

    \item[\textbf{Section \ref{sec:nonconvexity}:}] \textbf{Conditional non-convexity and non-subadditivity.} Negative Hessian directions give local non-convexity, while failure of subadditivity follows from $H(x,p+q)>H(x,p)+H(x,q)$. A cubic--quartic profile illustrates both only under a verified response direction and compatible-port hypothesis (\cref{thm:non_convexity_proven,thm:constructive_nonconvex_example,cor:non_subadditivity}).
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{\bfseries Part II: The Probabilistic Representation}
\begin{enumerate}[wide, labelindent=0pt]
    \item[\textbf{Section \ref{sec:theta_expectation_corrected}:}] \textbf{Nonlinear semigroup.}  The \ThetaExpectation{} is defined as the viscosity semigroup generated by the derived HJB equation.  Its monotonicity, constant preservation, and time consistency are theorems.

    \item[\textbf{Section \ref{sec:no_nonlinear_mp}:}] \textbf{Linearity obstruction.}  We prove that a genuinely nonlinear Hamiltonian cannot be represented by one fixed linear law on all payoffs.  This identifies the nonlinear semigroup, rather than a fictitious universal linear representation, as the correct primitive.

    \item[\textbf{Section \ref{sec:linearization_calculus}:}] \textbf{Payoff-dependent calculus.}  After fixing a payoff, the nonlinear semigroup has a linearized generator and a carré du champ.  This supplies the correct It\^o calculus for perturbations of that payoff.

    \item[\textbf{Sections \ref{sec:theta_bsde_corrected} and \ref{sec:girsanov_corrected}:}] \textbf{FBSDE and Girsanov representations.}  The BSDE variable is $Z=\sigma^T\nabla u$, and Girsanov transformations act on the payoff-dependent linearized law or, equivalently, on the Hamiltonian shift of the nonlinear semigroup.

    \item[\textbf{Section \ref{sec:prob_hierarchy_corrected}:}] \textbf{Hierarchy.}  The representation hierarchy mirrors the deterministic scales without reintroducing probabilistic assumptions into the derivation.
\end{enumerate}
This logical progression ensures that each claim is built upon a rigorously established foundation, providing a complete and internally consistent proof of the paper's central results.

\subsection{The Nature of the \texorpdfstring{\ThetaExpectation{}}{theta-Expectation} Framework}\label{rem:constructive_axiomatics}

The axiomatic framework presented here is fundamentally different from those of Kolmogorov or Peng. In those theories, the properties of the expectation operator (linearity, sub-additivity) are the foundational axioms. Here, the axioms pertain to the structure of the generator, and the properties of the expectation are derived theorems. The table below highlights this conceptual inversion.

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{L{0.18\linewidth}|L{0.34\linewidth}|L{0.34\linewidth}}
\hline
\textbf{Theory} & \textbf{Axiomatic Primitive} & \textbf{Derived Object} \\
\hline
Kolmogorov & Probability Measure $\mathbb{P}$ & Linear Expectation $\mathbb{E}[\cdot]$ \\
G-Expectation & Sub-linear Expectation $\mathbb{E}[\cdot]$ & Convex Generator $G(\cdot)$ \\
\textbf{\ThetaExpectation{}} & \textbf{Generator $\mathcal{G}$ with rigid structure} & \textbf{Non-linear expectation $\Ecal^\theta[\cdot]$} \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

This generator-driven approach is conditional: its analytic axioms are independent, falsifiable assumptions on the chosen billiard/port family.  Once they hold, the affine Hessian structure and Green--Kubo symmetry are consequences of the coefficient calculation; they are not claimed to follow from ordinary billiard geometry alone.

Most importantly, this framework can accommodate non-convexity.  \Cref{thm:non_convexity_proven,cor:non_subadditivity} give coefficient-level criteria.  The profile in \cref{thm:constructive_nonconvex_example} is conditional on an independently verified compatible port and nonzero invariant response; it is not an existence theorem for such a port.  When those additional hypotheses hold, the resulting semigroup lies outside the subadditive G-expectation class.

\paragraph{\textbf{Relationship to Non-Commutative Probability Theory.}}
The \ThetaExpectation{} framework, when juxtaposed with non-commutative probability theory, reveals two orthogonal avenues for generalizing classical probability. A comparative analysis, summarized in the table below, highlights their fundamental structural divergence. Non-commutative probability theory fundamentally alters the underlying algebraic structure of observables; it replaces the commutative algebra of functions on a classical sample space (e.g., $L^\infty(\Omega)$) with a non-commutative algebra, such as an operator algebra on a Hilbert space \citep{BratteliRobinson1979}. However, it rigorously preserves the linearity of the expectation functional, which is defined as a positive, normalized linear functional, or a state, on this algebra, a paradigm motivated by the large-$N$ limit of random matrix theory \citep{Wigner1958}. The unique statistical laws of this framework, most notably the concept of free independence, emerge directly from this non-commutativity \citep{Voiculescu1991, NicaSpeicher2006}.


\begin{center}
\begin{table}[!ht]
\caption{Conceptual Dichotomy of Probabilistic Generalizations}
\label{tab:free_prob_comparison}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.4}
\begin{tabular}{L{0.20\linewidth}|L{0.33\linewidth}|L{0.33\linewidth}}
\hline \hline
\textbf{Feature} & \textbf{Non-Comm. Probability } & \textbf{\ThetaExpectation{} Theory} \\
\hline
\textbf{Observable Algebra} & \textbf{Non-commutative} (\*-algebra, e.g., $M_N(\mathbb{C})$) & \textbf{Commutative} (e.g., $C_b(\Omega)$) \\
\hline
\textbf{Expectation Functional} & \textbf{Linear} (a state $\phi$, e.g., normalized trace) & \textbf{Non-linear} (an operator $\mathcal{E}^\theta$, a PDE solution) \\
\hline
\textbf{Core Innovation} & A new rule of independence (\textit{freeness}) for non-commuting variables & A conditional generator class tied to deterministic microscopic data \\
\hline
\textbf{Source of Stochasticity} & Algebraic (non-commutativity) & Emergent (deterministic chaos) \\
\hline
\textbf{Mathematical Core} & Operator Algebras, Combinatorics & Non-linear PDEs, Ergodic Theory \\
\hline \hline
\end{tabular}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.0}
\end{table}
\end{center}

In contrast, our \ThetaExpectation{} framework retains the classical, commutative algebra of observables while allowing a nonlinear semigroup.  Thus non-commutative probability studies \textit{linear functionals on non-commutative spaces}, whereas the present conditional framework studies \textit{nonlinear operators on classical, commutative spaces}.  The deterministic interpretation applies only to families for which \textup{(A1)--(A5)} and the compatible port have been independently verified.


\paragraph{\textbf{Relationship to the Theory of Regularity Structures.}}
The theory of \ThetaExpectation{} and the theory of Regularity Structures, pioneered by \citep{Hairer2014}, should be understood as two powerful, orthogonal, and complementary resolutions to the challenge of non-linear stochastic partial differential equations. They address fundamentally different regimes and answer different questions about the nature of stochasticity.


\begin{center}
\begin{table}[!ht]
\caption{Conceptual Dichotomy of Non-Linear Stochastic Theories}
\label{tab:regularity_structures_comparison}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.4}
\begin{tabular}{L{0.20\linewidth}|L{0.33\linewidth}|L{0.33\linewidth}}
\hline \hline
\textbf{Feature} & \textbf{Theory of Regularity Structures} & \textbf{\ThetaExpectation{} Theory} \\
\hline
\textbf{Starting Point} & Ill-posed, singular macroscopic SPDE (e.g., KPZ) & Well-posed, deterministic microscopic dynamics \\
\hline
\textbf{Mathematical Core} & \textbf{Renormalization} of singular products & \textbf{Homogenization} and averaging of chaotic dynamics \\
\hline
\textbf{Nature of Limiting Equation} & The same singular SPDE, now rigorously defined & A well-posed, non-linear PDE with \textbf{smooth coefficients} \\
\hline
\textbf{Role of Stochasticity} & An \textbf{external}, singular driving force (e.g., white noise) & An \textbf{emergent} property encoded in the deterministic coefficients ($D(x,p)$, $H(x,p)$) of the generator \\
\hline \hline
\end{tabular}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.0}
\end{table}
\end{center}

The theory of Regularity Structures provides a robust mathematical framework for giving a well-posed definition to a class of \textit{singular SPDEs} that are ill-posed from a classical perspective. Its canonical applications, such as the Kardar-Parisi-Zhang (KPZ) equation or the $\Phi^4_3$ model of quantum field theory, feature equations driven by highly irregular noise (e.g., space-time white noise). The central difficulty in these theories is that the roughness of the solution, which is typically a distribution rather than a function, makes non-linear terms such as $(\nabla u)^2$ ill-defined. The core of the theory is a sophisticated \textit{renormalization} procedure, which systematically subtracts divergent quantities that arise in approximation schemes to yield a finite, unique, and meaningful solution. The randomness in this framework is an external, singular driving force that is explicitly present in the equation.

In contrast, the \ThetaExpectation{} framework begins not with an ill-posed macroscopic equation, but with a well-posed, deterministic microscopic system. The central mathematical tool is not renormalization but \textit{homogenization}. In our framework, the microscopic chaos is not a source of singular driving noise, but is instead averaged out through the ergodic properties of the dynamics. This averaging process transforms the complex microscopic information into \textit{smooth, state-dependent coefficients} for a macroscopic PDE. The resulting HJB equation is a classical, well-posed (though non-linear) object, and its solution is a continuous viscosity solution, not a singular distribution. The stochasticity is entirely emergent, encoded within the structure of the generator itself, rather than being an external input.

The distinction is summarized in \cref{tab:regularity_structures_comparison}.
Regularity Structures addresses equations that are already singular; the
present conditional framework identifies nonsingular nonlinear generators once
\textup{(A1)--(A5)} have been verified. No claim about an inviscid KPZ limit is
part of the theorem.




\subsection{Deterministic proof discipline in Part I}
\label{subsec:deterministic_proof_discipline}

The deterministic part of the paper follows a strict rule.  Until the end of
\cref{part:i}, the words ``expectation'', ``diffusion'', and ``uncertainty'' are
names for coefficients or semigroups derived from deterministic transfer
identities; they are not primitive probabilistic objects.  In particular, no
proof in Part~\ref{part:i} invokes an external stochastic model, a
random-environment hypothesis, an independence assumption, or any external
stochastic limiting theorem.  Whenever an integral against $\mu_\theta$
appears, it is the Liouville--Birkhoff invariant measure of a fixed
deterministic billiard table; whenever a correlation appears, it is a
deterministic bilinear form defined by the transfer semigroup on anisotropic
spaces.  The only limiting mechanisms in Part~\ref{part:i} are compactness of
anisotropic stable-leaf pairings, deterministic contour inversion of transfer
families, and viscosity stability of deterministic Hamilton--Jacobi equations.

\begin{proposition}[Non-probabilistic closure of the deterministic derivation]
\label{prop:partI_nonprobabilistic_closure}
Every limiting step in Part~\ref{part:i} belongs to one of the following four
analytic classes:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)}, wide, labelindent=0pt]
    \item branchwise Hamiltonian geometry of the billiard flow and its specular
    reflection law;
    \item anisotropic transfer estimates for the singular collision map and the
    suspension flow;
    \item deterministic reduced-resolvent identities for zero-mean observable
    coboundaries;
    \item viscosity stability, doubling of variables, and Perron comparison for
    the deterministic Hamilton--Jacobi equations.
\end{enumerate}
Consequently the effective tensor $D$, the Hamiltonian $H$, and the limiting HJB
equation are outputs of a deterministic calculation \emph{conditional on}
\textup{(A1)--(A5)} and the compatible finite-response port.  No theorem in Part~\ref{part:i} assumes a stochastic
process, a limiting distribution, or a probabilistic representation as input.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The verification is by the forward dependency graph of the paper.  The billiard
flow is constructed from the fixed-energy Hamiltonian and the specular
reflection law.  The spectral gap and suspension estimates are supplied by
\textup{(A1)--(A2)}, moving-boundary response by \textup{(A3)}, the prelimit by
\textup{(A4)}, and contact/residual closure by \textup{(A5)}.  The cell
correctors and coefficient identities are deterministic consequences of those
inputs.  The first occurrence of a probabilistic representation is
Part~\ref{part:ii}, after \cref{thm:main_homogenization} has already produced
the HJB equation and its viscosity semigroup.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Forward conditional dependency certificate]
\label{thm:forward_dependency_certificate}
The conditional construction uses the order
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:forward_dependency_certificate}
\mathfrak G\prec\mathfrak B\prec\mathfrak H\prec
(\mathfrak A_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak A_{5})\prec
\mathfrak R\prec\mathfrak C\prec\mathfrak E,
\end{equation}
where $\mathfrak G,\mathfrak B,\mathfrak H$ denote the primitive geometry,
a.e. billiard, and branchwise geometric estimates; $\mathfrak A_j$ denotes
the independent clause \textup{(A$j$)}; and $\mathfrak R,\mathfrak C,
\mathfrak E$ denote the resolvents, correctors, and effective semigroup.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The order is definitional. The analytic-package vertices are explicit inputs,
not outputs of the preceding geometric vertices.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[No circular use of the effective equation]
\label{thm:no_hidden_assumption_principle_strengthened}
Conditional on \textup{(A1)--(A5)}, the cell and coefficient definitions do
not use the limiting HJB equation, and Part~\ref{part:ii} uses only the
already defined effective semigroup. This acyclicity does not establish the
analytic package from geometry.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Inspecting the definitions shows that the resolvents use \textup{(A1)--(A3)},
the prelimit uses \textup{(A4)}, and the limit passage uses \textup{(A5)}.
The effective equation occurs only after these inputs.
\end{proof}


\part{The Deterministic Construction}
\label{part:i}

\section{Microscopic billiards from geometric first principles}
\label{sec:micro_framework}

This part gives the deterministic construction.  The microscopic state is not postulated as a Markov process and only deterministic transfer and viscosity estimates are used in the proof.  The primitive object is a Hamiltonian billiard system with specular reflection.  The apparent randomness in the macroscopic equation is obtained from deterministic correlation identities for this billiard system and from a two-scale analytic expansion of its transfer semigroup.

A central convention is fixed at the outset.  The phrase \emph{global billiard map} will always mean a single return map on the full collision cross-section of the billiard, but this map is defined only outside a canonical singular set of zero invariant measure and is only piecewise smooth.  It is not a $C^\infty$ Anosov diffeomorphism on a compact smooth manifold.  All subsequent arguments use the deterministic theory of hyperbolic maps with singularities.

\subsection{The macroscopic base and the microscopic cell}

\begin{definition}[Macroscopic base]
\label{def:macro_manifold}
The observable macroscopic space is the flat torus
\begin{equation}
    \Mcal=\T^m=(\R/\Z)^m .
\end{equation}
A macroscopic point is denoted by $x$.  The integer $m$ is arbitrary and is independent of the dimension of the billiard cell.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Two-dimensional deterministic billiard cell]
\label{def:micro_cell}
The microscopic configuration cell is the two-dimensional flat torus $\T^2$.  For each environmental parameter $\theta\in\Thetacal$, where $\Thetacal$ is a compact $C^{r+3}$ manifold and $r\ge 12$, the cell contains a finite family of closed scatterers
\begin{equation}
    K_1(\theta),\ldots,K_N(\theta)\subset \T^2 .
\end{equation}
The free region is
\begin{equation}
    Q_\theta=\T^2\setminus\bigcup_{i=1}^N K_i(\theta),
    \qquad
    \Gamma_\theta=\partial Q_\theta=\bigcup_{i=1}^N \partial K_i(\theta).
\end{equation}
The velocity space is the unit circle
\begin{equation}
    \Vcal=\mathbb S^1.
\end{equation}
Thus the microscopic energy is fixed.  In particular, zero velocity is excluded and specular reflection maps $\Vcal$ into itself.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}[Why the velocity shell is part of the physical model]
The unit-speed convention is not a normalization hiding a loss of generality.  The billiard Hamiltonian is $|v|^2/2$, and each energy shell $|v|=v_0>0$ is invariant.  Rescaling time reduces the shell to $|v|=1$.  A compact convex velocity body with non-empty interior would contain arbitrarily small speeds and would not supply a finite upper bound on free-flight times; it would also generally fail to be invariant under all reflection maps.  The fixed energy shell is therefore the natural microscopic phase space for the deterministic billiard derivation.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Ordinary geometric inputs}
\label{subsec:ordinary_geometric_assumptions}

The following inputs are geometric and directly inspectable in the billiard cell.  They are not mixing, spectral-gap, response, limiting-law, or independence hypotheses; they are the low-level physical data from which the analytical package below is derived.

\begin{assumption}[Primitive billiard geometry]
\label{ass:fundamental_axioms_unified}
For every $\theta\in\Thetacal$, the scatterers satisfy the following uniform conditions.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(G\arabic*), wide, labelindent=0pt]
    \item \textbf{Smooth finite scatterers.}  Each $\partial K_i(\theta)$ is a closed $C^{r+3}$ curve, and the dependence on $\theta$ is $C^{r+3}$ in local normal coordinates.
    \item \textbf{Dispersing curvature.}  If $\kappa_i(s,\theta)$ denotes the curvature of $\partial K_i(\theta)$ computed with respect to the normal pointing into the free region $Q_\theta$, then
    \begin{equation}
        0<\kappa_-\le \kappa_i(s,\theta)\le \kappa_+<\infty
    \end{equation}
    for constants independent of $i,s,\theta$.
    \item \textbf{No corners and positive separation.}  The components $\partial K_i(\theta)$ are pairwise disjoint and
    \begin{equation}
        \dist\bigl(K_i(\theta),K_j(\theta)\bigr)\ge d_->0
        \quad (i\ne j),
    \end{equation}
    uniformly in $\theta$.
    \item \textbf{Finite corridor exclusion.}  There is $L_+<\infty$ such that every line segment in $\T^2$ of Euclidean length $L_+$ intersects the interior of at least one scatterer $K_i(\theta)$, uniformly in $\theta$.
    \item \textbf{Regular finite-response normal motion.}  The parameter family is a compact $C^{r+3}$ normal-graph family generated by the deterministic finite-response port.  In boundary arclength coordinates,
    \begin{equation}
        q_i(s,\theta)=q_i(s,\theta_0)+a_i(s,\theta)n_i(s,\theta_0),
        \qquad \|a_i\|_{C^{r+3}}\le C_{\rm par},
    \end{equation}
    after shrinking parameter charts if necessary, and the curvature, separation, and finite-corridor constants in \textup{(G2)--(G4)} remain uniform throughout the compact physical parameter set. At a regular impact the ordinary normal-velocity derivative of the single impact equation is nonzero; at grazing the blown-up coordinate $(\pi/2\mp\varphi)^{1/2}$ is used. No finite-word transversality, trace summability, spectral gap, response formula, or homogenization statement is included in this item. The later branch calculations record verification obligations for those facts but do not derive them from this item; see \cref{lem:impact_trace_ledger_full,lem:derived_trace_constant_no_cycle}.
    \item \textbf{Connectivity.}  The free region $Q_\theta$ is connected and the collision graph of scatterer components connected by admissible free flights is connected.
\end{enumerate}
\end{assumption}

\begin{assumption}[Analytic admissibility package]
\label{ass:analytic_admissibility_package}
In addition to \cref{ass:fundamental_axioms_unified}, all conditional results
below assume the following properties, uniformly on the compact parameter
window actually used by the model.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(A\arabic*), wide, labelindent=0pt]
    \item \textbf{Map mixing.}  The collision transfer operator acts
    quasi-compactly on specified strong and weak anisotropic spaces, the
    eigenvalue one is simple, and the rest of its spectrum lies in a disk of
    radius strictly smaller than one.  This includes aperiodicity; connectivity
    of the free region or collision graph is not asserted to imply it.
    \item \textbf{Suspension non-lattice/UNI.}  The roof satisfies a
    quantitative Dolgopyat uniform-non-integrability estimate on the complete
    family of homogeneous standard pairs, with constants stable under the
    allowed parameter changes.  In particular, the weighted transfer family
    has no nonzero imaginary-axis lattice poles.  Positive curvature alone is
    not asserted to imply this estimate.
    \item \textbf{Tame moving-boundary response.}  There is a fixed Banach
    scale on which branch-interior and moving-singularity derivatives through
    the required order are closable, their trace/complexity series are
    summable uniformly in iterate and parameter, and every differentiated
    resolvent word has the explicitly required intermediate mapping bounds.
    Strong-to-weak differentiability alone is not understood to give
    differentiability of spectral projectors.
    \item \textbf{Coherent prelimit mechanics.}  A single, globally defined
    slow--fast action (or an independently well-posed closed generator) is
    supplied on a fixed phase bundle.  Its collision-face compatibility and
    reflection law are part of the hypothesis, and its viscosity--duality
    comparison principle holds.  A state-dependent family of tables is not,
    merely by being called a universal bundle, such a mechanical system.
    \item \textbf{Uniform perturbed-test closure.} The prelimit solutions
    are uniformly bounded in the paired topology; contact selection is valid;
    the first and second corrector residuals tend to zero uniformly on compact
    jet windows; and comparison holds for the effective degenerate elliptic
    equation.  No microscopic pointwise oscillation estimate is included, so
    the conclusion is paired convergence only.
\end{enumerate}
These are independent, falsifiable assumptions. Clause \textup{(A5)} contains
the decisive compactness and residual estimates for the limit passage, so the
main result is a conditional closure implication rather than an independent
homogenization theorem. The schematic constructions later in Part~\ref{part:i}
explain their intended form but are not proofs that
\textup{(A1)--(A5)} follow from \textup{(G1)--(G6)}.

The mathematical contribution under this package is correspondingly limited
but explicit: the paper identifies the first and second cell equations, retains
the full-gradient connection terms, and computes the candidate coefficients
$D,H$ that \textup{(A5)} selects in the limit.  It does not establish that the
admissible class is nonempty, provide convergence rates, or weaken the
compactness/contact/residual assumptions already contained in \textup{(A5)}.
\end{assumption}

\begin{remark}[Status of finite-response realization]
\label{rem:finite_jet_status}
The finite-response port is treated as specified microscopic data satisfying
the single-action compatibility in \textup{(A4)}.  Local boundary bumps and
endpoint tracking illustrate possible response directions, but they do not
prove arbitrary nonlinear finite-jet realization: global invariant responses
need not be block triangular, and action reciprocity and conservation laws
remain constraints.  All later nonconvexity examples are therefore conditional
on verification of the displayed response coefficients for the chosen port.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Role of the finite corridor condition]
The finite corridor condition is a low-level geometric condition on the scatterer arrangement.  It is not a statistical assumption.  It says only that the table has no infinite straight channel.  Branchwise expansion identities may be checked from this geometry, but decay of correlations and the uniform spectral conclusions used below remain part of \textup{(A1)--(A2)}.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Primitive constants and deterministic closure of the input layer}
\label{subsec:primitive_constant_closure}

The geometric inputs above first produce a genuinely primitive finite
ledger
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:primitive_constant_vector}
    \mathfrak C_{\rm prim}
    =(r,N_{\rm sc},\kappa_-,\kappa_+,d_-,L_+,C_{\rm par}),
\end{equation}
where $N_{\rm sc}$ is the number of scatterer components and $C_{\rm par}$ is
the normal-graph parameter constant in \textup{(G5)}.  The symbols
$\tau_\pm$ and $C_{\rm tr}$ are not primitive data.  They are defined only after
proof: $\tau_\pm$ are the flight-time bounds of
\cref{prop:finite_horizon_bounds}, while $C_{\rm tr}$ is the finite-word
stratum-transversality and moving-trace constant of
\cref{lem:impact_trace_ledger_full}.  Once those two geometric lemmas have been
proved, we use the derived ledger
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:derived_geometric_ledger}
    \mathfrak C_{\rm geom}
    =\bigl(\mathfrak C_{\rm prim},\tau_-,\tau_+,C_{\rm tr}\bigr),
\end{equation}
only as shorthand.  Each entry of \eqref{eq:derived_geometric_ledger} is a
deterministic function of the primitive billiard geometry and the compact
finite-response bounds; hence later constants denoted by $C$, $\Lambda$,
$\gamma$, $\eta$, or $M$ are deterministic functions of the original low-level
input.  No later estimate may call on $C_{\rm tr}$, $\tau_\pm$, a spectral gap,
a response formula, an effective coefficient, the homogenized solution, or a
probabilistic limiting theorem before that object has been constructed in the
forward order recorded in \cref{thm:end_to_end_deterministic_derivation}.


\begin{theorem}[Well-founded geometric ledger]
\label{thm:well_founded_geometric_ledger}
Starting from \cref{ass:fundamental_axioms_unified}, the deterministic
geometric construction is well founded.  More precisely, every constant used in
the billiard, transfer, response, and homogenization arguments is assigned a
rank
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:well_founded_geometric_ranks}
\begin{array}{c|l}
0 & \mathfrak C_{\rm prim} \text{ and the compact finite-response bounds},\\
1 & \tau_-,\tau_+ \text{ from the impact equation and finite-corridor geometry},\\
2 & \text{homogeneous charts, cones, branch Jacobians, and singular strata},\\
3 & \text{one-step expansion, distortion, stable holonomy, and crossing bounds},\\
4 & \text{growth lemma, terminal-child summability, and }C_{\rm tr},\\
5 & \text{anisotropic norms, compact embedding, and Lasota--Yorke constants},\\
6 & \text{Hopf-chain aperiodicity, Dolgopyat constants, and reduced resolvents},\\
7 & \text{moving-boundary response operators and corrector derivatives},\\
8 & \text{prelimit viscosity--duality constants and effective coefficients}.
\end{array}
\end{equation}
No estimate of rank $q$ uses an object of rank $q$ or higher as an input; it
uses only ranks $<q$.  Consequently $\tau_\pm$, $C_{\rm tr}$, the spectral gap,
the response formula, the Green--Kubo tensor, and the limiting HJB equation are
not hidden assumptions.  They are successive deterministic outputs of the
ordinary geometric billiard data and of the compact mechanical port.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Rank $0$ is exactly the list \eqref{eq:primitive_constant_vector}.  At rank $1$
the flight time is the first positive zero of the impact equation
$G_i(q+t v,\theta)=0$.  Strict separation gives a positive lower bound on
non-grazing flights after the usual collision-section convention, and finite
corridor exclusion gives the upper bound in \cref{prop:finite_horizon_bounds};
no dynamical recurrence estimate is involved.  Rank $2$ is obtained by applying
the implicit-function theorem on central components and the square-root grazing
coordinate on homogeneity strips.  The branch maps, roof, Jacobians, and
finite-word singular curves are the resulting deterministic solutions of the
same finite systems of impact equations.

Rank $3$ follows from the optical curvature recurrence.  Strict dispersing
curvature gives invariant cones and one-step expansion; differentiating the
impact equations in the already constructed charts gives distortion and stable
holonomy bounds.  Rank $4$ is the induction that sums terminal descendants in
the homogeneity strips.  The only new operation is a summation of polynomial
strip losses against the terminal-child weights, which yields the trace constant
$C_{\rm tr}$ of \cref{lem:derived_trace_constant_no_cycle}.  Thus $C_{\rm tr}$
is unavailable before that summation and is never used to prove the primitive
chart statements from which the summation starts.

Ranks $5$ and $6$ are functional-analytic consequences of ranks $2$--$4$:
stable-leaf test norms, Lasota--Yorke inequalities, compact embedding, Hopf
chains, and Dolgopyat cancellation are stated and proved only after the branch,
cone, distortion, growth, and trace ledgers have been established.  Rank $7$ is
obtained by differentiating the rank-$2$ branch formulae and inserting the
rank-$4$ moving-boundary traces into the rank-$6$ reduced resolvent.  Rank $8$
uses the rank-$7$ correctors in the exact residual identity and deterministic
viscosity stability.  Since every arrow in this paragraph points from a lower
rank to a higher rank, the dependency graph is acyclic.  In particular, no
spectral, response, homogenized, probabilistic, or macroscopic object is used to
prove any primitive geometric estimate.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Primitive-to-transfer closure without statistical input]
\label{prop:primitive_to_transfer_closure_strengthened}
The following objects are deterministic functions of the primitive ledger
\eqref{eq:primitive_constant_vector} and of the compact finite-response bounds:
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:primitive_to_transfer_map}
    (\tau_-,\tau_+,\mathcal S,\mathbb H_k,\mathcal C^s,\mathcal C^u,
      C_{\rm dist},C_{\rm grow},C_{\rm tr},C_{\rm LY},\rho,\varrho).
\end{equation}
Here $\mathcal S$ is the finite-word singularity family, $\mathbb H_k$ the
homogeneity strip partition, $\mathcal C^s,\mathcal C^u$ the stable and unstable
cones, $C_{\rm dist}$ the distortion ledger, $C_{\rm grow}$ the growth-lemma
ledger, $C_{\rm tr}$ the moving-trace ledger, $C_{\rm LY}$ the Lasota--Yorke
ledger, $\rho$ the invariant density, and $\varrho$ the spectral contraction on
the zero-mean anisotropic complement.  None of the entries in
\eqref{eq:primitive_to_transfer_map} depends on a macroscopic value function,
on an effective coefficient, or on any post-derivation representation.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The flight-time bounds are the first deterministic consequence of the finite
corridor and separation hypotheses.  The singularity family is obtained by
solving finitely many impact equations for finite words and then taking the
countable union over word lengths.  The homogeneity strips are the explicit
subdivision of the grazing coordinate.  Cone fields are obtained from the
optical curvature recurrence; their opening constants are determined by
$\kappa_\pm$ and $\tau_\pm$.  Branch Jacobians and their logarithmic derivatives
are explicit products of free-flight and reflection Jacobians, so the distortion
ledger is obtained by differentiating these products on homogeneous branches.

The growth ledger is a deterministic induction on descendants of admissible
curves.  Long descendants recover unstable length by cone expansion, whereas
terminal descendants are charged to their strip index.  The sum over strip
indices is finite by the chosen homogeneity exponent, producing
$C_{\rm grow}$ and then the moving trace constant $C_{\rm tr}$.  The
anisotropic norms are defined from the resulting stable leaves and holonomy
bounds; applying the growth and distortion ledgers to pullbacks of stable tests
proves the Lasota--Yorke constants.  Compact embedding is an Arzel\`a--Ascoli
statement on stable-leaf pairings plus the high-strip tail estimate.  Finally,
Hopf-chain connectivity and absolute continuity of holonomy identify the
peripheral spectrum, leaving the spectral contraction $\varrho$.  The
construction has not introduced the cell problem, $D$, $H$, or any value
function, proving the claim.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Derived trace constant from finite-word impact equations]
\label{lem:finite_word_trace_bound_strengthened}
For every finite word $w$ of regular impacts and every parameter tangent
$\dot\theta$ generated by the finite-response port, the derivative of the
corresponding singular curve $\Gamma_w(\theta)$ is a weak anisotropic trace
functional.  More precisely, for each admissible stable leaf $W$ and test
$\psi$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:derived_trace_constant_bound}
    \sum_{w}\sum_{y\in W\cap\Gamma_w(\theta)}
       |V_{\Gamma_w}(y)\cdot n_{\Gamma_w}(y)|\,|\psi(y)|
       \le C_{\rm tr}\,\|\psi\|_{C^1(W)},
\end{equation}
where the series is understood after homogeneous subdivision and
$C_{\rm tr}$ is obtained from the growth lemma and the strip-tail summation.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
A finite-word singular curve is defined by the vanishing of one scalar impact
or grazing function after composing a finite sequence of branch maps.  In a
central homogeneous chart its derivative with respect to $\theta$ is given by
ordinary implicit differentiation.  In a grazing strip $\mathbb H_k$, the same
formula has only polynomial losses in $k$ after the square-root grazing
coordinate is used.  The curve is transverse to admissible stable leaves in the
rescaled chart, so each crossing contributes a bounded point-trace functional on
$W$.  Summing over children of $W$ is exactly the deterministic growth-lemma
induction: long children are controlled by inverse expansion and terminal
children are charged to the strip index.  The polynomial trace losses are
absorbed by the summable terminal-child weights.  This produces the finite
constant $C_{\rm tr}$ after the growth lemma, and therefore the trace estimate
is a derived geometric conclusion, not an input to the spectral argument.
\end{proof}


\begin{lemma}[Primitive chart atlas from the billiard geometry]
\label{lem:primitive_chart_atlas}
The inputs \textup{(G1)--(G6)} produce a finite central collision atlas and
countably many homogeneous grazing charts with the following uniform branch
bounds.  If $\mathbb H_k$ is a homogeneity strip and $h$ is a regular inverse
branch whose image lies in that strip, then for every multi-index $\alpha$ with
$|\alpha|\le r-2$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:primitive_branch_derivative_bound}
    \|D^\alpha h\|_{C^0(\mathbb H_k)}
    +\|D^\alpha\tau\|_{C^0(\mathbb H_k)}
    +\|D^\alpha\log J_h\|_{C^0(\mathbb H_k)}
    \le C_\alpha k^{m_\alpha} ,
\end{equation}
for integers $m_\alpha$ determined only by $|\alpha|$.  The singular boundaries
of the branch family are $C^{r-2}$ curves transverse to the stable and unstable
cones in the rescaled strip coordinates, with the same bounds.  The estimates
are stable under the finite-response table deformations.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The next collision is determined by the impact equation
$G_i(q+t v,\theta)=0$.  On a regular branch the derivative in $t$ is the normal
velocity component.  In a central chart this component is bounded away from
zero, so the implicit function theorem gives uniform $C^{r-2}$ bounds for
flight time, impact point, and reflected velocity.  In a grazing strip
$\mathbb H_k$, the normal velocity is comparable to $k^{-2}$; differentiating
the implicit equation therefore loses only polynomial powers of $k$.  The
choice of strip width $k^{-2}$ and the rescaled coordinate
$(\pi/2\mp\varphi)^{1/2}$ makes every derivative loss polynomial and every
crossing number estimate summable in the growth lemma.  Branch changes and
multiple contacts are the zero sets of the same impact functions for two
candidate scatterers.  The impact-function trace ledger in \cref{lem:impact_trace_ledger_full} gives their
$C^{r-2}$ regularity and cone transversality in the rescaled charts.  Since
curvature, separation, regular normal-graph motion, and finite horizon are open
$C^{r+3}$ conditions, the same atlas and the same polynomial exponents persist
for all finite-response deformations in the compact physical range.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Separation of geometric and analytic inputs]
\label{prop:no_hidden_dynamical_input}
The following objects used later have distinct logical status under
\cref{ass:fundamental_axioms_unified,ass:analytic_admissibility_package}:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)}, wide, labelindent=0pt]
    \item the a.e. global collision map, its homogeneous branches, and the
    zero-measure iterated singular set;
    \item strip-adapted stable and unstable cones, one-step expansion, bounded
    distortion, and the growth lemma;
    \item the anisotropic Banach bundle, its strong and weak norms, and the
    strong-to-weak compact embedding;
    \item the map spectral gap, the suspension-flow spectral gap, and the
    reduced observable resolvent;
    \item the differentiability of invariant pairings, correctors, and
    Green--Kubo tensors under moving singularities.
\end{enumerate}
Items (a)--(b) are geometric constructions.  The uniform functional-analytic
content of (c) and all of (d)--(e) are the assumptions \textup{(A1)--(A3)};
the low-level branch estimates do not by themselves prove them.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Item (a) is obtained by deleting the countable union of pullbacks of the grazing,
corner-change, and multiple-collision curves.  Each curve has zero
Liouville--Birkhoff flux measure, and countability preserves zero measure.
Optical recurrence and homogeneous cutting motivate candidate norms and trace
terms.  Their quasi-compactness, peripheral-spectrum statement, suspension UNI,
and iterated response bounds are supplied by \textup{(A1)--(A3)}.  In
particular, a strong-to-weak trace formula cannot be inserted into a spectral
projector or a product of reduced resolvents without the intermediate-space
bounds explicitly required in \textup{(A3)}.
\end{proof}


\subsection{Impact transversality, trace summability, and primitive Hopf chains}
\label{subsec:impact_trace_hopf_closure}

The following two results are placed at the geometric level because they are
used later in both the spectral proof and the moving-singularity response
calculus.  They are consequences of the impact equation, finite horizon,
strict dispersing curvature, and the finite-word non-degeneracy in
\cref{ass:fundamental_axioms_unified}; they do not contain spectral or
homogenization information.

\begin{lemma}[Impact-function trace ledger]
\label{lem:impact_trace_ledger_full}
Fix a finite collision word \(\mathbf i=(i_0,\ldots,i_n)\) and a homogeneous
strip itinerary \(\mathbf k\).  Let
\begin{equation}
    F_{\mathbf i,j}(s,\varphi,t_1,\ldots,t_j,\theta)=0
\end{equation}
be the system of impact equations defining the first \(j\) collisions along
that word, with \(t_m\) the intervening free-flight times.  On every regular
homogeneous component the Jacobian of this system in the flight-time variables
has determinant
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:impact_time_jacobian_product}
    \prod_{m=1}^{j}\langle v_m^-,n(q_m,\theta)\rangle,
\end{equation}
so that, after the grazing change of variable
\(u=(\cos\varphi)^{1/2}\) in the terminal strip, all branch maps, singular
curves, and their parameter velocities satisfy polynomial strip bounds.  In
particular, if \(\Gamma\) is any moving singular curve generated by a finite
word, then the trace functional
\begin{equation}
    \rho\longmapsto (V_\Gamma\cdot n_\Gamma)\rho\,\delta_{\Gamma}
\end{equation}
maps the strong anisotropic density space to the weak one and obeys
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:trace_ledger_bound_main}
    \sum_{\Gamma\in\Ssing_n}
    \|(V_\Gamma\cdot n_\Gamma)\rho\,\delta_{\Gamma}\|_{\Bcal^w_\theta}
    \le C_n\|\rho\|_{\Bcal_\theta},
\end{equation}
where \(C_n\) depends only on the primitive geometric constants and polynomially
on the maximal strip index encountered before time \(n\).  After summing over
homogeneous terminal descendants with the growth lemma, the high-strip tail in
\eqref{eq:trace_ledger_bound_main} tends to zero uniformly in the table
parameter.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The next impact with the component \(\partial K_{i_m}(\theta)\) is determined
by \(G_{i_m}(q+t v,\theta)=0\).  Differentiation in \(t\) gives the normal
velocity \(\langle v,n(q+t v,\theta)\rangle\).  This proves
\eqref{eq:impact_time_jacobian_product} away from grazing.  In a homogeneity
strip the normal velocity is comparable to \(u^2\), and differentiating the
implicit equations loses only powers of \(u^{-1}\), equivalently polynomial
powers of the strip label.  The same computation applies to branch-change
curves, because those curves are zeros of differences of two impact functions.
The non-zero crossing derivative is obtained directly from the same finite
impact system.  If a branch-change curve or a finite-word singular curve were
tangent to the stable or unstable cone in the rescaled chart, then the two
candidate impact functions would have a common first variation along a Jacobi
field.  The optical curvature recurrence sends that Jacobi field through a
strictly positive curvature kick at each dispersing reflection.  The resulting
monotone curvature parameter cannot return to the value required for tangency
unless one of the excluded low-level degeneracies occurs: a corner, a zero
separation contact, an infinite corridor, or a grazing impact not represented in
its square-root strip coordinate.  These alternatives are ruled out by
\textup{(G1)--(G5)} and by the central/strip dichotomy.  Hence finite-word
singular strata are crossed with a non-zero normal derivative in the rescaled
coordinates before any transfer operator is introduced.

Let \(W\) be an admissible stable leaf.  The intersection number
\(W\cap\Gamma\) is uniformly bounded in each central chart and grows at most
polynomially in high strips, because the singular curves are uniformly
transverse to the stable cone after the strip rescaling.  Pairing the moving
boundary distribution with a stable test function therefore produces a weak
anisotropic functional.  The polynomial growth is compensated by the standard
terminal-child estimate: descendants entering strip \(k\) have total stable
mass bounded by a summable strip weight.  Summing over children gives
\eqref{eq:trace_ledger_bound_main}.  Every constant is obtained from the
impact equation and from the primitive strip ledger; no decay-of-correlation or
limiting theorem is used.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Derived trace constant and acyclic use]
\label{lem:derived_trace_constant_no_cycle}
There is a finite constant $C_{\rm tr}$, depending only on
$\mathfrak C_{\rm prim}$, such that every finite-word singular stratum and every
moving-boundary trace functional generated by the compact finite-response normal
motion satisfies the following uniform estimate.  For every admissible stable
family $\mathcal W$, every homogeneous cutoff level $K$, and every strong density
$\rho$,
\begin{equation}
\begin{split}
&\sum_{n\ge0}\vartheta_0^n
  \sum_{\Gamma\in\Ssing_n:\,\Gamma\cap\{|k|\le K\}\ne\varnothing}
  \sup_{W\in\mathcal W}
  \big|\langle (V_\Gamma\cdot n_\Gamma)\rho\,\delta_\Gamma,\,\psi_W\rangle\big|  \\
&\qquad\le C_{\rm tr}\,\|\rho\|_{\Bcal_\theta}
       \sup_{W\in\mathcal W}\|\psi_W\|_{C^1(W)},
\end{split}
\end{equation}
with the high-strip tail
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{n\ge0}\vartheta_0^n
  \sum_{\Gamma\in\Ssing_n:\,\Gamma\cap\{|k|>K\}\ne\varnothing}
  \|(V_\Gamma\cdot n_\Gamma)\rho\,\delta_\Gamma\|_{\Bcal^w_\theta}
  \le C_{\rm tr}\,K^{-1}\|\rho\|_{\Bcal_\theta} .
\end{equation}
Here $0<\vartheta_0<1$ is the expansion weight fixed in the strong norm, and
$\psi_W$ ranges over the stable test functions in that norm.  Thus
$C_{\rm tr}$ is a derived geometric constant.  It becomes available to
transfer-operator, response, and homogenization arguments only after
\cref{lem:impact_trace_ledger_full} and the growth lemma have been proved.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The finite-word singular curves are obtained by solving a finite triangular
system of impact equations.  The determinant in
\eqref{eq:impact_time_jacobian_product} is the product of normal velocities.  In
central charts it is bounded below by a primitive constant.  In a terminal
homogeneity strip $\mathbb H_k$ it has order $k^{-2}$, and the blown-up
coordinate $u=(\cos\varphi)^{1/2}$ converts this loss into a polynomial factor
$k^{a_j}$ for the $j$th derivative of the branch and of the boundary velocity.
The exponent $a_j$ is fixed once the differentiability order in the anisotropic
norm is fixed.

An admissible stable curve meets each rescaled singular curve a uniformly
bounded number of times in central charts and at most polynomially many times in
strip $k$.  Pairing a moving-boundary distribution with a stable test therefore
costs at most $Ck^{a}\|\psi_W\|_{C^1}\|\rho\|_{\Bcal}$ on that strip.  The growth
lemma gives the complementary estimate that descendants whose terminal child
enters strip $k$ carry strong mass $O(k^{-a-3})$ after increasing the
homogeneity exponent.  Multiplying these two bounds and summing over $k$ gives a
convergent series; starting the sum at $K$ gives the $K^{-1}$ tail in
the high-strip tail estimate displayed above.  The exponential weight $\vartheta_0^n$ is the
long-child recovery weight of the strong norm and makes the finite-word
summation uniform in $n$.

No spectral gap, response formula, cell problem, or HJB coefficient enters this
construction.  The constant is produced before the transfer operator is used in
any limiting argument, which is why placing $C_{\rm tr}$ in the derived ledger
\eqref{eq:derived_geometric_ledger} does not create a circular proof.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Aperiodicity under the map-mixing hypothesis]
\label{prop:primitive_hopf_chain_aperiodicity}
Under \textup{(A1)} in \cref{ass:analytic_admissibility_package}, the only
peripheral eigenvalue of the collision transfer operator is one, it is simple,
and the regular invariant component is aperiodic modulo null sets.  No
quantitative transition of both lengths (n) and (n+1) is claimed from
\textup{(G1)--(G6)}.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
This is exactly the peripheral-spectrum clause of \textup{(A1)}.  We record it
as a proposition only to expose where that hypothesis enters later resolvent
arguments.  The formerly proposed ``extra reflection on the same obstacle'' is
invalid for a strictly convex dispersing obstacle and has been withdrawn;
neither collision-graph connectivity nor equality in an upper Lasota--Yorke
bound supplies the missing aperiodicity argument.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Specular reflection and the billiard flow}

Let $n(q,\theta)$ be the unit normal to $\Gamma_\theta$ pointing into $Q_\theta$.  At a collision point $q\in\Gamma_\theta$, a pre-collision velocity $v^-$ with $\langle v^-,n(q,\theta)\rangle<0$ is transformed into
\begin{equation}
    v^+=v^- -2\langle v^-,n(q,\theta)\rangle n(q,\theta).
\end{equation}
This reflection preserves $|v|=1$ and reverses the normal component.  Between collisions the motion is
\begin{equation}
    \dot q=v,\qquad \dot v=0 .
\end{equation}

\begin{definition}[Grazing and regular states]
\label{def:grazing_regular_states}
The grazing set in the boundary phase space is
\begin{equation}
    \mathcal G_\theta
    =\{(q,v)\in \Gamma_\theta\times\mathbb S^1: \langle v,n(q,\theta)\rangle=0\}.
\end{equation}
Regular collisions are the points outside $\mathcal G_\theta$.  We do not remove grazing by an artificial lower bound.  Grazing is part of the singularity structure and is controlled analytically.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Collision coordinates]
\label{def:collision_coordinates}
On each component $\partial K_i(\theta)$ let $s$ be arc length.  Let $\varphi\in(-\pi/2,\pi/2)$ be the angle between the outgoing velocity and the inward normal $n(q,\theta)$.  The outgoing collision space is
\begin{equation}
    \Sigma_\theta=\bigcup_{i=1}^N \{(i,s,\varphi):s\in\R/\ell_i(\theta)\Z,\\ |\varphi|<\pi/2\}.
\end{equation}
The Birkhoff measure on $\Sigma_\theta$ is
\begin{equation}
    d\nu_\theta(i,s,\varphi)=Z_\theta^{-1}\cos\varphi\,ds\,d\varphi .
\end{equation}
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Global a.e. billiard map]
\label{def:global_billiard_map_singular}
For $z=(q,v)\in\Sigma_\theta$, let $\tau_\theta(z)$ be the first positive time for which $q+\tau v\in\Gamma_\theta$, if such a time exists and the collision is not grazing.  The global billiard map is
\begin{equation}
    \Pcal_\theta z=\bigl(q+\tau_\theta(z)v,\ R_\theta(q+\tau_\theta(z)v)v\bigr).
\end{equation}
Its forward singular set is
\begin{equation}
    \mathcal S^+_\theta
    =\{z\in\Sigma_\theta:\tau_\theta(z)\text{ is undefined or the next collision is grazing}\}.
\end{equation}
The map is understood as
\begin{equation}
    \Pcal_\theta: \Sigma_\theta\setminus\mathcal S^+_\theta
       \longrightarrow \Sigma_\theta\setminus\mathcal S^-_\theta,
    \qquad \mathcal S^-_\theta=\Pcal_\theta(\mathcal S^+_\theta).
\end{equation}
Thus ``global'' means that all scatterer components and all regular collision branches are included in one return map; it does not mean that the map is smooth across $\mathcal S^+_\theta$.
\end{definition}



\subsection{The moving phase-space and anisotropic Banach bundle}
\label{subsec:phase_banach_bundle}

Because the microscopic table depends on the transmitted cotangent signal, the
fast state spaces are not literally the same set for different values of
$(x,p)$.  The construction below fixes this point before any coefficient is
differentiated and before any viscosity test is introduced.  It is a geometric
bundle construction over the same low-level billiard data; it does not add a
mixing, spectral, or response hypothesis.

\begin{definition}[Suspension phase-space bundle]
\label{def:phase_space_bundle}
Let
\begin{equation}
    \widehat{\mathfrak Z}
    =\{(\theta,z,u):\theta\in\Thetacal,
        z\in\Sigma_\theta\setminus\bigcup_{n\in\Z}\Pcal_\theta^{-n}\mathcal S_\theta^+,
        0\le u<\tau_\theta(z)\}/\sim
\end{equation}
with the usual suspension identification
$(\theta,z,\tau_\theta(z))\sim(\theta,\Pcal_\theta z,0)$.  The fiber over
$\theta$ is denoted by $\widehat\Sigma_\theta$.  A local trivialization over a
parameter ball $O\subset\Thetacal$ is obtained by moving each scatterer boundary
in its normal coordinate, preserving the component label, preserving the
physical velocity vector, and then re-expressing the velocity by the new angle
coordinate.  In the roof direction the coordinate is the normalized height
$r=u/\tau_\theta(z)$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Geometric trivialization and strip covariance]
\label{lem:phase_bundle_trivialization}
For every $\theta_0$ there is a neighborhood $O$ and branchwise maps
\begin{equation}
    \mathfrak I_{\theta\leftarrow\theta_0}:
    \widehat\Sigma_{\theta_0}\longrightarrow\widehat\Sigma_\theta,
    \qquad \theta\in O,
\end{equation}
which are $C^{r-3}$ on homogeneous flow boxes and have the following uniform
properties.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*), wide, labelindent=0pt]
    \item Singular hypersurfaces and homogeneity-strip boundaries are carried
    to finite unions of corresponding hypersurfaces and strip boundaries, up to
    a remainder whose flux measure is zero.
    \item In a strip $\mathbb H_k$ the derivatives of
    $\mathfrak I_{\theta\leftarrow\theta_0}$ and of its inverse are bounded by
    the same polynomial powers of $k$ as the billiard map, while the measure of
    the strip tail is summable.
    \item The pullback of the suspension measure satisfies
    \begin{equation}
        (\mathfrak I_{\theta\leftarrow\theta_0})^*\mu_\theta
        =J_{\theta,\theta_0}\mu_{\theta_0},
        \qquad
        \|J_{\theta,\theta_0}-1\|_{C^{r-3}_{\rm br,w}}
        \le C\,d(\theta,\theta_0),
    \end{equation}
    where the norm is the branchwise weighted norm determined by the
    homogeneity estimate scheme.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Write a boundary component near $\theta_0$ as
$q_i(s,\theta)=q_i(s,\theta_0)+a_i(s,\theta)n_i(s,\theta_0)$ in the normal
coordinate supplied by the geometric axioms.  The positive separation and
curvature bounds keep this chart nonsingular.  A collision state
$(i,s,\varphi)$ is sent to the point with boundary coordinate $s$ on the moved
component and with the same physical outgoing unit vector, reparametrized by
its new angle with the moved inward normal.  The suspension coordinate is
transported by the normalized height $r=u/\tau_{\theta_0}(z)$ and then set to
$u_\theta=r\tau_\theta(\mathfrak I_{\theta\leftarrow\theta_0}z)$.

Away from grazing, the implicit function theorem applied to the next-collision
equation gives ordinary $C^{r-3}$ bounds.  Near grazing, the only singular
factor is a power of $(\cos\varphi)^{-1}$; the homogeneity coordinate
$\eta=(\pi/2\mp\varphi)^{1/2}$ converts this into a polynomial power of the
strip index $k$.  The derived trace ledger in
\cref{lem:impact_trace_ledger_full,lem:derived_trace_constant_no_cycle} prevents moving singular curves from
sliding tangentially along admissible stable or unstable leaves, so their
boundary contributions are weak-norm bounded.  Since the strip partition has
summable tails by \cref{lem:strip_entry_terminal_closure}, the polynomial losses
are absorbed by the same closure used for the billiard map.  The Jacobian formula
is the product of the boundary arclength change, the angle change, and the roof
change; each factor is $1+O(d(\theta,\theta_0))$ in the weighted branch norm.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Anisotropic Banach bundle]
\label{def:anisotropic_banach_bundle}
The density fibers are the spaces $\widehat\Bcal_\theta$ constructed from
stable-leaf pairings in the fiber $\widehat\Sigma_\theta$, and the observable
fibers are the restricted dual spaces $\widehat\Bcal_\theta^{\#}$.  For
$\theta,\theta_0\in O$ define transition maps
\begin{align}
    \mathfrak T^{\rm obs}_{\theta\leftarrow\theta_0}f
        &=f\circ\mathfrak I_{\theta_0\leftarrow\theta},\\
    \mathfrak T^{\rm den}_{\theta\leftarrow\theta_0}\rho
        &=(\mathfrak I_{\theta\leftarrow\theta_0})_*
          (J_{\theta,\theta_0}^{-1}\rho),
\end{align}
where the Jacobian is chosen so that pairings are preserved:
\begin{equation}
    \langle \mathfrak T^{\rm obs}_{\theta\leftarrow\theta_0}f,
            \mathfrak T^{\rm den}_{\theta\leftarrow\theta_0}\rho\rangle_\theta
    =\langle f,\rho\rangle_{\theta_0}.
\end{equation}
A section $f_\theta$ of the observable bundle is $C^k$ if
$\mathfrak T^{\rm obs}_{\theta_0\leftarrow\theta}f_\theta$ is $C^k$ as a map
from $O$ into the fixed Banach space
$\widehat\Bcal_{\theta_0}^{\#}$; density sections are defined analogously.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Norm equivalence, positivity, and generator covariance]
\label{prop:banach_bundle_covariance}
The transition maps of \cref{def:anisotropic_banach_bundle} are uniformly
bounded isomorphisms on the density and observable fibers.  They preserve the
positive cone of admissible densities, up to a uniform change of the admissible
constant $M$, and satisfy the covariance estimate
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:generator_covariance_bundle}
    \left\|
    \mathfrak T^{\rm obs}_{\theta_0\leftarrow\theta}\Acal_\theta
    \mathfrak T^{\rm obs}_{\theta\leftarrow\theta_0}f
    -\Acal_{\theta_0}f
    \right\|_{\widehat\Bcal_{\theta_0}^{\#,w}}
    \le C\,d(\theta,\theta_0)
       \|f\|_{\Dom(\Acal_{\theta_0})} .
\end{equation}
Consequently every expression below involving
$\widehat\Sigma_{\Theta(x,p)}$, $\Adm_M(\Theta(x,p))$, $\Acal_{\Theta(x,p)}$,
or a horizontal derivative is an expression in this Banach bundle, not an
unidentified comparison of different phase spaces.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The stable, unstable, and weak norms are defined by pairings against admissible
stable leaves and test functions.  The trivialization maps stable leaves into a
finite union of stable leaves with uniformly comparable curvature and length;
when a leaf is cut by a moving singular curve the unmatched piece is estimated
in the weak norm.  The Jacobian estimate in
\cref{lem:phase_bundle_trivialization} transfers the stable norm, the holonomy
comparison transfers the unstable norm, and the summable high-strip tails
transfer the weak norm.  This gives the norm equivalence and the preservation of
admissible positive densities in the distributional sense, because nonnegative
branchwise $C^1$ observables pull back to nonnegative branchwise $C^1$
observables.

For \eqref{eq:generator_covariance_bundle}, write the generator in a flow box as
$\partial_u$ plus the jump relation at the roof.  The transition map changes the
roof by a $C^{r-3}$ branchwise amount and moves the jump hypersurface by a
transversal displacement.  Interior terms are therefore first-order Taylor
remainders bounded by $d(\theta,\theta_0)\|f\|_{\Dom(\Acal)}$; boundary terms are
weak anisotropic functionals supported on moving singular hypersurfaces and are
bounded by the same strip-entry closure.  This proves the covariance estimate
and fixes the meaning of all later parameter and horizontal derivatives.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Finite horizon and zero-measure singularities}

\begin{proposition}[Deterministic bounds on free-flight time]
\label{prop:finite_horizon_bounds}
Under \cref{ass:fundamental_axioms_unified}, there exist constants
\begin{equation}
    0<\tau_-\le \tau_+<\infty
\end{equation}
such that every regular free flight between two consecutive non-grazing collisions satisfies
\begin{equation}
    \tau_-\le \tau_\theta(z)\le \tau_+ .
\end{equation}
The constants are independent of $z$ and $\theta$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The upper bound is a direct conversion of the finite corridor condition into time.  Since $|v|=1$, a free flight of duration $t$ is a straight segment of length $t$.  A segment of length $L_+$ must meet the interior of a scatterer, hence the first collision occurs before time $L_+$.  Thus $\tau_+=L_+$.

For the lower bound, after reflection the particle leaves the obstacle into the exterior region.  It cannot meet another scatterer before traveling at least the uniform separation distance $d_-$.  It cannot immediately re-enter the same strictly convex scatterer because a line leaving a strictly convex boundary in an outward direction lies outside that scatterer for a one-sided interval, and the possible one-sided intervals have a uniform lower bound by compactness and the curvature bound.  Combining the same-scatterer and different-scatterer alternatives gives $\tau_->0$.  No lower bound on the angle $\cos\varphi$ is used; trajectories arbitrarily close to grazing remain allowed and belong to the regular domain until the limiting grazing state is reached.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Finite horizon]
\label{thm:finite_horizon}
The billiard flow generated by the free motion and specular reflection is defined for all real times for every initial state whose orbit avoids the grazing singularity.  In every finite time interval the number of collisions is finite.  The return map $\Pcal_\theta$ is defined $\nu_\theta$-almost everywhere.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The lower bound in \cref{prop:finite_horizon_bounds} prevents accumulation of collision times in finite time.  The upper bound prevents infinite free flights.  The grazing condition is a scalar equation $\cos\varphi=0$ on the smooth collision coordinates, hence has $\nu_\theta$-measure zero.  The preimages of the grazing set under finitely many smooth branches are finite unions of smooth curves, hence also have zero $\nu_\theta$-measure.  The countable union over all iterates has zero measure.  Therefore the flow and the return map are well-defined on a full-measure invariant set.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Birkhoff measure invariance]
\label{prop:birkhoff_invariance}
For each $\theta$, the global a.e. map $\Pcal_\theta$ preserves $\nu_\theta$:
\begin{equation}
    \nu_\theta(\Pcal_\theta^{-1}A)=\nu_\theta(A)
\end{equation}
for every Borel set $A$ for which the pullback is defined modulo the singular set.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The Liouville measure $dq\,dv$ on $Q_\theta\times\mathbb S^1$ is preserved by free flight because the motion is translation in $q$ at fixed $v$.  Specular reflection is an orthogonal transformation in the velocity variable and has Jacobian one.  Taking the flux of $dq\,dv$ through the collision cross-section gives the density $\cos\varphi\,ds\,d\varphi$.  Equality of incoming and outgoing fluxes proves invariance of $\nu_\theta$ under the return map.  The argument is local on each smooth branch, and the excluded singular curves have zero measure.
\end{proof}

\section{Conditional micro-action and the prelimit Hamilton--Jacobi equation}
\label{sec:first_principles_prelimit}

This section records the prelimit equation conditional on the coherent
slow--fast action required in \textup{(A4)}.  When the table parameter depends
on a cotangent field that varies with the microscopic state, the family of
ordinary billiard flows does not automatically define a single phase space,
collision boundary, or specular Hamiltonian flow.  The universal-bundle
notation below is bookkeeping only; existence, collision-face compatibility,
closedness of the generator, and comparison are supplied by \textup{(A4)}, not
derived merely from the read-out map $\Theta(x,p)$.


\begin{definition}[Low-level deterministic finite-response coupling]
\label{def:finite_response_coupling}
The coupling between the slow cotangent signal and the billiard cell is specified
before any transfer operator, cell problem, or macroscopic equation is
introduced.  Its primitive data are the following finite-dimensional mechanical
objects.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(M\arabic*), wide, labelindent=0pt]
