    \item A compact internal energy surface $\mathcal Y$ carrying a $C^r$
    deterministic response flow
    \begin{equation}
        \mathcal R_s^{x,P}:\mathcal Y\longrightarrow\mathcal Y,
        \qquad 0\le s\le s_{\rm fr},
    \end{equation}
    depending $C^r$ on the macroscopic position $x$ and on the transmitted
    cotangent signal $P$.  The compactness of $\mathcal Y$ is the finite-energy
    statement: the microscopic device that reads the cotangent signal has a
    bounded energy shell.
    \item A calibrated deterministic endpoint map
    \begin{equation}
        \Pifr:\R^m\longrightarrow\R^m,
    \end{equation}
    obtained by solving the internal Euler--Lagrange response on
    $\mathcal Y$ for the fixed response time $s_{\rm fr}$.  There is a compact
    physical window $K_{\rm phys}\subset\R^m$ on which $\Pifr(p)=p$; outside the
    window the compact energy shell forces a smooth saturation.  Thus $\Pifr$
    and all of its derivatives are bounded, but no local Taylor coefficient in
    $K_{\rm phys}$ is removed.
    \item Smooth read-out maps on the fixed energy shell,
    \begin{equation}
        \vartheta:\Mcal\times\mathcal Y\to\Thetacal,
        \qquad
        \beta:\Mcal\times\mathcal Y\times\widehat\Sigma_{\vartheta(x,y)}\to\R^m,
        \qquad
        \mathcal V:\Mcal\times\mathcal Y\times\widehat\Sigma_{\vartheta(x,y)}\to\R,
    \end{equation}
    which record, respectively, the deformed billiard geometry, the slow
    mechanical velocity, and the action density.  They are branchwise $C^r$ in
    the billiard variable and satisfy the same homogeneity-strip summability as
    the billiard maps.
\end{enumerate}
With $y_0$ denoting the calibrated rest state and
$y(x,P)=\mathcal R_{s_{\rm fr}}^{x,P}y_0$, the coefficients used below are the
read-outs
\begin{align}
    \Theta(x,p)&=\vartheta(x,y(x,\Pifr(p))),\nonumber\\
    B(x,p,z)&=\beta(x,y(x,\Pifr(p)),z),\label{eq:finite_response_readouts}\\
    V(x,p,z)&=\mathcal V(x,y(x,\Pifr(p)),z).\nonumber
\end{align}
They are therefore not macroscopic effective coefficients and are not chosen
from the limiting HJB equation.  They are restrictions of a deterministic
finite-energy interaction on the microscopic phase space.  Compactness of
$\mathcal Y$, smooth dependence of $\mathcal R_s^{x,P}$, and the homogeneity
strip bounds give
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:finite_response_global_bounds}
    \sup_{x,p,\theta,z}\left|
    \partial_x^\alpha\partial_p^\beta\partial_z^\gamma
    (\Theta,B,V)(x,p,z)\right|<\infty,
    \qquad |\alpha|+|\beta|+|\gamma|\le r-4,
\end{equation}
where $z$-derivatives are taken branchwise in homogeneous flow boxes.  Locally
inside $K_{\rm phys}$ the transmitted signal is the genuine cotangent variable,
so quadratic, quartic, and higher response terms in $p$ are physical Taylor
terms of the underlying deterministic interaction.

The slow drift used in the singular perturbation is the deterministic
fluctuation around the instantaneous microcanonical transport,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:centered_drift_from_comoving_frame}
    b(x,p,z)=B(x,p,z)-\int B(x,p,\zeta)\,d\mu_{\Theta(x,p)}(\zeta).
\end{equation}
Consequently
\begin{equation}
    \int b(x,p,z)\,d\mu_{\Theta(x,p)}(z)=0
\end{equation}
identically.  This centering is the deterministic change from the laboratory
frame to the comoving slow frame after the ballistic transport velocity has
been subtracted.  It is not a statistical hypothesis and it is not inferred from
the target HJB equation.  The work term in the Hamilton--Jacobi equation is
$b(x,p,z)\cdot p$, because the physical signal read by the transducer is the
slow cotangent coordinate.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Finite-response coefficients are derived before homogenization]
\label{prop:finite_response_from_low_level}
The objects $\Theta$, $B$, $V$, and $b$ in
\cref{def:finite_response_coupling} are determined entirely by the low-level
mechanical data $(\mathcal Y,\mathcal R,\vartheta,\beta,\mathcal V,\Pifr)$ and
by the invariant billiard measure at the instantaneous table.  In particular,
none of the following is used in their construction: the transfer spectral gap,
the cell corrector, the Green--Kubo tensor, the effective Hamiltonian, or the
homogenized solution $u$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For fixed $(x,p)$ the internal response endpoint $y(x,\Pifr(p))$ is obtained by
solving an ordinary deterministic flow on the compact energy surface
$\mathcal Y$ for the finite time $s_{\rm fr}$.  The maps $\vartheta$, $\beta$,
and $\mathcal V$ are then evaluated at this endpoint and at the current billiard
state $z$.  This gives $\Theta$, $B$, and $V$ before any averaging in time is
performed.  The only invariant measure appearing in the definition of $b$ is
the Liouville--Birkhoff microcanonical measure of the already specified table
$\Theta(x,p)$; its use subtracts the deterministic comoving velocity
$\int B\,d\mu_{\Theta(x,p)}$ and does not invoke decay of correlations or a
limiting law.  The uniform bounds follow by differentiating the finite-time
flow $\mathcal R_s^{x,P}$ and the read-out maps on compact sets and then using
$\Pifr$ for global saturation.  Thus the finite-response structure is a
low-level deterministic mechanical consequence and not a postulated property of
the macroscopic limit.
\end{proof}








\begin{proposition}[Compatibility constraints for finite-response jets]
\label{prop:finite_response_constraints}
Any specified finite-response port used in the paper must satisfy the following
necessary low-level constraints:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}, wide, labelindent=0pt]
    \item the scatterer motion is a normal $C^r$ displacement small enough to
    preserve strict convexity, separation, finite horizon, and the derived grazing
    trace constants of \cref{lem:impact_trace_ledger_full,lem:derived_trace_constant_no_cycle};
    \item the read-outs respect the specular reflection law and the parity of
    incoming and outgoing covectors on the collision section;
    \item the table displacement, slow force, and running cost are read from one
    finite-time Lagrangian response action, so their mixed derivatives satisfy
    the ordinary equality of mixed partials on regular branches;
    \item the zero-signal table and zero-action gauge are fixed before any cell
    problem is introduced, namely $\Theta(x,0)=\Theta_0(x)$ and the normalized
    potential obeys \eqref{eq:C0_gauge_prelimit};
    \item every dependence on the cotangent signal passes through the saturated
    variable $\Pifr(p)$, so all derivatives required by the anisotropic Banach
    estimates are bounded globally;
    \item the amplitudes, supports, and endpoint coordinates are chosen on
    regular collision rectangles before the transfer operator, correctors,
    Green--Kubo tensor, effective Hamiltonian, or limiting solution are defined.
\end{enumerate}
These conditions are not asserted to be sufficient for realization.  In
particular, they do not establish local surjectivity of the endpoint map,
independence of invariant-response directions, or compatibility of an arbitrary
prescribed jet with reciprocity and conservation laws.  They only ensure that a
port already verified to exist is specified before $D$, $H$, any corrector, or
the solution $u$ is defined.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
This follows directly from the definition in
\cref{def:finite_response_coupling}.  A compatible compact Lagrangian port moves the
scatterers by normal boundary displacements, transports velocities by the
ordinary specular reflection map, and evaluates force and action read-outs from
the same finite-time action.  Thus its Taylor coefficients automatically obey
smallness, parity, gauge, and saturation requirements, and those requirements
are stated entirely before any averaging or homogenization step.
Nothing in this observation proves that prescribed coefficients can be realized;
existence and compatibility remain application-specific hypotheses.
\end{proof}


\begin{theorem}[Finite-response port is not an effective-coefficient oracle]
\label{thm:port_not_effective_oracle}
Fix the primitive billiard geometry and a compact finite-response port satisfying
\cref{def:finite_response_coupling,prop:finite_response_constraints}.  Let
\(\mathscr F_{\rm port}\) denote the finite list of data obtained before any
transfer operator is introduced:
\[
   (\mathcal Y,\mathcal R_s^{x,P},\Pifr,\vartheta,\beta,\mathcal V,
   \hbox{scatterer charts, specular reflection, fixed energy}).
\]
Then the effective objects
\[
   \Pi_\theta,quad
   (I-\Ltransfer_\theta)^{-1}\Piperp_\theta,quad
   (-\Acal_\theta|_{\ker\Pi_\theta})^{-1},quad
   \chi_p,quad \chi^{(2)}_{p,X},quad D(x,p),quad H(x,p)
\]
are not coordinates of \(\mathscr F_{\rm port}\).  They are obtained only by
applying the already constructed singular-billiard transfer calculus to the
coefficient functions read from \(\mathscr F_{\rm port}\).  More concretely,
the deterministic map from primitive data to the effective coefficients factors
as
\[
\mathscr F_{\rm port}
\longmapsto
(\Theta,b,V)
\longmapsto
(\Pcal_\theta,\Ltransfer_\theta,\Acal_\theta,\Pi_\theta,R_\theta)
\longmapsto
(\chi_p,\chi^{(2)}_{p,X})
\longmapsto
(D,H),
\]
and no arrow in this factorization admits \((D,H)\) or the homogenized solution
as an input.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The first arrow is finite-dimensional.  It consists of solving the internal
Euler--Lagrange response for the finite time \(s_{\rm fr}\), reading the normal
scatterer displacement, force, and action density, and subtracting the
Liouville--Birkhoff comoving velocity in \eqref{eq:centered_drift_from_comoving_frame}.
This arrow ends before a Banach space or cell equation has been defined.

The second arrow combines construction and hypotheses.  From the table
\(\Theta(x,p)\) one solves impact equations and constructs homogeneous branches;
\textup{(A1)--(A2)} then supply the spectral gap and reduced resolvent for the
resulting transfer operator.
These operations are infinite-time analytic operations on the collision map; they
cannot be read off from a finite endpoint coordinate of the port.  They depend on
the orbit structure of the resulting billiard table, not on an effective
Hamiltonian chosen in advance.

The third and fourth arrows are likewise analytic.  The first corrector is
\(-\Acal_\theta^{-1}\Piperp_\theta(b\cdot p)\), the second corrector is the
same reduced inverse applied to the centered full-gradient order-one observable,
and \(D,H\) are invariant pairings of those objects.  Thus changing the port
changes the microscopic coefficients, and the transfer-resolvent calculation then
determines the corresponding \(D,H\).  There is no operation in the reverse
direction from \(D,H\) or from the macroscopic solution to the primitive port.
This proves that finite-response flexibility is a microscopic realization
statement rather than an oracle for the effective equation.
\end{proof}


\begin{proposition}[Admissibility template for a mechanical port]
\label{thm:mechanical_port_endogenous}
Suppose a specified finite-response port is generated by a deterministic
finite-time Lagrangian response system
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:internal_lagrangian_port}
    L_{\rm fr}(x,P,y,\dot y)
    =\frac12 g_{ab}(y)\dot y^a\dot y^b-U_0(y)-U_1(x,\Pifr(P),y)
\end{equation}
on a compact energy shell, with endpoint
$y(x,P)=\mathcal R_{s_{\rm fr}}^{x,P}y_0$ and the three read-outs
$\vartheta,\beta,\mathcal V$ are obtained by differentiating the same
microscopic action and by reading the induced scatterer displacement.  In
particular:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)}, wide, labelindent=0pt]
    \item the table parameter $\Theta(x,p)$ is the normal boundary displacement
    produced by the internal endpoint $y(x,\Pifr(p))$;
    \item the velocity field $B(x,p,z)$ is the slow force conjugate to $x$ in the
    same Lagrangian action, transported to the collision section by the
    Liouville flux map;
    \item the running cost $V(x,p,z)$ is the branchwise action density of the
    same finite-time response and the billiard flight;
    \item all global bounds in \eqref{eq:finite_response_global_bounds} follow
    from compactness of the internal energy shell and from the smooth saturation
    $\Pifr$;
    \item the particular response jet used in an application must be verified
    to satisfy the endpoint equations, action reciprocity, conservation laws,
    and billiard compatibility of \textup{(A4)}.
\end{enumerate}
No arbitrary finite-jet realization theorem is asserted.  Non-convex and
non-subadditive examples later in the paper are conditional examples until the
specified port and its invariant-response coefficients are independently
verified.
This proposition is a compatibility checklist, not an existence or realization
theorem.  In particular, adjoining formal endpoint coordinates does not prove
that the requested read-outs arise from one action.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For a port satisfying the premise, the three read-outs are derivatives or
endpoint evaluations of the same action, so the listed reciprocity and gauge
conditions are necessary.  Compactness and saturation give bounded derivatives
only after the endpoint flow and collision-face read-outs have been shown smooth
on the chosen branches.  Exact endpoint matching, global collision-face
compatibility, conservation, and the required uniform bounds remain obligations
of \textup{(A4)}; no extension theorem or formal tracking coordinate discharges
them.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Conditional finite-time endpoint-jet construction]
\label{lem:exact_endpoint_jet_compact_port}
Fix a compact cotangent window $K_{\rm phys}$ and a finite order $J\le r-6$.
Let $\mathcal J(P)$ be a finite list of saturated polynomial jets.  If the
finite-dimensional endpoint map is locally onto this list while preserving the
single-action constraints in \textup{(A4)}, then a compactly supported local
construction realizes the prescribed $J$-jet on $K_{\rm phys}$.  Local
surjectivity and compatibility are hypotheses, not consequences of disjoint
bumps.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Choose saturated monomials $M_\alpha(P)=\Pifr(P)^\alpha$, $|\alpha|\le J$,
which agree with ordinary monomials on $K_{\rm phys}$.  Work first in a compact
coordinate ball of a torus factor of the internal phase space and take initial
condition $(y,\dot y)=(0,0)$.  For a finite vector of amplitudes $A$ define a
potential
\begin{equation}
    U_A(x,P,y)=-\sum_{\alpha,\ell} A_{\alpha\ell}(x)M_\alpha(P)y_\ell
       +\sum_{q=2}^{J+1} U_{A,q}(x,P,y)+U_{\rm conf}(y),
\end{equation}
where the confining term is flat on the chosen coordinate ball and positive
outside it.  The endpoint $E_A(x,P)=y(s_{\rm fr};x,P,A)$ has a Taylor expansion
in $A$ obtained by Picard iteration of the deterministic Euler--Lagrange ODE.
The linear part is
\begin{equation}
    E_A(x,P)=\frac{s_{\rm fr}^2}{2}
      \sum_{\alpha,\ell}A_{\alpha\ell}(x)M_\alpha(P)e_\ell+O(|A|^2).
\end{equation}
The higher correction potentials $U_{A,q}$ are chosen recursively.  At degree
$q$ the lower-degree amplitudes determine a known polynomial error in the
endpoint jet; adding a new internal coordinate with a linear force proportional
to the negative of that error changes no lower-degree jet and cancels the
degree-$q$ error.  Because only finitely many monomials and degrees are involved,
the recursion terminates and the finite Jacobian from the newly introduced
amplitudes to the degree-$q$ endpoint coefficients is diagonal with entries
$s_{\rm fr}^2/2\ne0$.

After the required jet has been matched inside the coordinate ball, extend the
local potential to the torus by a cutoff that is identically one on the ball and
add the confining term so that the selected energy level is compact and regular.
The endpoint orbit for all $P\in K_{\rm phys}$ stays inside the ball when the
response amplitudes are chosen within the smallness range required to preserve
the billiard geometry.  Outside the physical window the saturation $\Pifr$ keeps
the vector field and all derivatives bounded.  The endpoint jet is thus exact on
$K_{\rm phys}$ and globally smooth after saturation.

The argument is finite-dimensional deterministic controllability of an ODE
endpoint map.  It neither knows nor uses the transfer spectrum, the Green--Kubo
tensor, the effective Hamiltonian, or a limiting solution.  Those objects are
computed only after the microscopic endpoint data have been fixed.
\end{proof}



\begin{proposition}[Single-action reciprocity of finite-response read-outs]
\label{prop:single_action_reciprocity_port}
The finite-response port is generated by one deterministic microscopic action,
not by three independent coefficient choices.  On each homogeneous branch there
is an action functional
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:finite_response_generating_action}
    \mathscr S_{\rm fr}(x,P,y_0;z)
       =\int_0^{s_{\rm fr}}L_{\rm fr}(x,P,y_s,\dot y_s)\,ds
        +\mathscr S_{\rm flight}(\Theta(x,P),z)
\end{equation}
such that, after the zero-energy gauge and the comoving Liouville subtraction,
\begin{align}
\label{eq:port_reciprocity_identities}
    \Theta(x,p)&=\vartheta(x,y(x,\Pifr(p))),\\
    B^a(x,p,z)&=-\partial_{x_a}\mathscr S_{\rm fr}(x,\Pifr(p),y_0;z)
        +\Acal_{\Theta(x,p)}R^a_{\rm fr}(x,p,z),\\
    V(x,p,z)&=\partial_{s_{\rm fr}}\mathscr S_{\rm fr}(x,\Pifr(p),y_0;z)
        +\Acal_{\Theta(x,p)}R^0_{\rm fr}(x,p,z),\\
    D_p\Theta(x,p)&=D_y\vartheta(x,y(x,\Pifr(p)))
        D_Py(x,\Pifr(p))D\Pifr(p).
\end{align}
The branch functions $R^a_{\rm fr}$ and $R^0_{\rm fr}$ are finite-response
coboundary gauges with zero invariant averages.  Consequently the allowed
Taylor jets of $\Theta$, $B$, and $V$ obey the reciprocity identities obtained
by differentiating \eqref{eq:port_reciprocity_identities}; the constructive
freedom of \cref{thm:mechanical_port_endogenous} is a finite-dimensional
mechanical controllability statement and cannot insert an effective Hamiltonian
coefficient after homogenization.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For fixed $(x,P)$ the internal endpoint $y(x,P)$ is the endpoint of the
Euler--Lagrange flow generated by $L_{\rm fr}$.  Hamilton's principle gives the
ordinary endpoint-variation identity for \eqref{eq:finite_response_generating_action}:
variations in $x$ produce the slow force, variations in response time produce
the running action density, and variations in $P$ are transmitted only through
the endpoint map and the saturated signal $\Pifr(P)$.  The first and fourth
identities in \eqref{eq:port_reciprocity_identities} are therefore immediate
from the definition of the boundary displacement read-out.

The billiard flight contribution is Liouville exact on every homogeneous branch:
changing the branch action by a flight endpoint potential adds a generator
coboundary.  These endpoint potentials are the gauges $R^a_{\rm fr}$ and
$R^0_{\rm fr}$.  Their invariant averages vanish because
$\int\Acal_{\Theta(x,p)}R\,d\mu_{\Theta(x,p)}=0$ for generator-domain observables,
with the identity justified branchwise and then closed in the weak anisotropic
norm by \cref{prop:universal_bundle_branch_generator_domain}.  Hence the displayed
formulas give the same centered drift and normalized potential used in the cell
problem.

Differentiating the endpoint-variation formulas yields the mixed-derivative
reciprocity relations that any admissible finite jet must satisfy.  Whether a
chosen endpoint family has enough independent directions while satisfying all
these relations is a separate rank condition.  The effective tensor and
Hamiltonian are computed only after a compatible port has been fixed.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Conditional local-bump response directions]
\label{prop:triangular_bump_realization}
Fix a finite jet order $J\le r-6$, a compact cotangent window
$K_{\rm phys}$, and a finite list of response coefficients satisfying the
specular parity and the normalization at $p=0$.  Assume additionally that the
full endpoint/response Jacobian, including global invariant-density response and
single-action reciprocity, has the required rank.  Then a compact
finite-response port locally realizes these coefficients to order $J$.
Disjoint bump supports alone do not imply this rank condition.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Let $F$ be the finite-dimensional map from admissible port parameters to the
listed endpoint and invariant-response coefficients, restricted to the
single-action compatibility manifold.  The premise says that $DF$ has the
required rank at the reference port and that \textup{(A3)--(A4)} provide the
needed differentiability.  The finite-dimensional submersion theorem therefore
gives local realization of coefficients in the image of $F$.  This argument does
not prove the rank premise.  In particular, disjoint supports do not eliminate
normalization, recurrence, moving-singularity, reciprocity, or conservation
couplings in $DF$.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Acyclic jet graph for the microscopic action]
\label{def:acyclic_jet_graph}
Fix $\varepsilon>0$ and a homogeneous flow box in the bundle of
\cref{def:phase_space_bundle}.  A deterministic slow--fast jet graph is a
branchwise $C^1$ submanifold
\begin{equation}
    \Lambda_s^\varepsilon
    =\{(x,z,P_s(x,z),A_s(x,z)): (x,z)\in\Mcal\times\widehat\Sigma_{\Theta(x,P_s(x,z))}\}
\end{equation}
where $P_s$ is a cotangent signal and $A_s$ is the accumulated action.  The
coefficient maps $\Theta$, $b$, and $V$ are evaluated at the independent jet
coordinate $P_s$ of this graph.  The graph is propagated by the deterministic
branch equations
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:closed_jet_graph_flow}
    \dot z_s=\varepsilon^{-2}\Ffast_{\Theta(x_s,P_s)}(z_s),
    \qquad
    \dot x_s=\varepsilon^{-1}b(x_s,P_s,z_s),
    \qquad
    \dot A_s= -V^\circ(x_s,P_s,z_s),
\end{equation}
with specular reflection in the $z$ variable and with $P_s$ transported by
branchwise differentiation of the same action graph:
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:jet_graph_closure}
    P_s(x_s,z_s)=D_x A_s(x_s,z_s).
\end{equation}
Equations \eqref{eq:closed_jet_graph_flow}--\eqref{eq:jet_graph_closure} are a
closed deterministic graph transform on finite time slabs.  The cotangent
signal is therefore not chosen from the macroscopic limit and is not an
independent external control.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Deterministic graph-transform closure]
\label{lem:deterministic_graph_transform_closure}
For every branchwise $C^2$ terminal graph with finite-response cotangent range,
the map that sends a trial jet graph on a sufficiently short backward time slab
to the graph obtained by integrating \eqref{eq:closed_jet_graph_flow} and then
differentiating the resulting action is a contraction in the $C^1$ graph norm
after the usual exponential weight in time.  The contraction constants depend
only on the finite-response bounds, the billiard geometric constants, and
$\varepsilon$ on that fixed slab.  Concatenating finitely many slabs constructs a
unique branchwise jet graph on $[0,T]$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
All coefficients in \eqref{eq:closed_jet_graph_flow} are branchwise $C^{r-4}$
and have bounded derivatives in the transmitted signal because of
\eqref{eq:finite_response_global_bounds}.  In a fixed homogeneous flow box the
difference of two trial graphs obeys a Gronwall inequality whose source is the
Lipschitz difference of their cotangent signals.  Differentiating the action
identity with respect to $x$ gives the same inequality for the cotangent part,
because the horizontal derivatives of the billiard bundle are bounded by
\cref{prop:banach_bundle_covariance}.  Choosing the slab length so that the
Gronwall factor times the coefficient Lipschitz constant is less than one gives
a contraction.  Specular reflections paste the branchwise estimates because the
reflection map is $C^{r-3}$ on each homogeneous component and preserves the
Liouville action.  If a branch reaches a singular hypersurface the trace is
ignored in the graph norm, since the hypersurface has zero flux measure and its
movement is controlled in the weak anisotropic norm.  Compactness of the time
interval gives the finite concatenation.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Microscopic Hamilton principal function]
\label{def:microscopic_principal_function}
For a terminal payoff $\phi\in C^1(\Mcal)$, the branchwise Hamilton principal
function $U^\varepsilon$ is the action component of the unique deterministic jet
graph of \cref{lem:deterministic_graph_transform_closure} with terminal graph
$A_T(x,z)=\phi(x)$ and $P_T(x,z)=D\phi(x)$.  Equivalently, on every regular
branch and for every sufficiently small $h>0$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:deterministic_action_identity}
    U^\varepsilon(t,x,z)=U^\varepsilon(t+h,x_h,z_h)
    +\int_t^{t+h} V^\circ(x_s,P_s,z_s)\,ds+o(h),
\end{equation}
where $(x_s,z_s,P_s)$ is the closed jet graph trajectory starting from
$(t,x,z,D_xU^\varepsilon(t,x,z))$.  The zero-energy gauge is fixed directly from
the microscopic running cost at zero transmitted signal:
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:C0_gauge_prelimit}
    C_0(x)=\int V(x,0,z)\,d\mu_{\Theta(x,0)}(z),
    \qquad
    V^\circ(x,p,z)=V(x,p,z)-C_0(x).
\end{equation}
This gauge is determined before the cell problem and before the macroscopic HJB
equation are introduced.
\end{definition}


\begin{definition}[Finite-response branch generator]
\label{def:finite_response_branch_generator}
Let $P$ be a branchwise finite-response cotangent field on a homogeneous flow
box of the universal billiard bundle.  The deterministic fast vector field with
finite-response port $P$ is denoted
\begin{equation}
    \Ffast^{\rm fr}_{x,P}(z)
    :=\Ffast_{\Theta(x,P(z))}(z)
\end{equation}
after transporting the state $z$ to the corresponding moved billiard fiber by
\cref{lem:phase_bundle_trivialization}.  On regular branches the associated
observable generator is
\begin{equation}
    \Acal^{\rm fr}_{x,P}F(z)
    =\Ffast^{\rm fr}_{x,P}(z)\cdot D_z^{\rm br}F(z),
\end{equation}
with the specular reflection jump condition imposed at the moved boundary.  If
$P$ is constant in $z$, this is exactly the fixed-table generator
$\Acal_{\Theta(x,P)}$.  If $P$ is not constant, the expression is not treated as
a fixed billiard flow with a fictitious $z$-dependent table; it is the
branchwise deterministic vector field on the universal bundle.  Its weak
anisotropic closure is obtained by the same moving-boundary estimates as in
\cref{prop:banach_bundle_covariance}.
\end{definition}



\begin{proposition}[Universal-bundle domain of the finite-response branch generator]
\label{prop:universal_bundle_branch_generator_domain}
Let $P$ be a branchwise $C^1$ finite-response cotangent field with bounded
finite-response norm.  The operator $\Acal^{\rm fr}_{x,P}$ of
\cref{def:finite_response_branch_generator} has a closed weak anisotropic graph
on the universal billiard bundle.  Its domain consists of observable sections
$F$ such that
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)}, wide, labelindent=0pt]
    \item $F$ is branchwise $C^1$ in homogeneous flow boxes after trivialization;
    \item the specular jump condition is satisfied on every moving collision
    face determined by $\Theta(x,P(z))$;
    \item for every admissible density $\rho$ the Green identity
    \begin{equation}
\label{eq:finite_response_green_identity}
        \langle \Acal^{\rm fr}_{x,P}F,\rho\rangle
        =-\langle F,\operatorname{div}_{\mu_0}(X_{x,P}\rho)\rangle
          +\sum_{\Gamma\subset\Ssing(P)}
             \langle [F]_{\Gamma},(X_{x,P}\cdot n_\Gamma)\rho\rangle_\Gamma
    \end{equation}
    is finite in the weak anisotropic norm.
\end{enumerate}
Here $X_{x,P}$ is the pulled-back branch vector field on the fixed reference
bundle, and the sum ranges over the moved singular hypersurfaces.  The boundary
term in \eqref{eq:finite_response_green_identity} vanishes on true specular
collision faces and is weak-norm bounded on grazing and branch-change faces.
Moreover, if $P_\eta=p+\eta q$ then the first variation
\eqref{eq:variable_table_generator_expansion} is the derivative of this closed
graph in the strong-to-weak topology.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Choose a reference table $\theta_0$ and pull every nearby table to the reference
fiber by the geometric trivialization of \cref{lem:phase_bundle_trivialization}.
For a non-constant field $P(z)$ this does not define a single fixed billiard
table; it defines the ordinary first-order vector field
\begin{equation}
    X_{x,P}(z)
    =D\mathfrak I_{\theta(z)\leftarrow\theta_0}^{-1}
      \Ffast_{\theta(z)}(\mathfrak I_{\theta(z)\leftarrow\theta_0}z),
    \qquad \theta(z)=\Theta(x,P(z)),
\end{equation}
on each homogeneous branch of the universal bundle.  Since $P$ has bounded
finite-response norm and $\Theta$ is globally bounded with bounded derivatives,
$X_{x,P}$ has the same strip losses as the ordinary billiard vector field.

For branchwise smooth $F$ and $\rho$, integration by parts with respect to the
pulled-back Liouville volume gives \eqref{eq:finite_response_green_identity}.
On physical collision faces the two traces of $F$ are identified by specular
reflection and the Liouville flux is preserved; therefore the corresponding
boundary contributions cancel exactly.  Grazing and branch-change hypersurfaces
are not physical boundaries of the Hamiltonian flow.  Their contributions are
trace functionals on stable leaves.  The transversality of these hypersurfaces
and the high-strip estimate of \cref{lem:strip_entry_terminal_closure} imply
\begin{equation}
    \sum_{\Gamma\subset\Ssing(P)}
       |\langle [F]_{\Gamma},(X_{x,P}\cdot n_\Gamma)\rho\rangle_\Gamma|
    \le C\|F\|_{\widehat\Bcal^{\#,s}}
        \|\rho\|_{\widehat\Bcal},
\end{equation}
which gives weak graph boundedness.  If $F_n\to F$ in the observable norm and
$\Acal^{\rm fr}_{x,P}F_n\to G$ in the weak norm, passing to the limit in
\eqref{eq:finite_response_green_identity} shows that $F$ satisfies the same
Green identity with generator $G$; hence the graph is closed.

Finally let $P_\eta=p+\eta q$.  The vector field, the roof identification, and
the singular hypersurfaces vary $C^1$ in $\eta$ in the strong-to-weak topology by
exactly the moving-boundary computation of
\cref{thm:moving_singularity_response_formula}.  Differentiating the Green
identity at $\eta=0$ gives
\eqref{eq:variable_table_generator_expansion}.  This proves that the full-gradient
prelimit operator is a single deterministic branch operator on the universal
bundle, not an ill-defined family of fixed billiards with a $z$-dependent
parameter.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Exact deterministic prelimit Hamilton--Jacobi equation]
\label{thm:exact_prelude_hj}
On every regular homogeneous flow branch, the principal function constructed by
\cref{lem:deterministic_graph_transform_closure} satisfies
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:exact_branchwise_prelude_hj}
    -\partial_tU^\varepsilon
    -\varepsilon^{-2}\Ffast^{\rm fr}_{x,p}(z)\cdot D_zU^\varepsilon
    -\varepsilon^{-1}b(x,p,z)\cdot p
    -V^\circ(x,p,z)=0,
    \qquad p=D_xU^\varepsilon(t,x,z).
\end{equation}
Equivalently, after completing branchwise observables in the anisotropic bundle
and writing $\Acal^{\rm fr}_{x,p}$ for the finite-response branch generator on
the universal billiard bundle, \eqref{eq:exact_branchwise_prelude_hj} becomes
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:prelimit_from_action_principle}
    -\partial_t u^\varepsilon
    -\varepsilon^{-2}\Acal^{\rm fr}_{x,p}u^\varepsilon
    -\varepsilon^{-1}b(x,p,z)\cdot p
    -V^\circ(x,p,z)=0,
    \qquad p=D_xu^\varepsilon(t,x,z).
\end{equation}
The construction and the proof are deterministic graph-transform statements on
the cotangent jet bundle.  They do not use the macroscopic limit, the cell
problem or any post-derivation representation.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The graph-transform lemma constructs $U^\varepsilon$ and its cotangent signal
first.  Thus, when a derivative is written below, the identity
$p=D_xU^\varepsilon$ is the closure relation of the already constructed graph,
not an external prescription using an unknown limiting solution.

Fix a regular branch and start the graph trajectory from $(t,x,z,p)$ with
$p=D_xU^\varepsilon(t,x,z)$.  Taylor expansion of the terminal side of
\eqref{eq:deterministic_action_identity} gives
\begin{align}
    U^\varepsilon(t+h,x_h,z_h)-U^\varepsilon(t,x,z)
    &=h\partial_tU^\varepsilon
      +h\varepsilon^{-1}b(x,p,z)\cdot D_xU^\varepsilon  \\
    &\quad +h\varepsilon^{-2}\Ffast^{\rm fr}_{x,p}(z)\cdot D_zU^\varepsilon
      +o(h),
\end{align}
where the coefficient arguments are the jet coordinates at the starting point.
Substituting into the action identity, dividing by $h$, and letting $h\downarrow0$
gives \eqref{eq:exact_branchwise_prelude_hj}.  Specular reflection contributes
no boundary action term: the tangential coordinate is continuous, the normal
velocity changes sign, and the elastic reflection preserves the fixed energy
shell.  The singular hypersurfaces are excluded from pointwise evaluation and
have zero flux measure; their traces are accounted for only through the
anisotropic weak boundary terms of the bundle construction.

The passage to \eqref{eq:prelimit_from_action_principle} is the definition of
the observable generator on $\widehat\Bcal_\theta^{\#}$, transported through the
Banach-bundle charts of \cref{prop:banach_bundle_covariance}.  Since the graph
construction precedes the cell problem, there is no circular dependence between
the prelimit equation and the homogenized HJB equation.
\end{proof}


\begin{theorem}[Closed deterministic construction of the prelimit jet graph]
\label{thm:closed_prelimit_jet_graph_construction}
Fix \(\varepsilon>0\), a terminal payoff \(\phi\in C^4\), and a regular
homogeneous branch rectangle in the universal billiard bundle.  Before any
macroscopic limit is considered, there is a unique branchwise exact Lagrangian
jet graph
\[
   \Lambda_s^\varepsilon
   =\{(x_s,z_s,P_s,A_s): P_s=D_{x_s}A_s\}
\]
on its deterministic lifetime.  It is generated by the finite-dimensional
Hamilton system consisting of the slow transport, the fast billiard branch
vector field, and the finite-response action read-out:
\begin{align}
   \dot z_s&=\varepsilon^{-2}\Ffast^{\rm fr}_{x_s,P_s}(z_s),\nonumber\\
   \dot x_s&=\varepsilon^{-1} b(x_s,P_s,z_s),\nonumber\\
   \dot A_s&=\varepsilon^{-1}b(x_s,P_s,z_s)\cdot P_s+V^\circ(x_s,P_s,z_s),
      \label{eq:closed_prelimit_characteristic_system}\\
   \dot P_s&=-D_x\bigl(\varepsilon^{-1}b(x_s,P_s,z_s)\cdot P_s
      +V^\circ(x_s,P_s,z_s)\bigr)
      -D_x\Ffast^{\rm fr}_{x_s,P_s}(z_s)^*\lambda_s .\nonumber
\end{align}
Here \(\lambda_s\) is the cotangent multiplier for the fast branch coordinate,
transported through the specular reflection law.  Across regular impacts the
canonical one-form is preserved, and branch changes occur only through the
zero-measure singular boundary whose derivative is represented later by weak
trace functionals.  Consequently the relation \(P_s=D_xA_s\) is propagated by
the branch dynamics itself; it is not an imposed fixed-point condition involving
the limiting HJB solution.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
On a fixed homogeneous branch all functions in
\eqref{eq:closed_prelimit_characteristic_system} are branchwise \(C^{r-4}\) and
globally bounded on compact jet windows by \cref{eq:finite_response_global_bounds}
and the branch atlas.  The finite-dimensional ODE therefore has a unique
solution until it reaches the boundary of the chosen homogeneous rectangle.  The
terminal graph is exact because it is generated by \(A_T(x,z)=\phi(x)\), so the
canonical one-form on the graph is \(dA_T=P_T\,dx\).

The Hamiltonian vector field in
\eqref{eq:closed_prelimit_characteristic_system} is obtained from the same
finite-response action as \cref{thm:exact_prelude_hj}.  Therefore the Lie
derivative of the one-form \(dA_s-P_s\,dx_s-\lambda_s\,dz_s\) along the branch
flow is zero.  Equivalently, differentiating the action identity with respect to
the initial slow coordinate gives the adjoint equation for \(P_s\).  Thus exactness
of the graph is transported from the terminal graph to every earlier regular
time.

At a specular reflection, \cref{lem:branchwise_endpoint_variation_specular}
shows that the jump in velocity is normal to the boundary, while allowed
endpoint variations are tangent.  Hence the canonical endpoint term is unchanged
by the impact.  The only points where the branch identity is not evaluated are
grazing and multiple-collision singularities; they have zero flux measure and are
accounted for, after completion, by the moving-boundary trace estimates.  The
construction of \(\Lambda_s^\varepsilon\) is therefore complete before the cell
problem, the effective coefficients, or the homogenized solution are introduced.
Substituting \(P_s=D_xA_s\) in the branch action identity gives exactly
\eqref{eq:exact_branchwise_prelude_hj}.
\end{proof}


\begin{proposition}[Single universal-bundle meaning of a z-dependent contact covector]
\label{prop:z_dependent_contact_covector_universal_bundle}
Let \(\Phi(t,x,z)=\varphi(t,x)+\varepsilon\Psi_1(t,x,z)+\varepsilon^2\Psi_2(t,x,z)\)
be a branchwise perturbed test function used in the viscosity-duality proof,
and write
\begin{equation}
    P_\Phi(t,x,z)=D_x\Phi(t,x,z).
\end{equation}
The prelimit operator evaluated at \(P_\Phi\) is a single deterministic vector
field on the pulled-back universal billiard bundle.  More precisely, after
normal-coordinate trivialization of the moving tables, the map
\begin{equation}
    (x,z,P)\longmapsto
    \Acal^{\rm fr}_{x,P}+\frac1\varepsilon b(x,P,z)\cdot P+V^\circ(x,P,z)
\end{equation}
is branchwise \(C^{r-3}\) in \((x,P,z)\) on homogeneous components, with
strong-to-weak trace bounds across moving singular curves.  Therefore a
\(z\)-dependent contact covector does not mean that several billiard tables are
run simultaneously; it means that the universal branch vector field is
linearized along the graph \(z\mapsto P_\Phi(t,x,z)\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The mechanical port constructs scatterer deformations, finite-response drift,
and potential as functions on the universal bundle
\((x,P,\zeta)\), where \(\zeta\) is a reference collision coordinate.  The
normal-coordinate trivialization sends the table \(\Theta(x,P)\) back to the
reference section and expresses the reflection law, roof, and Jacobian as
branchwise functions of \((x,P,\zeta)\).  The impact-function ledger
\cref{lem:impact_trace_ledger_full} gives the stated regularity and the weak
trace bounds.  Substitution of the graph \(P=P_\Phi(t,x,z)\) is then an
ordinary composition in this deterministic bundle.  The singular set is not a
place where a pointwise equation is evaluated; it contributes only the already
controlled trace functionals in the weak anisotropic topology.  Consequently
the full-gradient closure used later is not a change of dynamics after
homogenization but the original branch vector field evaluated on a contact jet
of the exact prelimit Hamilton--Jacobi equation.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Branchwise endpoint variation through specular reflection]
\label{lem:branchwise_endpoint_variation_specular}
On a regular homogeneous branch, variation of the Hamilton principal action
through a collision contributes only the standard endpoint cotangent terms;
there is no unaccounted reflection boundary work.  If \(q\) is the collision
point and \(v^-\), \(v^+\) are the incoming and outgoing velocities, then for a
boundary variation \(\delta q\in T_q\partial Q_\theta\),
\begin{equation}
    \langle v^- - v^+,\delta q\rangle=0,
\end{equation}
and the normal component cancels because \(v^+=v^- -2\langle v^-,n\rangle n\).
The only boundary terms left by differentiating a moving family of branches are
the moving-singularity trace terms of \cref{lem:impact_trace_ledger_full}.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The tangential component of velocity is preserved by elastic reflection, while
the normal component changes sign.  Since a regular endpoint variation of the
collision point is tangent to the boundary, its scalar product with the jump
\(v^- -v^+\), which is purely normal, vanishes.  If the branch boundary itself
moves, the variation is not an endpoint term of a regular trajectory but the
derivative of a characteristic function selecting a homogeneous branch.  This
is exactly the trace distribution estimated in \cref{lem:impact_trace_ledger_full}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Acyclic role of the coupling]
The maps $\Theta$, $B$, and $V$ are low-level constitutive data. The spectral
gap and response bounds are independent clauses of \textup{(A1)--(A3)}.
Conditional on them, the cell resolvent, Green--Kubo tensor, and HJB
coefficients are defined by the stated transfer and two-scale formulae.
\end{remark}

\section{The global billiard map as a hyperbolic map with singularities}
\label{sec:singular_hyperbolicity}

\subsection{Homogeneity strips and smooth branches}

The derivative of $\Pcal_\theta$ becomes singular near $|\varphi|=\pi/2$.  Instead of excluding such states, we use the standard homogeneity decomposition.  For $k\ge k_0$ define
\begin{equation}
    \mathbb H_{k}^{\pm}
    =\left\{(s,\varphi):\frac{1}{(k+1)^2}<\frac{\pi}{2}\mp\varphi<\frac{1}{k^2}\right\},
\end{equation}
and let $\mathbb H_0$ be the complement of the union of these boundary strips.  A homogeneous component is a connected component of the complement of the singular curves and the homogeneity strip boundaries.

\begin{proposition}[Piecewise smoothness of the global map]
\label{prop:piecewise_smoothness}
For every $\theta$, the map $\Pcal_\theta$ is $C^r$ on each homogeneous component.  On such a component it is a diffeomorphism onto its image.  The singular set is a countable union of $C^r$ curves whose slopes are uniformly transverse to the stable and unstable cones defined below.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Fix a regular branch.  The next impact point is determined by the equation
\begin{equation}
    F(s,\varphi,t,\theta)=G(q(s,\theta)+t v(s,\varphi,\theta),\theta)=0.
\end{equation}
At a non-grazing collision,
\begin{equation}
    \partial_tF=\langle v,n(q+t v,\theta)\rangle\ne0.
\end{equation}
The implicit function theorem gives a $C^r$ flight time $t=\tau_\theta(s,\varphi)$ on that branch.  The impact point and reflected velocity are compositions of $C^r$ functions, hence $\Pcal_\theta$ is $C^r$.  The inverse branch is obtained by time reversal, so it is also $C^r$.  Singularities occur only when $\partial_tF=0$ or when the identity of the next scatterer changes.  The impact-function trace ledger \cref{lem:impact_trace_ledger_full} gives $C^r$ singular curves after the square-root grazing blow-up.  Homogeneity strip boundaries are smooth by construction.  Uniform transversality follows from curvature and the angle-coordinate definition, and is therefore a derived geometric estimate rather than an input.
\end{proof}


\subsection{Cone fields}

The billiard metric used for expansion is a length element on unstable and
stable curves, not a norm on the full tangent plane.  For a non-vertical tangent
vector $u=(ds,d\varphi)$ tangent to a homogeneous unstable curve we write
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:p_length_element}
    |u|_p=\cos\varphi\,|ds|,
\end{equation}
and for estimates involving arbitrary tangent vectors we use the genuine
auxiliary norm
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:true_tangent_norm}
    |u|_*=\cos\varphi\,|ds|+|d\varphi| .
\end{equation}
For $ds\ne0$ set
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:wavefront_curvature_variable}
    \mathcal B(u)=\frac{d\varphi}{ds}+\kappa(s,\theta).
\end{equation}
This is the curvature of the infinitesimal post-collision wave front represented
by $u$.  The upper size of $\mathcal B$ is not uniformly bounded near grazing;
indeed a dispersing reflection contains the term $2\kappa/\cos\varphi$.  The
correct invariant object is therefore a lower cone together with a strip estimate scheme.

\begin{definition}[Strip-adapted stable and unstable cones]
\label{def:cones_singular}
Choose $a_->0$ depending only on the finite horizon and curvature constants.  If
$z\in\mathbb H_k^\pm$, write $k(z)=k$, and put $k(z)=1$ on the central region
$\mathbb H_0$.  The unstable cone is
\begin{equation}
    C^u_\theta(z)=\{u:ds\ne0,\ \mathcal B(u)\ge a_-\}.
\end{equation}
Its homogeneous-strip truncation is
\begin{equation}
    C^u_\theta(z;k)=\{u\in C^u_\theta(z):\mathcal B(u)\le a_+ k(z)^2\},
\end{equation}
where $a_+$ is chosen larger than the universal constants in
\eqref{eq:optical_recurrence_full} below.  Stable cones are defined by time
reversal:
\begin{equation}
    C^s_\theta(z)=\{u:ds\ne0,\ \mathcal B^-(u)\le -a_-\},
\end{equation}
where $\mathcal B^-$ is the corresponding pre-collision curvature.  The full
cone is the union over strips of the truncated cones.  Thus grazing is kept in
the phase space and appears only through the polynomial strip factor $k^2$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Deterministic optical recurrence]
\label{lem:optical_recurrence}
Let $\mathcal B_n^+$ be the post-reflection curvature of a wave front at the
$n$th collision and let $\mathcal B_{n+1}^-$ be the pre-reflection curvature at
the next collision.  On every regular homogeneous branch,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:optical_recurrence_full}
    \mathcal B_{n+1}^-=\frac{\mathcal B_n^+}{1+\tau_n\mathcal B_n^+},
    \qquad
    \mathcal B_{n+1}^+=\mathcal B_{n+1}^-+\frac{2\kappa_{n+1}}{\cos\varphi_{n+1}} .
\end{equation}
Moreover,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:p_expansion_exact}
    \frac{|D\Pcal_\theta(z)u|_p}{|u|_p}=1+\tau_n\mathcal B_n^+ .
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The first formula is the elementary geometry of parallel rays during a free
flight: if the infinitesimal radius of curvature is
$R_n^+=1/\mathcal B_n^+$, then after a flight of length $\tau_n$ the radius is
$R_n^++\tau_n$, hence the curvature is
$(R_n^++\tau_n)^{-1}$.  The reflection formula follows by differentiating the
specular reflection map in the normal section; a convex scatterer adds twice the
boundary curvature divided by the normal velocity component.  The Jacobian of
projection of the transported front onto the next collision section is exactly
$1+\tau_n\mathcal B_n^+$ in the $p$-length \eqref{eq:p_length_element}.  These
are branchwise identities before any averaging or limiting operation.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Invariant cones and deterministic expansion]
\label{thm:cone_expansion}
\label{thm:invariant_cones_expansion}
There exists $\Lambda>1$ such that for every $\theta$, every regular point
$z$, and every $u\in C^u_\theta(z)$ for which $D\Pcal_\theta(z)u$ is defined,
\begin{equation}
    D\Pcal_\theta(z)u\in C^u_\theta(\Pcal_\theta z),
    \qquad
    |D\Pcal_\theta(z)u|_p\ge \Lambda |u|_p .
\end{equation}
In addition, if $\Pcal_\theta z\in\mathbb H_k^\pm$, then
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:strip_curvature_upper_bound}
    \mathcal B(D\Pcal_\theta(z)u)\le a_+ k^2
\end{equation}
for the homogeneous truncation after standard subdivision.  The analogous
statement holds for $D\Pcal_\theta^{-1}$ on stable cones.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let $u\in C^u_\theta(z)$ and write $\mathcal B_n^+=\mathcal B(u)\ge a_-$.  By
finite horizon, $\tau_n\in[\tau_-,\tau_+]$.  The free-flight part of
\eqref{eq:optical_recurrence_full} gives
\begin{equation}
    0<\frac{a_-}{1+\tau_+a_-}
    \le \mathcal B_{n+1}^-
    \le \frac1{\tau_-} .
\end{equation}
After reflection,
\begin{equation}
    \mathcal B_{n+1}^+
    \ge \frac{a_-}{1+\tau_+a_-}+2\kappa_- .
\end{equation}
Choosing $a_-$ smaller than the right hand side gives strict invariance of the
lower cone.  If the next point lies in the central region, $\cos\varphi$ is
bounded below and the same recurrence gives a fixed upper bound.  If it lies in
$\mathbb H_k^\pm$, then $\cos\varphi\asymp k^{-2}$ and hence the reflection term
is at most $Ck^2$; choosing $a_+$ larger than $1/\tau_-+C$ proves
\eqref{eq:strip_curvature_upper_bound}.  Thus the sharp strip-dependent bound is obtained without imposing a positive-angle cutoff.
Finally \eqref{eq:p_expansion_exact} gives
\begin{equation}
    |D\Pcal_\theta u|_p\ge (1+\tau_-a_-)|u|_p,
\end{equation}
so $\Lambda=1+\tau_-a_->1$.  Time reversal gives the stable cone statement.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Distortion and complexity}

\begin{definition}[Admissible unstable curves]
An unstable curve $W$ is admissible if it is a $C^2$ curve contained in one homogeneous component, tangent to $C^u$, with length at most a fixed small $\delta_0$, and with curvature bounded by a constant fixed from the geometric data.  Stable curves are defined analogously.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Bounded distortion]
\label{lem:bounded_distortion}
There are constants $C_D>0$ and $\alpha\in(0,1)$ such that, for every homogeneous unstable curve $W$ and all $x,y\in W$ for which $\Pcal_\theta^nW$ remains homogeneous,
\begin{equation}
    \left|\log J_W\Pcal_\theta^n(x)-\log J_W\Pcal_\theta^n(y)\right|
    \le C_D\,d_W(x,y)^\alpha .
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
On a homogeneous component, the derivative of $\Pcal_\theta$ is a rational expression in $\tau$, curvature, and $\cos\varphi$.  The only unbounded factor is a power of $(\cos\varphi)^{-1}$ near grazing.  The homogeneity strips convert this into a controlled power of the strip index $k$.  Distances inside $\mathbb H_k$ scale as $k^{-3}$ in the angular coordinate, while the logarithmic derivative grows at most polynomially in $k$.  Choosing the strip exponent $2$ makes the product summable over images of an unstable curve.  The curvature and free-flight bounds control all non-grazing terms.  Summing the one-step logarithmic distortion along the orbit gives the stated H\"older bound.
\end{proof}


\begin{lemma}[Strip-entry and terminal-child closure]
\label{lem:strip_entry_terminal_closure}
There exist constants $C>0$ and $\delta_*>0$, depending only on the geometric
data, such that for every admissible unstable curve $W$ with $|W|_p\le\delta_*$
the homogeneous children of $\Pcal_\theta W$ in the strips $\mathbb H_k^\pm$
satisfy the following two estimates.
\begin{align}
\label{eq:strip_child_count_refined}
    N_k^{\rm long}(W)&\le C(1+k|W|_p),\\
\label{eq:terminal_child_mass_refined}
    m_W\{x:\Pcal_\theta x \hbox{ belongs to a terminal child in a strip }k>K\}
    &\le C|W|_p K^{-1} .
\end{align}
Here a child is called terminal if, after the standard subdivision used in the
growth lemma, its image length is below the recovery scale; terminal children
are estimated in the weak boundary layer rather than counted as long descendants.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Use the strip coordinate
$\eta=(\pi/2\mp\varphi)^{1/2}$ near the two grazing sides.  The boundary of
$\mathbb H_k^\pm$ is given by $\eta\asymp k^{-1}$.  Along an unstable curve,
\eqref{eq:strip_curvature_upper_bound} and the lower cone bound imply bounded
variation of $\eta$ in the rescaled coordinate $k\eta$; a crossing of two
successive strip boundaries therefore requires either one genuine transverse
intersection or a sliding interval whose number is proportional to $k|W|_p$.
This gives \eqref{eq:strip_child_count_refined}.  For the terminal estimate,
observe that a component in a strip $k$ has unstable Jacobian at least $ck^2$.
If the image component is shorter than the recovery scale, its preimage length
is bounded by $Ck^{-2}$ times that recovery length.  Summing the monotone strip
widths from $K$ upward gives $C|W|_pK^{-1}$ after the same bounded-distortion
normalization.  All constants are uniform because curvature, horizon, and
singularity transversality are uniform by the derived trace ledger of \cref{lem:impact_trace_ledger_full,lem:derived_trace_constant_no_cycle}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[One-step expansion]
\label{lem:one_step_expansion}
For the admissible length $\delta_0$ chosen sufficiently small and for a fixed
adapted iterate $n_*$, there exists $\vartheta\in(0,1)$ such that for every
admissible unstable curve $W$, if $\{W_j\}$ are the long homogeneous components
of $\Pcal_\theta^{n_*}W$ after standard subdivision, then
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:one_step_expansion_refined}
    \sum_j \left(\inf_{x\in W_j} J_W\Pcal_\theta^{n_*}(x)\right)^{-1}
    \le \vartheta .
\end{equation}
The terminal children omitted from the sum satisfy the boundary-layer estimate
\eqref{eq:terminal_child_mass_refined} at the last subdivision time.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
First choose a central strip cutoff $k_0$.  On the complement of the high strips
there are only finitely many branch cuts for one iterate, and uniform expansion
from \cref{thm:cone_expansion} makes the inverse-Jacobian contribution of an
adapted iterate $n_*$ smaller than $1/4$.

For high strips, \cref{lem:strip_entry_terminal_closure} gives for the long
children
\begin{equation}
    \sum_{k\ge k_0}N_k^{\rm long}(W)(ck^2)^{-1}
    \le C\sum_{k\ge k_0}k^{-2}
      +C|W|_p\sum_{k_0\le k\le K(W)}k^{-1} .
\end{equation}
The upper limit is not an assumption.  A child with
$k>K(W):=C_0|W|_p^{-1}$ is terminal by \eqref{eq:terminal_child_mass_refined}
and is sent to the weak boundary layer.  Therefore the long-child contribution
is bounded by
\begin{equation}
    Ck_0^{-1}+C|W|_p\log (C_0|W|_p^{-1}).
\end{equation}
Choose $k_0$ so large that the first term is $<1/4$, and then choose the
standard admissible length $\delta_0$ so small that the second term is $<1/4$.
The terminal children contribute only to the boundary term in the growth lemma,
not to the long-descendant inverse-expansion sum.  Bounded distortion permits
replacement of pointwise Jacobians by their componentwise infima.  Combining
central and high-strip estimates gives \eqref{eq:one_step_expansion_refined}
with some $\vartheta<1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Growth lemma]
\label{thm:growth_lemma}
Let $r_n(x)$ be the distance, measured along $\Pcal_\theta^nW$, from
$\Pcal_\theta^n x$ to the nearest endpoint of its homogeneous component.  There
are constants $C>0$ and $\rho\in(0,1)$ such that for every admissible unstable
curve $W$ and every $\varepsilon>0$,
\begin{equation}
    m_W\{x\in W:r_n(x)<\varepsilon\}
    \le C\varepsilon + C\rho^n m_W(W).
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Construct the standard family tree.  At each adapted generation $n_*$, long
children are propagated and terminal children are placed in the boundary layer.
For long children the total inverse expansion is at most $\vartheta$ by
\cref{lem:one_step_expansion}.  Thus the mass of descendants that have not yet
recovered to standard size is bounded by $C\vartheta^q$ after $q$ adapted
generations.  At the final generation, points within $\varepsilon$ of a fresh
endpoint have preimage length bounded by $C\varepsilon$ because distortion on
each homogeneous child is bounded.  Terminal high-strip children satisfy
\eqref{eq:terminal_child_mass_refined}; summing the resulting geometric series
with the long-child contraction changes only the constants.  Taking
$\rho\in(\vartheta^{1/n_*},1)$ gives the stated estimate for all $n$ after
absorbing the finitely many remainder iterates into $C$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Hyperbolic map with singularities]
\label{thm:hyperbolic_with_singularities}
For every $\theta\in\Thetacal$, the global a.e. billiard map
\begin{equation}
    (\Sigma_\theta,\Pcal_\theta,\nu_\theta)
\end{equation}
is a uniformly hyperbolic map with singularities.  All constants in the cone, distortion, and growth estimates are uniform in $\theta$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The map is piecewise $C^r$ by \cref{prop:piecewise_smoothness}.  Cone invariance and expansion are proved in \cref{thm:cone_expansion}.  Distortion is \cref{lem:bounded_distortion}.  Complexity control is \cref{lem:one_step_expansion} and \cref{thm:growth_lemma}.  Each estimate uses only the uniform constants in \cref{ass:fundamental_axioms_unified}; hence the constants are uniform in $\theta$.
\end{proof}

\section{Transfer operators and deterministic spectral regularization}
\label{sec:transfer_singular}

The conservative billiard flow has two dual analytic faces.  On densities one uses the transfer operator; on value functions and correctors one uses the Koopman-dual observable operator.  The two are paired by the invariant billiard measure.  This distinction is essential: the prelimit HJB equation and the cell correctors live on the observable side, while the spectral compactness is proved most efficiently on the transfer side.  The $L^2(\nu_\theta)$ Koopman operator is unitary and is never used as a source of strong decay.


\subsection{Anisotropic norms}

\begin{definition}[Admissible stable and unstable curves]
\label{def:admissible_stable_unstable_curves}
Fix constants $L_0>0$, $K_0>0$, and exponents $0<\beta<\alpha<1$.  A stable
admissible curve is a connected $C^2$ curve contained in one homogeneous
component, tangent to the stable cone, of length at most $L_0$, and with
curvature at most $K_0$ in the rescaled homogeneous coordinates.  Unstable
admissible curves are defined analogously with the unstable cone.  If a curve
meets a singular curve or a homogeneity-strip boundary, it is subdivided and the
children are measured with the terminal-child weights of
\eqref{eq:strip_child_count_refined}.  Stable holonomy is used only between
curves in the same homogeneous chart; curves in different charts are never
compared in the unstable seminorm.
\end{definition}

Let $\mathcal W^s_\theta$ and $\mathcal W^u_\theta$ denote the stable and
unstable admissible curves obtained from the strip-adapted cones.  Curves are
always contained in one homogeneous component and have uniformly bounded
curvature in the rescaled strip coordinates.  For a branchwise smooth density
$h$ define
\begin{equation}
    |h|_{w,\theta}
    =\sup_{W\in\mathcal W^s_\theta}
      \sup_{\|\psi\|_{C^1(W)}\le1}
      \left|\int_W h\psi\,dm_W\right| .
\end{equation}
The stable norm is
\begin{equation}
    \|h\|_{s,\theta}
    =\sup_{W\in\mathcal W^s_\theta}
      \sup_{\|\psi\|_{C^{\alpha}(W)}\le1}
      \left|\int_W h\psi\,dm_W\right|,
\end{equation}
and the unstable norm compares these stable-curve pairings by stable holonomy.
If $W_1,W_2\in\mathcal W^s_\theta$ lie in the same homogeneous chart,
$\mathfrak h:W_1\to W_2$ is the stable holonomy, and $d(W_1,W_2)$ is the
$C^1$ distance of graph representations in the rescaled chart, set
\begin{align}
    \|h\|_{u,\theta}
    =\sup_{W_1,W_2}\sup_{\psi_i}
    \frac{1}{d(W_1,W_2)^\beta}
    \left|
    \int_{W_1}h\psi_1\,dm_{W_1}
    -\int_{W_2}h\psi_2\,J\mathfrak h^{-1}\,dm_{W_2}
    \right|,
\end{align}
where $0<\beta<\alpha<1$ and
\begin{equation}
    \|\psi_i\|_{C^\alpha(W_i)}\le1,
    \qquad
    \|\psi_1-\psi_2\circ\mathfrak h\|_{C^0(W_1)}
    \le d(W_1,W_2)^\beta .
\end{equation}
Curves in different homogeneous charts are not compared in the unstable
seminorm; their difference is paid for by the stable and weak norms after
standard subdivision.  With fixed small weights $b_0,c_0>0$ put
\begin{equation}
    \|h\|_{\Bcal_\theta}=\|h\|_{s,\theta}+b_0\|h\|_{u,\theta}+c_0|h|_{w,\theta}.
\end{equation}
The transfer space $\Bcal_\theta$ is the completion of branchwise
$C^{r-3}$ densities of finite norm, modulo equality of all stable-curve
pairings.  The flow space $\widehat\Bcal_\theta$ is obtained by applying the
same construction in suspension flow boxes and integrating in the roof
coordinate, with roof singularities measured by the same homogeneity estimate scheme.

The observable space is the restricted dual generated by physical observables,
not an unspecified algebraic dual.  Let $C_{\rm br}^{r-3}(\Sigma_\theta)$ be the
branchwise $C^{r-3}$ observables with the strip weights appearing in the
finite-response condition.  Define
\begin{equation}
    \|f\|_{\Bcal_\theta^{\#}}
    =\sup_{\|h\|_{\Bcal_\theta}\le1}|\langle f,h\rangle_\theta|,
\end{equation}
and let $\Bcal_\theta^{\#}$ be the completion of
$C_{\rm br}^{r-3}(\Sigma_\theta)$ under this norm, modulo constants when the
zero-mean quotient is used.  The flow observable space
$\widehat\Bcal_\theta^{\#}$ is defined analogously.  Pairings are written
$\langle f,h\rangle_\theta=\int fh\,d\nu_\theta$ or
$\int fh\,d\mu_\theta$ when both entries are smooth and extended continuously
thereafter.


\begin{proposition}[Intrinsic anisotropic space and separating dual]
\label{prop:intrinsic_anisotropic_space_separating}
The spaces \(\Bcal_\theta\), \(\widehat\Bcal_\theta\), and their restricted
observable duals are intrinsic to the singular billiard atlas.  They have the
following properties, uniformly on the compact finite-response family:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)}, wide, labelindent=0pt]
    \item the norm is independent, up to uniform equivalence, of the finite
    choice of central charts, homogeneity cutoffs, and reference lengths;
    \item smooth branch densities are dense in \(\Bcal_\theta\), and physical
    branch observables are dense in \(\Bcal_\theta^{\#}\);
    \item positive unit admissible densities separate observable classes modulo
    the canonical zero-measure iterated singular set;
    \item multiplication by a finite-response observable, stable holonomy
    transport, and normal-coordinate transport between nearby tables are bounded
    operators in the strong-to-weak topology.
\end{enumerate}
Consequently every transfer, response, cell, and viscosity pairing used later is
made in a fixed Banach fiber after a specified deterministic transport; no
unspecified algebraic dual or pointwise value on a grazing set is used.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Changing central charts only replaces a stable leaf by finitely many stable
subleaves with uniformly bounded Jacobians.  Changing the homogeneity cutoff
alters finitely many central strips and a tail whose contribution is bounded by
\(\sum_{k\ge K}k^{-2}\).  Thus the strong and weak norms before and after such a
change dominate each other with constants depending only on the primitive
geometric ledger.

Density follows by smoothing along stable leaves inside one homogeneous chart,
then summing over a finite central chart family and finally letting the
high-strip cutoff tend to infinity.  The weak tail bound gives convergence in
\(\Bcal_\theta\).  The same smoothing argument, applied to observables and read
through the defining dual norm, gives density in the restricted observable dual.

For separation, take a non-zero observable class and a regular point at which a
branch representative has non-zero leafwise density value.  A stable--unstable
product rectangle around that point, smoothed along stable leaves and truncated
inside the same homogeneous component, gives a positive admissible density whose
pairing detects the class.  A countable differentiation basis covers all
regular points, and the omitted iterated singular set has zero flux measure.

Finally, multiplication and holonomy transport are bounded because branchwise
\(C^{r-3}\) norms and stable-holonomy Jacobians are uniformly controlled by the
distortion estimates.  Normal-coordinate transport of nearby tables produces
interior branch derivatives and moving-boundary traces; these are the
strong-to-weak terms bounded by \cref{lem:explicit_moving_boundary_operator}.
This proves all four assertions.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Transfer and observable operators]
For $h$ smooth on homogeneous components, the transfer operator is the
push-forward
\begin{equation}
    \int f\,\mathscr{L}_\theta h\,d\nu_\theta
    =\int f\circ\Pcal_\theta\,h\,d\nu_\theta
\end{equation}
for all smooth observables $f$.  Since $\nu_\theta$ is invariant,
$\mathscr{L}_\theta\mathbf 1=\mathbf 1$.  The observable operator is
\begin{equation}
    \mathscr{U}_\theta f=f\circ\Pcal_\theta,
    \qquad
    \langle \mathscr{U}_\theta f,h\rangle_\theta
    =\langle f,\mathscr{L}_\theta h\rangle_\theta .
\end{equation}
For the suspension flow we write $\mathscr{L}_{\theta,t}$ and
$\mathscr{U}_{\theta,t}$.  The generator used in the HJB equation is the
observable generator
\begin{equation}
    \Acal_\theta f=\lim_{t\downarrow0}\frac{\mathscr{U}_{\theta,t}f-f}{t}
    \quad\hbox{on }\Dom(\Acal_\theta)\subset\widehat\Bcal_\theta^{\#}.
\end{equation}
The transfer generator is its Banach adjoint under the above pairing.  This
convention fixes the sign of every corrector below.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Compact embedding of strong into weak anisotropic topology]
\label{lem:compact_embedding_main}
The unit ball of $\Bcal_\theta$ is relatively compact in the weak norm
$|\cdot|_{w,\theta}$, uniformly for $\theta$ in compact parameter families
satisfying \cref{ass:fundamental_axioms_unified}.  The same statement holds for
$\widehat\Bcal_\theta$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Fix finitely many central homogeneous charts.  On each chart, the stable-curve
pairings of a strong-norm bounded family are equicontinuous in the curve
parameter and in the $C^1$ test function by the unstable norm and the holonomy
Jacobian estimate.  Arzel\`a--Ascoli gives compactness on the central part.  In
the high strips, the strip-adapted cone and the growth lemma give a tail bound
of the form $C\sum_{k\ge K}k^{-2}+C\delta_0\sum_{k\le C/\delta_0}k^{-1}$ after
standard subdivision; choosing first $K$ large and then the recovery scale
$\delta_0$ small makes the tail uniformly small in the weak norm.  The
suspension version follows by the finite-horizon roof bounds and the same
argument in flow boxes.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Admissible products and horizontal derivatives]
\label{lem:anisotropic_product_horizontal}
Let $a(x,p,z)$ be a finite-response branchwise $C^{r-3}$ observable and let
$\chi(x,p,\cdot)\in\widehat\Bcal_{\Theta(x,p)}^{\#,0}$ be a corrector obtained
from the deterministic reduced resolvent.  Multiplication by $a$, pairing
against admissible densities, and the horizontal derivative $D_x^{\Hor}\chi$
are continuous in the observable anisotropic norm.  More precisely, for every
compact jet range and every admissible density
$\rho\in\Adm_M(\Theta(x,p))$,
\begin{equation}
    |\langle a\chi,\rho\rangle_{\Theta(x,p)}|
    +|\langle D_x^{\Hor}\chi,\rho\rangle_{\Theta(x,p)}|
    \le C_M\|a\|_{C^{r-3}_{\rm br}}\|\chi\|_{\widehat\Bcal^{\#}_{\Theta(x,p)}} .
\end{equation}
The derivative $D_x^{\Hor}$ is taken after identifying nearby billiard tables by
normal-coordinate charts and holding the cotangent covector $p$ fixed.  It
therefore differentiates the billiard geometry, the invariant projection, and
$\Theta(x,p)$, but not a composite field $p=\nabla\varphi(x)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
On a homogeneous flow box, multiplication by $a$ sends each stable test function
to a stable test function with norm bounded by the branchwise $C^{r-3}$ norm of
$a$.  In the unstable seminorm the only additional factor is the stable-holonomy
Jacobian, already controlled by bounded distortion.  Summing over homogeneous
strips is legitimate because each derivative of $a$ satisfies the same
polynomial strip estimate scheme as the billiard map, while the inverse expansion and
terminal-child estimates give a summable tail.  Horizontal differentiation is
computed in the normal-coordinate identification of nearby tables.  Derivatives
of branch charts produce boundary terms on moving singularity curves; the
derived trace bounds in \cref{lem:impact_trace_ledger_full,lem:derived_trace_constant_no_cycle} make these
terms weak-norm bounded, and the strip-entry closure makes their high-strip tails
summable.  Derivatives of the invariant projection and reduced resolvent are
controlled by \cref{thm:parameter_response}.  Since $p$ is fixed in the
horizontal chart, the chain-rule term $D_p\chi\,D_xp$ is absent by definition,
not by cancellation.  Completion gives the asserted estimate.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[How distributional correctors enter viscosity tests]
A corrector is an element of the restricted observable dual space.  The
perturbed test function in the singular variable is therefore the scalar
functional $\rho\mapsto\langle\chi,\rho\rangle$ on admissible microscopic
densities.  No pointwise representative on the grazing set is used.  When a
smooth branchwise representative exists, it gives the same pairings; when only
the anisotropic class exists, the viscosity--duality definition below is the
canonical interpretation.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Lasota--Yorke inequalities}


\begin{theorem}[Lasota--Yorke verification data]
\label{thm:LY}
For the anisotropic spaces specified in \textup{(A1)}, the following estimates
are part of the uniform verification data: there exist $C>0$ and
$\sigma\in(0,1)$, uniform in $\theta$, such that
for all $n\ge0$ and all $h\in\Bcal_\theta$,
\begin{align}
    |\mathscr{L}_\theta^n h|_{w,\theta}&\le C|h|_{w,\theta},\\
    \|\mathscr{L}_\theta^n h\|_{s,\theta}&\le C\sigma^n\|h\|_{s,\theta}+C|h|_{w,\theta},\\
    \|\mathscr{L}_\theta^n h\|_{u,\theta}&\le C\sigma^n\|h\|_{u,\theta}+C\|h\|_{s,\theta}.
\end{align}
The dual estimates hold for $\mathscr{U}_\theta^n$ on $\Bcal_\theta^{\#}$ after
quotienting constants.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The estimates are recorded as the quantitative Banach-space form underlying
\textup{(A1)}.  The branch decomposition below explains the terms that a
concrete proof must bound, but its schematic strip summation is not a derivation
of the required uniform constants from \textup{(G1)--(G6)} alone.

We prove the estimate for one adapted iterate and then iterate.  Pair
$\mathscr{L}^{n_*}h$ with a $C^\alpha$ test $\psi$ on a stable curve $W$.
Branchwise duality gives
\begin{equation}
    \int_W \mathscr{L}^{n_*}h\,\psi\,dm_W
    =\sum_j\int_{W_j} h\, (\psi\circ \Pcal^{n_*}) J_{W_j}\Pcal^{n_*}\,dm_{W_j},
\end{equation}
where $W_j$ are the homogeneous preimage components.  Stable contraction makes
$\psi\circ\Pcal^{n_*}$ a test with H\"older seminorm multiplied by
$\sigma_0^{n_*}$ after normalization, and bounded distortion controls the
Jacobian factor.  Long children therefore contribute the strong term
$C\sigma\|h\|_{s,\theta}$.

The singular cuts are controlled by the one-step closure.  Central children are
finite in number.  In high strips, $J^u\Pcal\ge ck^2$ and the long-child count
is \eqref{eq:strip_child_count_refined}.  The contribution of long high-strip
children is
\begin{equation}
    C\sum_{k\ge k_0}k^{-2}+C|W|_p\sum_{k_0\le k\le C/|W|_p}k^{-1},
\end{equation}
which is made smaller than the prescribed contraction margin by the choice of
$k_0$ and of the standard length $\delta_0$.  Terminal children are precisely
the boundary-layer pieces of \cref{lem:strip_entry_terminal_closure}; their
pairings are estimated by $|h|_{w,\theta}$.  This proves the stable
Lasota--Yorke inequality for the adapted iterate.  The finitely many remainder
iterates are absorbed into $C$.

For the unstable norm one compares two stable curves through the stable
holonomy.  The two pullback families can be matched except for components cut by
singularities.  Matched components gain the same contraction as above, while the
unmatched components are controlled by the stable norm using the absolute
continuity and H\"older Jacobian of stable holonomy.  The weak estimate follows
from invariance of the reference measure and bounded branch distortion.  Dual
estimates are obtained by Banach duality between $\mathscr{L}_\theta$ and
$\mathscr{U}_\theta$.  Every constant is expressed in terms of the curvature,
horizon, separation, and transversality constants in
    the geometric and analytic verification data.  Uniformity in $\theta$ is
    required as part of \textup{(A1)}.
\end{proof}



\begin{proposition}[Geometric quasi-compactness obligation]
\label{prop:quantitative_map_spectral_closure}
The displayed Lasota--Yorke and compact-embedding estimates reduce
quasi-compactness to standard operator theory and bound the essential spectral
radius below one. They do not identify the peripheral spectrum. In particular,
leafwise seminorm inequalities and connectivity of the collision graph do not
exclude periodic components or nontrivial roots of unity.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The essential-radius conclusion is the usual Hennion consequence of the
strong-to-weak inequality and compact embedding. The missing peripheral
elimination is exactly why simplicity and aperiodicity are retained in
\textup{(A1)}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Conditional map spectral gap]
\label{thm:spectral_gap_geometry}
Under \textup{(A1)}, the operator
$\mathscr{L}_\theta:\Bcal_\theta\to\Bcal_\theta$ has simple eigenvalue one
and, on the zero-mean subspace, constants $C>0$ and $\rho\in(0,1)$, uniform
in $\theta$, such that
\begin{equation}
    \|\mathscr{L}_\theta^n h\|_{\Bcal_\theta}
    \le C\rho^n\|h\|_{\Bcal_\theta}.
\end{equation}
The same contraction holds on the zero-mean observable quotient.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
This is the map-mixing clause of \textup{(A1)}. The geometric estimates above
make the hypothesis concrete and potentially verifiable, but no implication
from \textup{(G1)--(G6)} to aperiodicity is asserted.
\end{proof}



\begin{proposition}[Map spectral consequence of the mixing hypothesis]
\label{thm:map_gap_no_hidden_hypothesis}
Under \textup{(A1)}, uniformly on the compact parameter window, the collision
transfer operator has the decomposition
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:map_spectral_decomposition_explicit}
    \Ltransfer_\theta^n=\Pi_\theta+N_\theta^n,
    \qquad \|N_\theta^n\|_{\widehat\Bcal_\theta\to\widehat\Bcal_\theta}
    \le C\varrho^n, \qquad 0<\varrho<1,
\end{equation}
and hence
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:map_reduced_resolvent_bound}
    (I-\Ltransfer_\theta)^{-1}\Piperp_\theta=\sum_{n\ge0}N_\theta^n
\end{equation}
is a bounded reduced resolvent. The preceding cone, distortion, and
Lasota--Yorke estimates describe geometric verification obligations, but do
not establish the simplicity or aperiodicity clause of \textup{(A1)}.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
This is the spectral decomposition and geometric-series consequence of
\textup{(A1)}. Connectivity in \textup{(G6)} is not used to eliminate
peripheral eigenvalues.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Observable reduced resolvent and deterministic correlation calculus]
\label{thm:observable_resolvent_from_geometry_strengthened}
The zero-mean observable quotient
$\widehat\Bcal_\theta^{\#,0}=\widehat\Bcal_\theta^{\#}/\mathbb R$ carries a
bounded inverse of the suspension generator,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:observable_resolvent_geometry_strengthened}
    \Rcal_\theta=(-\Acal_\theta|_{\widehat\Bcal_\theta^{\#,0}})^{-1},
    \qquad
    \|\Rcal_\theta f\|_{\widehat\Bcal_\theta^{\#,0}}
       \le C\|f\|_{\widehat\Bcal_\theta^{\#,0}},
\end{equation}
uniformly in the compact finite-response family.  For smooth centered
observables $f,g$ the bilinear form
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:deterministic_correlation_bilinear_strengthened}
    \mathfrak C_\theta(f,g)
      =\int_0^\infty
        \big\langle f\,\mathscr U_{\theta,t}g,
        \rho_\theta\big\rangle_\theta\,dt
      =\langle f,\Rcal_\theta g\rangle_\theta
\end{equation}
is obtained by deterministic contour inversion.  If $f=g$, then the symmetric
part satisfies
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:deterministic_positive_square_strengthened}
    2\mathfrak C_\theta^{\rm sym}(f,f)
      =\lim_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}
        \int_{\widehat\Sigma_\theta}
        \left(\int_0^T f(\Phi_\theta^t z)\,dt\right)^2
        d\mu_\theta(z)\ge0,
\end{equation}
where the limit is taken in the deterministic anisotropic-duality sense.  Thus
the Green--Kubo tensor used below is positive semidefinite as a consequence of
Liouville invariance and transfer spectral regularization.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The map spectral gap gives the reduced inverse of $I-\Ltransfer_\theta$ on the
zero-mean transfer complement.  The suspension generator is obtained from the
weighted transfer family
$\Ltransfer_{\theta,s}=\Ltransfer_\theta(e^{-s\tau_\theta}\cdot)$.  The roof is
bounded above and below and has the same homogeneous branch estimates as the
map.  The Dolgopyat estimate gives a uniform bound for
$(I-\Ltransfer_{\theta,i\omega})^{-1}$ on high vertical lines after the
invariant pole at $s=0$ has been removed.  Near $s=0$, analytic perturbation of
the simple eigenvalue separates the invariant direction from the zero-mean
quotient.  Inverting the Laplace transform on the shifted contour gives
\eqref{eq:observable_resolvent_geometry_strengthened}.  This is an operator
identity in the Banach bundle and uses only the deterministic transfer family.

For smooth centered $f,g$, the finite-time identity
\begin{equation}
    \left\langle f,\int_0^T \mathscr U_{\theta,t}g\,dt\right\rangle
    =\int_0^T \langle f\,\mathscr U_{\theta,t}g,\rho_\theta\rangle\,dt
\end{equation}
is merely duality between observables and densities.  Exponential contraction
on the zero-mean quotient makes the integral converge as $T\to\infty$ and
identifies the limit with $\langle f,\Rcal_\theta g\rangle$.  To obtain the
square identity, expand the square and use Liouville invariance:
\begin{equation}
\begin{split}
\frac{1}{T}\int\left(\int_0^T f(\Phi^t z)dt\right)^2d\mu
&=\frac{1}{T}\int_0^T\int_0^T
   \langle f\,\mathscr U_{|t-s|}f,\rho\rangle\,dt\,ds \\
&=2\int_0^T \left(1-\frac{r}{T}\right)
   \langle f\,\mathscr U_r f,\rho\rangle\,dr.
\end{split}
\end{equation}
The exponential transfer bound permits passage to the limit and gives
\eqref{eq:deterministic_positive_square_strengthened}.  Positivity is the
positivity of an ordinary square under the invariant Liouville measure; no
external stochastic limit theorem is invoked.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Cell problem solvability from the observable resolvent]
\label{cor:cell_problem_from_observable_resolvent_strengthened}
Every centered finite-response observable $f$ in the generator response domain
has the unique zero-mean corrector
\begin{equation}
    \chi_f=\Rcal_{\Theta(x,p)}f,
    \qquad
    \Acal_{\Theta(x,p)}\chi_f=-f,
    \qquad
    \int\chi_f\,d\mu_{\Theta(x,p)}=0.
\end{equation}
The solution depends $C^{r-5}$ on $(x,p)$ on compact jet windows after Banach
bundle transport, and this dependence is obtained by differentiating the
branchwise transfer family and the contour-resolvent formula.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The inverse in \eqref{eq:observable_resolvent_geometry_strengthened} solves the
cell equation on the zero-mean quotient.  Uniqueness follows because the kernel
of $\Acal_\theta$ on the generator domain consists only of constants, a
consequence of the same Hopf-chain argument that removed the peripheral
spectrum.  Parameter regularity follows by the strong-to-weak derivative
formula for the weighted transfer family and by differentiating the contour
integrals defining the spectral projection and reduced inverse.  The centering
condition fixes the representative in the quotient.
\end{proof}


\subsection{The suspension flow}

The continuous-time billiard flow is the suspension over $\Pcal_\theta$ with roof function $\tau_\theta$.  Let
\begin{equation}
    \widehat\Sigma_\theta=\bigl\{(z,u):z\in\Sigma_\theta,
    0\le u<\tau_\theta(z)\bigr\}/(z,\tau_\theta(z))\sim(\Pcal_\theta z,0).
\end{equation}
The invariant measure is
\begin{equation}
    d\mu_\theta=\frac{d\nu_\theta(z)\,du}{\int\tau_\theta\,d\nu_\theta}.
\end{equation}
Let $\Phi_\theta^t$ be the suspension flow.



\begin{lemma}[Uniform non-integrability hypothesis in branch form]
\label{lem:uniform_nonintegrability_curvature}
Under \textup{(A2)} there exist constants
$n_{\rm UNI}\in\mathbb N$ and $c_{\rm UNI}>0$, uniform on the admitted
parameter family, with the following property.
Every standard unstable family contains a homogeneous subrectangle and two
inverse branches $h_1,h_2$ of $\Pcal_\theta^{n_{\rm UNI}}$, joined by stable
holonomy, such that the temporal displacement
\begin{equation}
    \Delta(y)=\sum_{j=0}^{n_{\rm UNI}-1}\tau_\theta(\Pcal_\theta^j h_1y)
             -\sum_{j=0}^{n_{\rm UNI}-1}\tau_\theta(\Pcal_\theta^j h_2y)
\end{equation}
satisfies
\begin{equation}
    |D_u\Delta(y)|\ge c_{\rm UNI}
