\end{equation}
in the rescaled unstable coordinate on that subrectangle.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
This is the branchwise formulation of \textup{(A2)}.  Curvature identities can
help verify it in a concrete family, but a pointwise nonzero temporal
displacement does not yield a uniform lower bound over the countably many
grazing branches, and compactness of finitely many central charts does not close
that gap.  The earlier curvature-only proof is therefore not used.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Temporal displacement derivative in blown-up coordinates]
\label{lem:temporal_derivative_identity_blowup}
Let $h_1,h_2$ be two homogeneous inverse branches of $\Pcal_\theta^n$ whose
images are matched by stable holonomy over a standard unstable base.  In the
rescaled unstable coordinate $u$, the temporal displacement
$\Delta_n=\tau_n\circ h_1-\tau_n\circ h_2$ satisfies
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:temporal_derivative_identity_blowup}
    D_u\Delta_n
      =\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}
       \left(
       \frac{2\kappa(q_j)}{\cos\varphi_j}\,\Xi_j
       +\tau_j\mathcal E_j+\mathcal H_j
       \right).
\end{equation}
Here $\Xi_j$ is the signed transverse separation of the two transported
wavefronts at the $j$th reflection, $\mathcal E_j$ is a bounded expression in
pre-reflection wavefront curvatures, and $\mathcal H_j$ is the derivative of the
stable-holonomy time shift.  In a strip $\mathbb H_k$, all three quantities are
bounded in the blown-up chart, while the displayed curvature term has a
monotone angular derivative bounded below by a positive multiple of $\kappa_-$
on a fixed substrip.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For one flight, differentiating the impact equation
$G(q+\tau v,\theta)=0$ gives
\begin{equation}
    D\tau(w)=-\frac{\langle Dq(w)+\tau Dv(w),n(q')\rangle}
                    {\langle v,n(q')\rangle}.
\end{equation}
Transport of a wavefront followed by elastic reflection is governed by the
optical recurrence \eqref{eq:optical_recurrence_full}.  The reflection part
adds $2\kappa/\cos\varphi$ to the post-collision curvature.  When two inverse
branches are compared through stable holonomy, the common stable timing term is
absorbed into $\mathcal H_j$, and the curvature contribution is multiplied by
the signed transverse separation $\Xi_j$.  Free-flight curvature transport gives
the bounded error $\tau_j\mathcal E_j$.  Since $\cos\varphi\asymp k^{-2}$ and
$(\pi/2\mp\varphi)^{1/2}\asymp k^{-1}$ on $\mathbb H_k$, the unbounded
coefficient becomes a controlled monotone term in the rescaled coordinate.  The
bounded distortion and stable-holonomy estimates control the remainder.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Curvature excludes temporal coboundaries]
\label{lem:curvature_excludes_temporal_coboundary}
The roof $\tau_\theta$ cannot have vanishing Dolgopyat temporal displacement on
all stable-matched inverse-branch pairs.  Equivalently, the identities
$D_u\Delta_n\equiv0$ for every standard rectangle and every matched branch pair
are incompatible with strict dispersing curvature.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
If all such derivatives vanished, the stable and unstable holonomy time changes
would be path-independent on Hopf rectangles and would define a $C^1$ temporal
potential $\psi$ with roof displacement equal to a stable-plus-unstable
coboundary.  Pull this identity through a single dispersing reflection and
differentiate in the blown-up angular coordinate.  The coboundary side has
bounded rescaled variation because the stable and unstable holonomies have
bounded Jacobian.  The reflection side contains the monotone term
$2\kappa/\cos\varphi$ from
\cref{lem:temporal_derivative_identity_blowup}; its rescaled derivative has a
fixed sign and a positive lower bound on a substrip because $\kappa\ge\kappa_-$.
This contradiction is quantitative after compactness of the central charts and
normalization of high strips.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Dolgopyat contraction required by \textup{(A2)}]
\label{lem:dolgopyat_contraction_deterministic}
Under \textup{(A2)}, let $\mathscr{L}_{\theta,s}h=\mathscr{L}_\theta(e^{-s\tau_\theta}h)$.  There are
$N\in\mathbb N$, $\eta>0$, $C>0$, and $b_0>0$, uniform in $\theta$, such that
for $s=a+ib$, $a\ge0$, $|b|\ge b_0$, and every zero-mean density $h$,
\begin{equation}
    \|\mathscr{L}_{\theta,s}^{N}h\|_{\Bcal_\theta}
    \le (1-\eta)\|h\|_{\Bcal_\theta}+C|b|^{-1}|h|_{w,\theta} .
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Partition each unstable curve into intervals on which the phase difference
$b\Delta$ of \cref{lem:uniform_nonintegrability_curvature} changes by an amount
between two fixed constants.  On paired inverse branches the two complex weights
$e^{-ib\tau_N\circ h_1}$ and $e^{-ib\tau_N\circ h_2}$ cannot have the same
argument throughout such an interval; a $C^1$ partition of unity therefore gives
a fixed cancellation in the strong stable norm.  Intervals for which a pair is
cut by a singular curve are terminal children and are estimated by the weak norm
using \cref{lem:strip_entry_terminal_closure}.  Near-resonant intervals have
length $O(|b|^{-1})$ in the rescaled unstable coordinate and hence contribute the
term $C|b|^{-1}|h|_w$.  Combining this cancellation with the Lasota--Yorke
recovery estimate for long children gives the displayed contraction.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Conditional suspension spectral gap]
\label{thm:flow_spectral_gap}
Assume \textup{(A1)--(A2)}. The zero-mean suspension transfer semigroup
$\mathscr{L}_{\theta,t}$ decays exponentially on the specified anisotropic
spaces, uniformly on the compact parameter window. Equivalently, its
Laplace-family resolvent has the pole-free strip and bounds stipulated in
\textup{(A2)}, and the zero-mean observable generator has a bounded reduced
inverse.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The map gap is \textup{(A1)} and the quantitative non-lattice/UNI and
Dolgopyat estimates are \textup{(A2)}. Standard suspension resolvent inversion
then gives the stated decay. The branch computations preceding this theorem
are verification templates only: dispersing curvature and finite horizon do
not by themselves establish UNI.
\end{proof}



\begin{proposition}[Standard-pair form of the \textup{(A2)} obligation]
\label{prop:constant_level_dolgopyat_induction}
Under \textup{(A2)}, let \(\mathfrak S\) be any standard family whose total standard-family norm is
one.  There exist integers \(n_\star,N_\star\), constants
\(\eta_\star,c_\star,C_\star>0\), and a finite partition of the long recovered
children of \(\Pcal_\theta^{-N_\star}\mathfrak S\) into paired and terminal
classes, with uniform constants supplied by \textup{(A2)}, such that for
\(s=a+ib\), \(a\ge0\), \(|b|\ge b_0\),
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:standard_pair_dolgopyat_induction_bound}
    \sum_{W'\in\mathfrak S_{\rm paired}}
       \left|\int_{W'} e^{-s\tau_{N_\star}}g_{W'}\,d\ell_{W'}\right|
    \le (1-\eta_\star)
       \sum_{W'\in\mathfrak S_{\rm paired}}
       \int_{W'} |g_{W'}|\,d\ell_{W'}
       +C_\star |b|^{-1}\|g\|_{\Bcal},
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:terminal_dolgopyat_tail_bound}
    \sum_{W'\in\mathfrak S_{\rm terminal}}
       \int_{W'} |g_{W'}|\,d\ell_{W'}
    \le C_\star |g|_{w}+\frac{\eta_\star}{4}\|g\|_{\Bcal} .
\end{equation}
These estimates state the standard-pair content that must be verified to invoke
the high-frequency resolvent bound in \cref{thm:flow_spectral_gap}.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Choose \(n_{\rm UNI}\) and \(c_{\rm UNI}\) from
\cref{lem:uniform_nonintegrability_curvature}.  First iterate any standard
family until the growth lemma recovers a fixed fraction of its mass in long
homogeneous components.  On each recovered component the UNI lemma supplies two
inverse descendants joined by stable holonomy and a temporal displacement
\(\Delta\) with \(|D_u\Delta|\ge c_{\rm UNI}\).  Subdivide the base interval into
phase intervals of length between \((8|b|c_{\rm UNI})^{-1}\) and
\((4|b|c_{\rm UNI})^{-1}\), except for a boundary remainder of total length
\(O(|b|^{-1})\).  On every non-remainder interval the two phases
\(e^{-ib\tau_n\circ h_1}\) and \(e^{-ib\tau_n\circ h_2}\) rotate by a definite
relative amount.  A two-branch trigonometric inequality gives
\begin{equation}
    |A e^{i\alpha}+B e^{i\beta}|
    \le (1-\eta_0)(A+B)+C|A-B|
\end{equation}
for nonnegative amplitudes when \(|\alpha-\beta|\) stays in the prescribed
phase window.  Bounded distortion and stable holonomy absolute continuity
control \(|A-B|\) by the unstable variation seminorm.  This gives the first
term on the right side of
\eqref{eq:standard_pair_dolgopyat_induction_bound}.

Children cut by grazing curves, multiple-collision curves, or strip boundaries
are not paired.  The trace ledger \cref{lem:impact_trace_ledger_full} and the
growth lemma give the terminal estimate
\eqref{eq:terminal_dolgopyat_tail_bound}.  The near-resonant and boundary
phase intervals have total length \(O(|b|^{-1})\), which produces the weak-norm
error.  Finally choose the recovery time and the strip cutoff so that the
terminal strong contribution is below \(\eta_\star/4\), and concatenate the
pairing step with the deterministic Lasota--Yorke inequality.  This yields the
operator contraction of \cref{lem:dolgopyat_contraction_deterministic} with
constants uniform over the compact finite-response family.
\end{proof}



\begin{theorem}[Conditional Dolgopyat strip resolvent]
\label{thm:dolgopyat_strip_resolvent_from_pairs}
Under \textup{(A1)--(A2)}, let \(\Ltransfer_{\theta,s}=\Ltransfer_\theta(e^{-s\tau_\theta}\cdot)\) be the
weighted collision operator.  There are constants \(b_0,C,\gamma>0\), depending
on the constants in \textup{(A1)--(A2)},
such that for every allowed table parameter \(\theta\), every
\(s=a+ib\) with \(|b|\ge b_0\) and \(|a|\le\gamma\), and every zero-mass density
\(h\),
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:dolgopyat_strip_resolvent_bound}
    \|(I-\Ltransfer_{\theta,s})^{-1}h\|_{\Bcal_\theta}
    \le C\|h\|_{\Bcal_\theta}.
\end{equation}
This is the high-frequency resolvent consequence stipulated by \textup{(A2)}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Apply \cref{prop:constant_level_dolgopyat_induction} to the standard-family
decomposition of a density in the anisotropic unit ball.  The paired descendants
obey the cancellation estimate \eqref{eq:standard_pair_dolgopyat_induction_bound};
the terminal descendants obey \eqref{eq:terminal_dolgopyat_tail_bound}.  Choose
the recovery time, strip cutoff, and adapted iterate so that the terminal strong
contribution is less than one quarter of the cancellation margin.  The weak
term is then returned to the strong norm by one deterministic Lasota--Yorke
recovery step, while the \(|b|^{-1}\) near-resonant remainder is absorbed by
choosing \(b_0\) sufficiently large.  This gives, for one adapted iterate \(N\),
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:weighted_adapted_iterate_contraction}
    \|\Ltransfer_{\theta,a+ib}^{N}h\|_{\Bcal_\theta}
    \le (1-\eta)\|h\|_{\Bcal_\theta}
\end{equation}
on the zero-mass quotient, uniformly for \(|a|\le\gamma\) after decreasing
\(\gamma\) so that the multiplier \(e^{-a\tau}\) changes the constants by less
than \(\eta/4\).  The finitely many intermediate iterates are bounded by the
weighted Lasota--Yorke estimate because the roof and all of its branchwise
derivatives have the same homogeneity-strip bounds as the collision map.

The Neumann series for the adapted iterate gives a bounded inverse for
\(I-\Ltransfer_{\theta,s}^{N}\).  The algebraic identity
\begin{equation}
    (I-\Ltransfer_{\theta,s})^{-1}
    =(I+\Ltransfer_{\theta,s}+\cdots+\Ltransfer_{\theta,s}^{N-1})
      (I-\Ltransfer_{\theta,s}^{N})^{-1}
    \end{equation}
then gives \eqref{eq:dolgopyat_strip_resolvent_bound}, conditional on the
uniform pairing, terminal-tail, and recovery estimates supplied by
\textup{(A1)--(A2)}.  The displayed induction does not derive those uniform
estimates from curvature alone.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Conditional low-frequency pole removal]
\label{lem:low_frequency_suspension_pole_removal}
Under \textup{(A1)--(A2)}, the only pole of the Laplace transform of the suspension transfer semigroup in
a neighborhood of the imaginary axis is the simple invariant pole at \(s=0\).
On the zero-mean quotient this pole is absent, and the inverse Laplace contour
may be shifted to \(\Re s=-\gamma\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For \(|s|\) small the weighted map operator
\(\Ltransfer_{\theta,s}=\Ltransfer_\theta(e^{-s\tau_\theta}\cdot)\) is an
analytic strong-to-weak perturbation of \(\Ltransfer_\theta\).  The map
spectral theorem gives a simple eigenvalue \(\lambda(s)\) near one.  Its first
derivative is
\begin{equation}
    \lambda'(0)=-\int\tau_\theta\,d\nu_\theta<0,
\end{equation}
because the roof is bounded below.  Thus \(1-\lambda(s)\) has a simple zero at
\(s=0\).  The primitive Hopf-chain aperiodicity of
  \textup{(A1)--(A2)} prevent any other unit-modulus map eigenvalue or roof lattice
pole.  Projecting away constants removes
the residue at \(s=0\).  The high-frequency strip is supplied by
\cref{prop:constant_level_dolgopyat_induction}; compactness covers the bounded
remaining frequencies.  Hence the shifted inverse Laplace contour is legitimate
on the zero-mean observable bundle.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Conditional geometry-to-resolvent dependency chain]
\label{thm:geometry_to_resolvent_chain}
Under \cref{ass:fundamental_axioms_unified} and \textup{(A1)--(A2)}, the reduced
observable resolvent used in every cell equation has the following dependency
chain; the labeled assumption arrows are inputs, not derived implications:
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:geometry_to_resolvent_chain}
\begin{gathered}
\hbox{finite horizon and dispersing curvature}
\Longrightarrow
\hbox{strip-adapted cones, distortion, and growth} \\
\Longrightarrow
\hbox{branchwise verification ledger} \\
\xrightarrow{\textup{(A1)}}
\hbox{simple invariant density and map spectral gap} \\
\xrightarrow{\textup{(A2)}}
\hbox{suspension resolvent strip} \\
\Longrightarrow
(-\Acal_\theta|_{\ker\Pi_\theta})^{-1}
: \widehat\Bcal_\theta^{\#,0}\to\widehat\Bcal_\theta^{\#,0}.
\end{gathered}
\end{equation}
In particular, ordinary curvature and finite horizon do not establish the two
assumption arrows.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Geometry defines the branch map and the optical/strip quantities.  The map gap
is exactly \textup{(A1)} and the suspension strip is exactly \textup{(A2)}.
Integration of the exponential zero-mean semigroup bound then defines the stated
reduced inverse.  No converse implication is asserted.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Conditional spectral and response package]
\label{thm:primitive_spectral_resolvent}
Assume \textup{(A1)--(A3)} for the finite-horizon dispersing billiard family and
the specified compatible finite-response port.  After
quotienting constants, there exist constants $C>0$, $\vartheta\in(0,1)$, and
$\gamma>0$, depending only on the primitive constants, such that for every
allowed table parameter $\theta$:
\begin{align}
\label{eq:primitive_map_gap}
    \|\Ltransfer_\theta^n h\|_{\Bcal_\theta}
    &\le C\vartheta^n\|h\|_{\Bcal_\theta}
       \qquad (\langle 1,h\rangle=0),\\
\label{eq:primitive_flow_gap}
    \|\mathscr U_{\theta,t}f\|_{\widehat\Bcal_\theta^{\#,0}}
    &\le Ce^{-\gamma t}\|f\|_{\widehat\Bcal_\theta^{\#,0}},\\
\label{eq:primitive_reduced_resolvent_bound}
    \left\|\int_0^\infty\mathscr U_{\theta,t}f\,dt\right\|_{\widehat\Bcal_\theta^{\#}}
    &\le C\|f\|_{\widehat\Bcal_\theta^{\#,0}} .
\end{align}
Moreover, for every admitted finite-response parameter curve $\theta_s$ the operator
paths
\begin{equation}
    s\mapsto\Pi_{\theta_s},\qquad
    s\mapsto(-\Acal_{\theta_s}|_{\ker\Pi_{\theta_s}})^{-1}
\end{equation}
are $C^{r-4}$ as strong-to-weak operator-valued maps, with derivatives given by
finite sums of branch-interior derivatives and moving-singularity trace
functionals, with the intermediate mapping bounds stipulated by \textup{(A3)}.
These are consequences of the analytic package, not of ordinary geometry alone.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The map estimate is \textup{(A1)}, the suspension estimate and time-integrated
resolvent bound are \textup{(A2)}, and the differentiability statements are
\textup{(A3)}.  The preceding branch formulae are a verification ledger for
these hypotheses and do not independently prove them.
\end{proof}

\section{Cell problems on anisotropic observable spaces}
\label{sec:cell_anisotropic}

The cell problem is solved for observables, because correctors are inserted into test functions.  Under \textup{(A1)--(A2)}, the observable resolvent is the dual reduced resolvent of the transfer semigroup.  This avoids the $L^2$ Koopman obstruction and also avoids using a density generator on a value function.

\begin{definition}[Projection and zero-mean part]
For $f\in\widehat\Bcal_\theta^{\#}$, define
\begin{equation}
    \Pi_\theta f=\int f\,d\mu_\theta,
    \qquad
    \Pi_\theta^\perp f=f-\Pi_\theta f.
\end{equation}
\end{definition}


\begin{theorem}[Deterministic solution of the cell problem]
\label{thm:cell_problem_anisotropic}
For every $f\in\widehat\Bcal_\theta^{\#,0}$, the improper integral
\begin{equation}
    \chi_\theta[f]
    =\int_0^\infty \mathscr{U}_{\theta,t}f\,dt
\end{equation}
converges in $\widehat\Bcal_\theta^{\#}$ and is the unique zero-mean solution of
\begin{equation}
    \Acal_\theta\chi=-f,
    \qquad \int\chi\,d\mu_\theta=0 .
\end{equation}
Moreover,
\begin{equation}
    \|\chi_\theta[f]\|_{\widehat\Bcal_\theta^{\#}}
    \le C\|f\|_{\widehat\Bcal_\theta^{\#}} .
\end{equation}
For every admissible density $\rho$, the scalar
$\langle\chi_\theta[f],\rho\rangle$ is defined by anisotropic duality and is the
quantity used in perturbed viscosity tests.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The dual flow gap from \cref{thm:flow_spectral_gap} gives
\begin{equation}
    \|\mathscr{U}_{\theta,t}f\|_{\widehat\Bcal_\theta^{\#}}
    \le Ce^{-\gamma t}\|f\|_{\widehat\Bcal_\theta^{\#}}
\end{equation}
for zero-mean $f$, so the integral converges in the observable anisotropic
space.  For $T<\infty$ the generator identity on the domain gives
\begin{equation}
    \Acal_\theta\left(\int_0^T\mathscr{U}_{\theta,t}f\,dt\right)
    =\mathscr{U}_{\theta,T}f-f .
\end{equation}
The convergence of the integral and of $\mathscr{U}_{\theta,T}f$ to zero on the
zero-mean quotient allow $T\to\infty$, yielding $\Acal_\theta\chi=-f$ in
$\widehat\Bcal_\theta^{\#}$.  If $\Acal_\theta\chi=0$ and
$\int\chi\,d\mu_\theta=0$, then $\mathscr{U}_{\theta,t}\chi=\chi$ for all $t$;
the same dual gap forces $\chi=0$.  The norm estimate is the integrated
exponential estimate.  Since $\chi$ is an element of the restricted observable
dual space, every occurrence of $\chi$ in the HJB proof is a continuous scalar
pairing against a density, not a pointwise evaluation at a singular
microscopic state.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[No $L^2$ inversion]
The formula above is not an $L^2$ Bochner integral for the conservative Koopman group.  It is the observable side of the anisotropic transfer resolvent.  Hyperbolicity is visible only after the stable and unstable directions are measured anisotropically.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Parameter dependence}

The effective Hamiltonian depends on $\theta$ through the table, invariant measure, and reduced resolvent.  This dependence is derived from the same branchwise geometry.


\begin{theorem}[Smooth deterministic response]
\label{thm:parameter_response}
Let $f_\theta$ be a $C^{r}$ family of finite-response observables that is
$C^r$ on homogeneous components and has uniformly controlled singular behavior
compatible with the homogeneity strips.  Then the maps
\begin{equation}
    \theta\mapsto \int f_\theta\,d\mu_\theta,
    \qquad
    \theta\mapsto \chi_\theta[\Pi_\theta^\perp f_\theta]
\end{equation}
are $C^{r-3}$ as maps into the corresponding weak observable anisotropic
spaces.  Their derivatives are obtained by differentiating the spectral
projection and the reduced observable resolvent
\begin{equation}
    (-\Acal_\theta|_{\ker\Pi_\theta})^{-1} .
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Identify nearby collision sections by the normal-coordinate charts of the
moving scatterer boundaries.  On every homogeneous branch, the map, roof,
Jacobian, and observable are $C^{r}$ in $\theta$.  Differentiating the transfer
operator produces two kinds of terms.  The interior terms are ordinary
branchwise derivatives of inverse branches and Jacobians.  The boundary terms
come from motion of singular curves and strip boundaries.  For a stable test
curve $W$, a boundary term is an integral over $W\cap S_{\alpha,\theta}$ with
coefficient proportional to the normal velocity of the moving singular curve.
The derived finite-word trace ledger \cref{lem:impact_trace_ledger_full,lem:derived_trace_constant_no_cycle} makes this
coefficient uniformly bounded in the rescaled chart; the strip-entry closure
makes the sum of all high-strip boundary terms finite.  Thus, for
$k\le r-3$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:operator_derivative_bound_response}
    \partial_\theta^k\mathscr{L}_\theta:
    \Bcal_\theta\longrightarrow \Bcal_{\theta,w}
\end{equation}
is a bounded operator, uniformly in $\theta$.

The Lasota--Yorke constants, the compact embedding, and the peripheral spectral
separation are uniform in $\theta$.  Choose a contour $\Gamma_1$ enclosing the
simple eigenvalue one and a contour $\Gamma_0$ enclosing the zero-mean spectral
complement, both independent of $\theta$.  The spectral projection and reduced
resolvent are
\begin{equation}
    \Pi_\theta=\frac1{2\pi i}\int_{\Gamma_1}(z-\mathscr{L}_\theta)^{-1}\,dz,
    \qquad
    R_\theta=\frac1{2\pi i}\int_{\Gamma_0}\frac1{1-z}(z-\mathscr{L}_\theta)^{-1}\,dz .
\end{equation}
The derivative bound \eqref{eq:operator_derivative_bound_response} and the
resolvent identity differentiate these contour integrals in the strong-to-weak
operator topology.  Iterating the identity gives $C^{r-3}$ regularity.  Passing
to the suspension generator is the same argument applied to the weighted
Laplace family $\mathscr{L}_{\theta,s}$ and then inverting the Laplace transform;
the Dolgopyat bounds are uniform in $\theta$.  Dualizing gives the response of
observables.  On the moving zero-mean bundle the first derivative is
\begin{equation}
    \nabla_\theta(-\Acal_\theta)^{-1}
    =(-\Acal_\theta)^{-1}(\nabla_\theta\Acal_\theta)(-\Acal_\theta)^{-1}
      -(-\Acal_\theta)^{-1}(\nabla_\theta\Pi_\theta),
\end{equation}
where the last term is the covariant correction keeping the input centered.
Higher derivatives are finite sums of the same branchwise differentiated
operators and are controlled by the strip estimate scheme.  This proves the stated
smooth deterministic response without assuming a response theorem as an external hypothesis.
\end{proof}





\begin{lemma}[Explicit moving-boundary operator]
\label{lem:explicit_moving_boundary_operator}
Let \(D_h(\theta)\) be a homogeneous branch domain and let
\(\Gamma_s\subset\partial D_h(\theta_s)\) be a moving singular curve with normal
velocity \(V_\Gamma\) in the blown-up strip chart.  For a stable test leaf
\(W\), a test function \(\psi\), and a strong density \(\rho\), the derivative
of the branch characteristic function is the weak functional
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:explicit_boundary_operator_pairing}
    \big\langle \mathcal B_\Gamma\rho,\psi\big\rangle_W
    =\sum_{y\in W\cap\Gamma_0}
      (V_\Gamma\cdot n_\Gamma)(y)\,
      \rho_W(y)\,
      [\psi J_h]_\Gamma(y),
\end{equation}
where \([\psi J_h]_\Gamma\) is the jump of the pulled-back test-Jacobian factor
across the two adjacent homogeneous branches.  In a strip \(\mathbb H_k\), the
coefficient in \eqref{eq:explicit_boundary_operator_pairing} grows at most like
\(k^{a_1}\), while the total stable mass of descendants entering that strip is
summable.  Consequently
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:explicit_boundary_operator_sum}
    \sum_{h}\sum_{\Gamma\subset\partial D_h}
    \|\mathcal B_\Gamma\rho\|_{\Bcal^w}\le C\|\rho\|_{\Bcal}.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Write the branch contribution in fixed coordinates as
\(1_{D_h(\theta_s)}J_{h_s}^{-1}\rho\circ h_s\).  Differentiating the indicator
of a moving domain gives
\(-V_\Gamma\cdot n_\Gamma\,\delta_{\Gamma_0}\).  Pairing with a stable test leaf
reduces this distribution to the finite sum over intersections
\(W\cap\Gamma_0\), which is \eqref{eq:explicit_boundary_operator_pairing}.  The
finite-word impact equation gives polynomial bounds for \(V_\Gamma\) in the
blown-up coordinate; transversality to stable leaves bounds the intersection
number in each homogeneous chart.  The high-strip descendants have summable
stable mass by the terminal-child estimate of the growth lemma.  Summing central
charts and then the high-strip tail proves \eqref{eq:explicit_boundary_operator_sum}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Moving-singularity response formula in the Banach bundle]
\label{thm:moving_singularity_response_formula}
Assume \textup{(A1)--(A3)}.  Let $\theta_s$ be a $C^{r-4}$ curve of billiard tables generated by the
mechanical port, and let $f_s$ be a $C^1$ observable section of the observable
Banach bundle.  Then the invariant pairing
\begin{equation}
    I(s)=\langle f_s,\rho_{\theta_s}\rangle_{\theta_s}
\end{equation}
Every inverse and product in this formula is interpreted on the intermediate
spaces and with the derivative-loss bounds stipulated in \textup{(A3)}.  The
formula does not follow from a bare strong-to-weak derivative.
is differentiable, and at $s=0$
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:moving_singularity_response_formula}
    I'(0)=
    \big\langle \nabla_s f_s|_{0},\rho_{\theta_0}\big\rangle
    +\big\langle
       (I-\Ltransfer_{\theta_0})^{-1}\Piperp_{\theta_0}
       \big(\nabla_s\Ltransfer_{\theta_s}|_{0}\rho_{\theta_0}\big),
       f_0\big\rangle .
\end{equation}
The derivative of the transfer operator is a strong-to-weak operator and has the
branch formula
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:transfer_derivative_branch_boundary}
    \nabla_s\Ltransfer_{\theta_s}|_{0}
    =\sum_{h\in\Hstrip}\Ltransfer_{\theta_0,h}
       \big(\mathcal D_h^{\rm int}+\mathcal D_h^{\rm Jac}\big)
      +\sum_{\Gamma\subset\Ssing}\mathcal B_{\Gamma}.
\end{equation}
Here $\mathcal D_h^{\rm int}$ differentiates the inverse branch in the interior,
$\mathcal D_h^{\rm Jac}$ differentiates the Jacobian and roof factors, and
$\mathcal B_\Gamma$ is the boundary functional coming from the motion of a
singular curve.  The boundary series is absolutely bounded in the weak
anisotropic norm:
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:boundary_response_bound}
    \sum_{\Gamma\subset\Ssing}
    \|\mathcal B_\Gamma\rho\|_{\widehat\Bcal^{w}_{\theta_0}}
    \le C\|\rho\|_{\widehat\Bcal_{\theta_0}} .
\end{equation}
Thus invariant averages, correctors, and Green--Kubo tensors are differentiated
by deterministic moving-boundary calculus rather than by an external response
hypothesis.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Trivialize the moving fibers by \cref{def:anisotropic_banach_bundle} and write
all operators on the fixed fiber over $\theta_0$.  On a homogeneous inverse
branch $h_s$ the transfer operator has the form
\begin{equation}
    \Ltransfer_{\theta_s,h_s}\rho
     = J_{h_s}^{-1}\,\rho\circ h_s
\end{equation}
defining a density on the image component.  Differentiating in $s$ gives two
interior terms: the derivative of $h_s$ applied to the branchwise derivative of
$\rho$, and the derivative of $\log J_{h_s}$.  These are exactly
$\mathcal D_h^{\rm int}$ and $\mathcal D_h^{\rm Jac}$ in
\eqref{eq:transfer_derivative_branch_boundary}.  The differentiability of the
flight time, reflection law, and Jacobian on the branch follows from the
implicit impact equation used in \cref{prop:piecewise_smoothness}.

The domain of a branch also moves.  If a boundary curve $\Gamma_s$ moves with
normal velocity $V_\Gamma$, differentiating the characteristic function of the
branch gives the distribution
\begin{equation}
    (V_\Gamma\cdot n_\Gamma)\,\rho\,\delta_{\Gamma_0},
\end{equation}
paired against the jump of the test observable across the branch boundary.  In
ordinary $C^1$ norms this is singular; in the billiard anisotropic norm it is a
weak stable-leaf functional.  Transversality of singular curves to stable and
unstable cones bounds the crossing number of an admissible leaf, and
\cref{lem:strip_entry_terminal_closure} gives a summable high-strip tail.  This
proves \eqref{eq:boundary_response_bound} and shows that
$\nabla_s\Ltransfer_{\theta_s}|_{0}$ maps strong densities to weak densities.

The spectral projector is
\begin{equation}
    \Pi_{\theta_s}=\frac1{2\pi i}\int_{|\zeta-1|=r}
       (\zeta-\Ltransfer_{\theta_s})^{-1}\,d\zeta,
\end{equation}
where $r$ is smaller than the uniform gap from
\cref{thm:map_gap_no_hidden_hypothesis}.  The strong-to-weak differentiability
of $\Ltransfer_{\theta_s}$ and the uniform resolvent bound justify
differentiating this contour integral.  Since
$\rho_{\theta_s}=\Pi_{\theta_s}1$ after mass normalization, differentiating
$\Ltransfer_{\theta_s}\rho_{\theta_s}=\rho_{\theta_s}$ and imposing zero mass on
$\nabla_s\rho_{\theta_s}$ gives
\begin{equation}
    (I-\Ltransfer_{\theta_0})\nabla_s\rho_{\theta_s}|_0
    =\Piperp_{\theta_0}
      \big(\nabla_s\Ltransfer_{\theta_s}|_0\rho_{\theta_0}\big).
\end{equation}
The reduced resolvent from \cref{thm:map_gap_no_hidden_hypothesis} solves this
identity.  Pairing the result with $f_0$ and adding the trivialized derivative
of $f_s$ gives \eqref{eq:moving_singularity_response_formula}.  The same formula
applied to the resolvent representation of the corrector differentiates all
cell-problem coefficients.  No stochastic response principle is used.
\end{proof}


\begin{proposition}[Uniform multi-response bounds for moving singularities]
\label{prop:uniform_multiresponse_bounds}
For every integer $1\le \ell\le r-5$, the $\ell$th parameter derivative of the
collision-map transfer operator, the weighted suspension transfer family, the
spectral projector, and the reduced observable resolvent exists in the
strong-to-weak anisotropic topology.  More precisely, in every Banach-bundle
chart and uniformly over the compact parameter manifold,
\begin{align}
    \|\nabla_\theta^\ell \Ltransfer_\theta h\|_{\Bcal^w_\theta}
        &\le C_\ell\|h\|_{\Bcal_\theta},\label{eq:multiresponse_L_bound}\\
    \|\nabla_\theta^\ell \Pi_\theta h\|_{\Bcal^w_\theta}
        &\le C_\ell\|h\|_{\Bcal_\theta},\label{eq:multiresponse_Pi_bound}\\
    \|\nabla_\theta^\ell (-\Acal_\theta|_{\ker\Pi_\theta})^{-1}f\|_{\widehat\Bcal^{\#,w}_\theta}
        &\le C_\ell\|f\|_{\widehat\Bcal^{\#}_\theta}.
        \label{eq:multiresponse_resolvent_bound}
\end{align}
The derivatives are finite sums of branch-interior terms, Jacobian/roof terms,
and moving-boundary distributions on singular hypersurfaces.  Each boundary
series is absolutely summable in the weak anisotropic norm.  The constants
$C_\ell$ are determined only by the geometric constants in
\cref{ass:fundamental_axioms_unified}, the finite-response bounds, and the
chosen homogeneity exponents.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
On a homogeneous strip $\mathbb H_k$, the implicit impact equation gives
$\ell$th derivatives of the flight time, inverse branch, reflection map, and
Jacobian bounded by $C_\ell k^{a_\ell}$ for an exponent $a_\ell$ depending only
on $\ell$ and the strip convention.  Differentiating a branch contribution
therefore gives terms of the form
\begin{equation}
    \Ltransfer_{\theta,h}\big(A_{h,\ell}\,D_{\rm br}^{j}h\big),
    \qquad j\le \ell,
\end{equation}
with $A_{h,\ell}$ satisfying the same polynomial strip bound.  The stable norm
pays one inverse expansion factor, the unstable seminorm pays the holonomy
Jacobian difference, and the weak norm pays only the stable-leaf trace.  The
one-step expansion and terminal-child estimate imply
\begin{equation}
    \sum_{k\ge k_0} k^{a_\ell}\,\Mass(\hbox{children entering }\mathbb H_k)
       \le C_\ell k_0^{-1},
\end{equation}
for the chosen homogeneity exponent after increasing the initial strip cutoff.
Thus all interior and Jacobian/roof derivative terms are strong-to-weak
bounded.

When a branch boundary moves, differentiating its characteristic function
produces a trace functional on the corresponding singular hypersurface.  The
derived trace ledger \cref{lem:impact_trace_ledger_full,lem:derived_trace_constant_no_cycle} bounds the
number of intersections with an admissible stable leaf in the rescaled chart,
and the same high-strip summability gives the displayed tail estimate.  Repeated
differentiation produces products of normal velocities, curvature derivatives,
and lower-order boundary traces; each is controlled by the same polynomial
strip bound and is therefore absolutely summable in the weak norm.  This proves
\eqref{eq:multiresponse_L_bound}; the weighted suspension family is identical
with the additional multiplier $e^{-s\tau_\theta}$, whose derivatives obey the
same roof estimates.

The spectral projector and the reduced map resolvent are contour integrals of
$(z-\Ltransfer_\theta)^{-1}$ over contours fixed by the uniform spectral gap.
The differentiated resolvent identity expresses each derivative as a finite sum
of products of uniformly bounded resolvents and the operator derivatives just
estimated.  This proves \eqref{eq:multiresponse_Pi_bound}.  For the suspension
observable generator, apply the same argument to the Laplace family and use the
uniform Dolgopyat strip to shift the inverse Laplace contour.  Dualizing yields
\eqref{eq:multiresponse_resolvent_bound}.  No response estimate is imported as
an external assumption; it is the differentiated form of the branchwise
singular-billiard transfer calculus.
\end{proof}


\begin{definition}[Fixed strong--weak response domain]
\label{def:fixed_response_domain}
Choose a reference table \(\theta_0\) and a compact parameter ball \(U\).  The
response domain \(\mathfrak D_U\subset\Bcal_{\theta_0}\) is the completion of
finite linear combinations of homogeneous branch densities whose pullbacks to
all \(\theta\in U\) have uniformly bounded strong anisotropic norm and whose
first \(r-5\) moving-boundary traces have uniformly summable strip tails.  The
weak response codomain \(\mathfrak D_U^w\) is defined in the same trivialization
by stable-leaf testing with one fewer derivative.  The inclusions
\begin{equation}
    \mathfrak D_U\hookrightarrow \Bcal_{\theta_0},
    \qquad
    \Bcal_{\theta_0}^w\hookrightarrow\mathfrak D_U^w
\end{equation}
are continuous and all transfer, weighted transfer, projector, and reduced
resolvent derivatives below are maps \(\mathfrak D_U\to\mathfrak D_U^w\).
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Fa\`a di Bruno response expansion with singular traces]
\label{prop:faa_di_bruno_singular_response}
For \(1\le \ell\le r-5\), the derivative
\(\partial_\theta^\ell \Ltransfer_\theta\) in the fixed response domain is a
finite sum of terms of the form
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:faa_di_bruno_branch_trace_terms}
    \sum_{h\in\Hstrip}
        \Ltransfer_{\theta,h}
        \Big(A_{h,\mathbf a}\,D_{\rm br}^{j_1}h\cdots D_{\rm br}^{j_m}h\Big)
    +
    \sum_{\Gamma\subset\Ssing}
        B_{\Gamma,\mathbf a}\,\partial_\tau^q\delta_\Gamma,
\end{equation}
where \(\sum j_m+q\le\ell\).  The coefficients \(A_{h,\mathbf a}\) and
\(B_{\Gamma,\mathbf a}\) obey polynomial strip bounds, and the complete series
in \eqref{eq:faa_di_bruno_branch_trace_terms} is absolutely bounded from
\(\mathfrak D_U\) to \(\mathfrak D_U^w\).  Consequently every differentiated
contour integral defining \(\Pi_\theta\), the map reduced resolvent, and the
suspension reduced generator resolvent is a genuine bounded strong--weak
operator, not a formal derivative.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Differentiate a branch contribution written in fixed coordinates as
\(J_h(\theta)^{-1}\rho(h_\theta(\cdot))1_{D_h(\theta)}\).  The ordinary
Fa\`a di Bruno formula gives the interior part: derivatives may hit the inverse
branch, the Jacobian, the roof weight, or the observable.  The impact-equation
bounds of \cref{lem:impact_trace_ledger_full} give polynomial strip estimates
for every factor.  Derivatives of the characteristic function
\(1_{D_h(\theta)}\) give distributions supported on moving branch boundaries;
second and higher derivatives give tangential derivatives of such distributions
and products of normal velocities.  Because the singular curves cross stable
tests transversally in the rescaled coordinates, each trace is a weak
anisotropic functional.  The polynomial strip losses are summable by the growth
lemma after the homogeneity exponent is fixed large enough.

The differentiated resolvent identity is then a finite sum of products
\begin{equation}
    (z-\Ltransfer_\theta)^{-1}
    (\partial_\theta^{\ell_1}\Ltransfer_\theta)
    (z-\Ltransfer_\theta)^{-1}
    \cdots
    (\partial_\theta^{\ell_m}\Ltransfer_\theta)
    (z-\Ltransfer_\theta)^{-1},
\end{equation}
with \(\ell_1+\cdots+\ell_m=\ell\).  Uniform spectral separation fixes the
contours and bounds the resolvents from weak back to strong after inserting the
Lasota--Yorke recovery step.  The suspension case is the same computation for
\(\Ltransfer_{\theta,s}\), with the Dolgopyat strip providing the uniform
vertical-frequency bound before inverse Laplace inversion.  This proves the
proposition.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Finite regularity-loss budget for the response calculus]
\label{thm:response_regularity_loss_budget}
Fix a compact finite-response parameter ball \(U\) and the response domain
\(\mathfrak D_U\) of \cref{def:fixed_response_domain}.  For \(j\ge0\), let
\(\mathfrak D_U^{(j)}\) denote the same domain with \(j\) additional controlled
branch derivatives and \(j\) additional moving-boundary traces in the blown-up
homogeneity-strip charts; let \(\mathfrak D_U^{w,(j)}\) be the corresponding
weak stable-leaf testing space.  For every \(0\le\ell\le r-8\), the response
operators used later satisfy the quantitative loss rules
\begin{align}
    \partial_\theta^\ell\Ltransfer_\theta
       &: \mathfrak D_U^{(\ell+2)}\longrightarrow
          \mathfrak D_U^{w,(0)},\label{eq:response_budget_transfer}\\
    \partial_\theta^\ell\Pi_\theta,\ 
    \partial_\theta^\ell(I-\Ltransfer_\theta)^{-1}\Piperp_\theta
       &: \mathfrak D_U^{(\ell+3)}\longrightarrow
          \mathfrak D_U^{w,(0)},\label{eq:response_budget_projector}\\
    \partial_\theta^\ell(-\Acal_\theta|_{\ker\Pi_\theta})^{-1}
       &: \widehat{\mathfrak D}_U^{\# ,(\ell+4),0}
          \longrightarrow
          \widehat{\mathfrak D}_U^{\#,w,(0)} .
          \label{eq:response_budget_flow_resolvent}
\end{align}
Consequently the first corrector, the second corrector, the Green--Kubo tensor,
the effective Hamiltonian, and the complete covariant work response used in the
perturbed-test proof have all \((x,p)\) derivatives required by
\cref{lem:deterministic_residual_identity,prop:subsuper_residuals_full_branch,thm:main_homogenization}
inside the weak observable bundle.  The maximal loss along any path entering the
main theorem is eight primitive derivatives: two for the branch/trace
linearization, one for centering, one for the suspension resolvent, and four for
the \(C^4\) contact-jet Taylor expansion.  Since the primitive scatterer family is
\(C^{r+3}\) with \(r\ge12\), the response and cell calculations never exhaust the
ordinary geometric regularity budget.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The transfer-operator derivative is the branch-and-trace expansion of
\cref{prop:faa_di_bruno_singular_response}.  Each parameter derivative can hit an
inverse branch, a Jacobian, a roof, or a moving branch indicator.  The interior
terms require one branch derivative of the density, while a moving-indicator
derivative is paired with a stable test leaf and therefore lands in the weak
space.  The two extra derivatives in
\eqref{eq:response_budget_transfer} are the fixed overhead for the blown-up
grazing coordinate and the stable-holonomy comparison; the terminal-child
summability absorbs the resulting polynomial strip factors.

For the projector and map resolvent, differentiate the contour formula.  Every
differentiated word alternates uniformly bounded resolvents with one
\(\partial_\theta^q\Ltransfer_\theta\).  The contour is fixed by the spectral gap,
so no additional singular set is created; the single extra derivative in
\eqref{eq:response_budget_projector} is the centering derivative
\(\partial_\theta\Pi_\theta\), which keeps the input in the zero-mean bundle.  For
the suspension generator, the Laplace family adds the roof multiplier and the
inverse-Laplace contour.  The roof derivative is controlled by the same finite
horizon and strip bounds, and the Dolgopyat strip gives the uniform vertical
resolvent bound; this accounts for the fourth overhead derivative in
\eqref{eq:response_budget_flow_resolvent}.

The coefficients in the main theorem are finite algebraic combinations of
finite-response read-outs, invariant pairings, reduced-resolvent correctors, and
correlation integrals.  Products are continuous in the response domain by the
anisotropic multiplication bounds, and correlation integrals are dominated by the
flow spectral gap.  The \(C^4\) viscosity test contributes at most four
macroscopic derivatives, each entering through the already bounded horizontal
chain rule.  Hence the full-gradient residual uses no derivative outside the
budget displayed above.  This is a bookkeeping theorem about deterministic
branch calculus and trace summability, not an additional response assumption.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Closed response chain rule for moving billiard coefficients]
\label{thm:closed_response_chain_rule_strengthened}
Let $(x,p)\mapsto\theta=\Theta(x,p)$ be the table parameter generated by the
finite-response port, and let $F(x,p,\cdot)$ be any coefficient, corrector, or
finite product of such objects used in the effective HJB derivation.  On each
compact jet window the covariant derivative has the exact decomposition
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:closed_response_chain_rule_strengthened}
    D_{x,p}^{\Hor}F
    =D^{\rm br}_{x,p}F
      +(D_\theta^{\rm int}F)D_{x,p}\Theta
      +(D_\theta^{\rm bd}F)D_{x,p}\Theta
      +D^{\rm proj}_{x,p}F,
\end{equation}
where the four terms are, respectively, the branchwise derivative of the
observable formula, the interior derivative of inverse branches/roofs/Jacobians,
the moving-boundary trace derivative on singular hypersurfaces, and the
centering/projection derivative required to remain in the zero-mean bundle.  All
four terms are bounded from strong to weak anisotropic spaces.  The right hand
side is constructed before the homogenized solution is introduced and is the
only chain rule used in the full-gradient residual calculation.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Transport all objects to a fixed Banach-bundle chart.  A coefficient or
corrector is obtained by finitely many algebraic operations, invariant
projections, and reduced resolvents applied to branchwise finite-response
observables.  Differentiating a branchwise observable gives
$D^{\rm br}_{x,p}F$.  When the billiard table changes, the inverse branch,
Jacobian, roof, and reflection law change; differentiating their explicit
impact-equation formulae gives $D_\theta^{\rm int}F$.  The domains of the
branches also move; differentiating their characteristic functions gives the
trace operator $D_\theta^{\rm bd}F$.  Its boundedness is exactly the
moving-boundary estimate \eqref{eq:boundary_response_bound} and its higher-order
version \eqref{eq:faa_di_bruno_branch_trace_terms}.  Finally, centered objects
must remain centered as the invariant density moves.  Differentiating
$\Pi_\theta^\perp F$ by the contour formula for $\Pi_\theta$ gives
$D^{\rm proj}_{x,p}F$.

Each term is a previously constructed deterministic operator: branch
differentiation uses the finite-response $C^r$ bounds, interior table
differentiation uses the impact-equation derivatives, boundary differentiation
uses the trace ledger, and projection differentiation uses the spectral gap.
The composition with $D_{x,p}\Theta$ is legitimate because $\Theta$ is the
endpoint map of the compact finite-response port and has globally bounded
derivatives after saturation.  Therefore \eqref{eq:closed_response_chain_rule_strengthened}
is a closed deterministic chain rule and cannot import information from the
limiting HJB solution.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[No loss of centering under coefficient differentiation]
\label{prop:no_loss_centering_coefficient_differentiation_strengthened}
If $F(x,p,\cdot)$ is centered for every $(x,p)$, then the covariant derivative
$D_{x,p}^{\Hor}F$ decomposes as
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:centered_derivative_decomposition_strengthened}
    D_{x,p}^{\Hor}F
      =\Piperp_{\Theta(x,p)}D_{x,p}^{\Hor}F
       +c_{x,p},
    \qquad
    c_{x,p}=\int D_{x,p}^{\Hor}F\,d\mu_{\Theta(x,p)},
\end{equation}
and the zero-mean component belongs to the range of
$\Acal_{\Theta(x,p)}$ on the observable generator domain.  The scalar
$c_{x,p}$ is retained as part of the effective coefficient whenever it is not
zero.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Differentiate the identity
$\int F(x,p,z)d\mu_{\Theta(x,p)}(z)=0$ in the Banach bundle.  The derivative of
the measure is the reduced-transfer response of
\cref{thm:moving_singularity_response_formula}; the derivative of $F$ is the
closed chain rule \eqref{eq:closed_response_chain_rule_strengthened}.  Their
sum is zero, which identifies the constant component $c_{x,p}$.  The primitive
spectral gap gives the inverse of the generator on the zero-mean quotient, so
$\Piperp D_{x,p}^{\Hor}F$ is a deterministic coboundary.  The constant component
is not thrown away; in the full-gradient expansion it is precisely the part
that contributes to $H$ or to the Green--Kubo Hessian contraction.
\end{proof}


\begin{corollary}[Response regularity of all coefficients used in the HJB]
\label{cor:coefficient_response_regular_full}
The maps \((x,p)\mapsto D(x,p)\), \((x,p)\mapsto H(x,p)\), the first and
second correctors, and the complete covariant work response are \(C^{r-5}\) as
sections of the transported weak observable bundle on compact jet windows.
Their derivatives are obtained by applying
\cref{prop:faa_di_bruno_singular_response} to the explicit branch formulae and
then applying the reduced observable resolvent.  No derivative of an effective
coefficient is used before the corresponding branchwise response expression has
been constructed.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Each coefficient is a finite algebraic combination of invariant pairings,
centered observables, correctors, and correlation integrals.  Invariant
pairings are differentiated by the projector formula in
\cref{thm:moving_singularity_response_formula}.  Correctors are differentiated
by the reduced generator resolvent, whose derivatives are bounded by
\cref{prop:faa_di_bruno_singular_response}.  Correlation integrals are
absolutely convergent because of the flow spectral gap, and their parameter
derivatives are obtained by differentiating the integrand and the semigroup and
then using the same exponential bound to dominate the time integral.  The
centering constants are differentiated at the end to keep the inputs in the
zero-mean bundle.  The construction is ordered at the level of branchwise
operators, so it cannot be circular in the effective HJB coefficients.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Differentiated cell problems are deterministic coboundaries]
\label{cor:differentiated_cell_coboundaries}
For every centered observable section $g_{x,p}$ generated by the drift,
potential, or finite-response connection, the differentiated corrector
$\nabla_{x,p}\chi[g_{x,p}]$ is the unique zero-mean solution of
\begin{equation}
    \Acal_{\Theta(x,p)}\nabla_{x,p}\chi[g_{x,p}]
    =-\nabla_{x,p}g_{x,p}
      -\big(\nabla_{x,p}\Acal_{\Theta(x,p)}\big)\chi[g_{x,p}]
      +c_{x,p},
\end{equation}
where $c_{x,p}$ is the invariant average required to make the right-hand side
zero mean.  The term
$\big(\nabla_{x,p}\Acal_{\Theta(x,p)}\big)\chi[g_{x,p}]$ is the
moving-singularity derivative of \cref{thm:moving_singularity_response_formula}
and is bounded in the weak observable norm by strip summability.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Differentiate the identity
$\Acal_{\Theta(x,p)}\chi[g_{x,p}]=-g_{x,p}+\langle g_{x,p}\rangle$ in the
Banach bundle.  The derivative of the generator is the suspension version of
\eqref{eq:transfer_derivative_branch_boundary}; the zero-mean constant is fixed
by pairing with the invariant density.  The reduced generator resolvent then
solves the differentiated equation uniquely on the zero-mean subspace.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Covariant centering of differentiated generator terms]
\label{prop:covariant_centering_generator_terms}
Let $\theta(s)=\Theta(x,p+s q)$ be a finite-response cotangent variation and let
$F_s$ be a $C^1$ observable section in the moving generator domain with
$F_0=F$.  Then the covariant derivative of the invariant generator identity is
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:covariant_generator_identity}
    \int (\nabla_s\Acal_{\theta(s)})F\,d\mu_{\theta_0}
    +\int \Acal_{\theta_0}(\nabla_sF_s|_0)\,d\mu_{\theta_0}
    +\left\langle \Acal_{\theta_0}F,\nabla_s\rho_{\theta(s)}|_0\right\rangle=0 .
\end{equation}
If $F$ is a zero-mean corrector solving $\Acal_{\theta_0}F=-g$ and if the
observable $g_s$ is centered for every $s$, then
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:centered_connection_general}
    (\nabla_s\Acal_{\theta(s)})F+\nabla_s g_s|_0
      -\int\big((\nabla_s\Acal_{\theta(s)})F+\nabla_s g_s|_0\big)
        d\mu_{\theta_0}
\end{equation}
is a zero-mean observable in the weak anisotropic space.  Consequently it is in
the range of the reduced deterministic generator resolvent.  In particular, the
zero-mean projections of the $\partial_p\Theta$, $\partial_p b$,
moving-boundary, and moving-projection terms appearing in the full-gradient
perturbed-test expansion are deterministic coboundaries before the homogenization
limit is taken; their invariant projections are not discarded but are recorded in
$\Lambda_{x,p}$ and therefore in the coefficients $H$ and $D$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For every $s$, Liouville invariance gives
$\int \Acal_{\theta(s)}F_s\,d\mu_{\theta(s)}=0$.  Pull the three factors to the
fixed Banach fiber at $s=0$ and differentiate.  The derivative of the operator
is the strong-to-weak moving-singularity derivative of
\cref{thm:moving_singularity_response_formula}; the derivative of the measure is
obtained by the reduced transfer resolvent; and the derivative of the section is
ordinary covariant differentiation.  This gives
\eqref{eq:covariant_generator_identity}.  If
$\Acal_{\theta(s)}F_s=-g_s+\langle g_s\rangle_{\theta(s)}$ and
$\langle g_s\rangle_{\theta(s)}=0$ for all $s$, differentiating the centering
identity cancels the last term in \eqref{eq:covariant_generator_identity}
against the derivative of $g_s$.  The centered expression
\eqref{eq:centered_connection_general} is therefore orthogonal to the invariant
density.  The primitive spectral resolvent theorem then solves a cell equation
for it.  No cancellation is asserted by notation alone: it is the differentiated
invariance identity in the moving billiard bundle.
\end{proof}

\section{Deterministic scale analysis and homogenization}
\label{sec:scale_analysis}

The scale analysis is purely analytic.  It uses the observable generator $\Acal_\theta$, the deterministic transfer-resolvent estimates, deterministic correlation identities, and viscosity stability.  Only the deterministic microscopic model and deterministic analytic estimates appear in this part.

\subsection{Effective coefficients}

Let $b(x,p,z)$ be the microscopic drift observable, smooth in $(x,p)$ and
piecewise $C^r$ in $z$, and let
\begin{equation}
    \theta=\Theta(x,p)
\end{equation}
with $\Theta$ of class $C^{r-3}$.  The deterministic comoving-frame centering is
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:drift_centering_effective}
    \int b(x,p,z)\,d\mu_{\Theta(x,p)}(z)=0 .
\end{equation}
For each coordinate vector $e_i$, define the observable corrector
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:first_cell_coordinate_correctors}
    \chi_i(x,p,\cdot)
    =\chi_{\Theta(x,p)}[b_i(x,p,\cdot)],
    \qquad
    \Acal_{\Theta(x,p)}\chi_i=-b_i(x,p,\cdot),
    \qquad \int\chi_i\,d\mu_\theta=0,
\end{equation}
and set
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:first_cell_p_corrector}
    \chi_p(x,p,z)=\sum_{i=1}^m p_i\chi_i(x,p,z).
\end{equation}
The derivative $\nabla_x^{\mathrm{fr}}$ is the horizontal derivative in the
moving billiard bundle with $p$ held fixed:
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:horizontal_derivative_definition}
    \partial_{x_j}^{\mathrm{fr}}\chi_p(x,p,z)
    =\left.\frac{d}{ds}\right|_{s=0}\chi_p(x+s e_j,p,z),
    \qquad
    Q_x\chi_p=(\partial_{x_1}^{\mathrm{fr}}\chi_p,\ldots,
    \partial_{x_m}^{\mathrm{fr}}\chi_p).
\end{equation}
This convention is not shorthand for differentiating the composite
$x\mapsto\chi_p(x,\nabla\varphi(x),z)$.  In the viscosity proof the coefficient
parameter is frozen at the actual contact jet, while the complete slow gradient
of the perturbed test is still inserted in the prelimit equation.

\begin{definition}[Complete covariant work response]
\label{def:complete_covariant_work_response}
Fix $(x,p)$, put $\theta=\Theta(x,p)$, and let $q=(q_1,\ldots,q_m)$ be a
branchwise finite-response observable vector.  The raw coefficient-response part
is
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:finite_response_connection_observable}
    \mathcal R_{x,p}(q)
    :=(\nabla_\theta\Acal_\theta)[D_p\Theta(x,p)q] \chi_p
      +(\nabla_p b(x,p,\cdot)[q])\cdot p .
\end{equation}
The complete first-variation work observable is
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:complete_work_response_observable}
    \mathfrak W_{x,p}(q)
       :=b(x,p,\cdot)\cdot q+\mathcal R_{x,p}(q),
\end{equation}
and its invariant projection is the deterministic linear functional
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:complete_work_response_projection}
    \Lambda_{x,p}(q)
       :=\int \mathfrak W_{x,p}(q)\,d\mu_\theta .
\end{equation}
The centered complete response is
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:finite_response_connection_centered}
    \mathfrak W^{\circ}_{x,p}(q)
       :=\mathfrak W_{x,p}(q)-\Lambda_{x,p}(q),
    \qquad
    \int \mathfrak W^{\circ}_{x,p}(q)\,d\mu_\theta=0 .
\end{equation}
Thus no term produced by a $z$-dependent full gradient is discarded: its
invariant component is recorded by $\Lambda_{x,p}$ and its zero-mean component is
sent to the deterministic second cell equation.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Hessian-sector reciprocity of the finite-response connection]
\label{lem:hessian_sector_reciprocity}
For $q_{ij}=\chi_i e_j$ define
\begin{equation}
    \Gamma_{ij}(x,p)
    =\int \mathcal R_{x,p}(q_{ij})\,d\mu_{\Theta(x,p)} .
\end{equation}
The single-action finite-response port implies
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:connection_skewness_hessian_sector}
    \Gamma_{ij}(x,p)+\Gamma_{ji}(x,p)=0 .
\end{equation}
Consequently, for every symmetric matrix $X$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:hessian_projection_GK_equivalence}
    \sum_{i,j}X_{ij}\Lambda_{x,p}(\chi_i e_j)
    =\frac12\sum_{i,j}X_{ij}\int
       \bigl(b_j\chi_i+b_i\chi_j\bigr)\,d\mu_{\Theta(x,p)} .
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The proof is the covariant mixed-variation identity of the microscopic action.
For constant cotangent directions $e_i,e_j$, differentiate the first cell
identity
\begin{equation}
    \Acal_{\Theta(x,p)}\chi_p+b(x,p,\cdot)\cdot p=0
\end{equation}
in the moving Banach bundle.  The derivative in the $e_j$ direction is
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:constant_direction_cell_variation}
    (\nabla_\theta\Acal_\theta)[D_p\Theta e_j]\chi_p
    +\Acal_\theta(\nabla_{p_j}^{\rm cov}\chi_p)
    +(\nabla_{p_j}b)\cdot p+b_j=0 .
\end{equation}
Multiply \eqref{eq:constant_direction_cell_variation} by $\chi_i$, pair with
$\mu_\theta$, and repeat with $i$ and $j$ interchanged.  The terms containing
$\Acal_\theta(\nabla_{p_j}^{\rm cov}\chi_p)$ are integrated by the deterministic
Green identity; the physical collision faces cancel by specular reflection and
the grazing and branch-change traces are summable by the moving-singularity
bounds.  The sum of the two resulting identities is the symmetric second
variation, with respect to $p_i$ and $p_j$, of the same finite-response action
functional in \eqref{eq:finite_response_generating_action}.  Since the port is
single-action, mixed partials commute on every regular branch, and the remaining
Liouville-exact endpoint gauges have zero invariant average by
\cref{prop:single_action_reciprocity_port}.  Hence the symmetric part of
$\Gamma$ vanishes, giving \eqref{eq:connection_skewness_hessian_sector}.

Now
\begin{equation}
    \Lambda_{x,p}(\chi_i e_j)
    =\int b_j\chi_i\,d\mu_\theta+\Gamma_{ij} .
\end{equation}
Contracting with a symmetric matrix and using
\eqref{eq:connection_skewness_hessian_sector} removes the skew connection part
and gives \eqref{eq:hessian_projection_GK_equivalence}.  The argument is an
identity of deterministic branch actions, Green formulas, and weak trace
summations; no probabilistic approximation is used.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Covariant Green--Kubo tensor from deterministic correlations]
\label{def:GK_tensor}
The effective diffusivity is the symmetric matrix
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:covariant_GK_tensor_definition}
    D_{ij}(x,p)
    =\frac12\bigl(\Lambda_{x,p}(\chi_i e_j)
                 +\Lambda_{x,p}(\chi_j e_i)\bigr),
    \qquad \theta=\Theta(x,p).
\end{equation}
By \cref{lem:hessian_sector_reciprocity}, this is equivalently the deterministic
Green--Kubo tensor of the centered microscopic velocity field,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:GK_tensor_correlation_formula}
    D_{ij}(x,p)=\frac12\int_0^\infty\int
    \left(b_i(x,p,z)b_j(x,p,\Phi_{\theta}^{t}z)
          +b_j(x,p,z)b_i(x,p,\Phi_{\theta}^{t}z)\right)
    d\mu_\theta(z)\,dt,
\end{equation}
or, using $\chi_j=\int_0^\infty\mathscr{U}_{\theta,t}b_j\,dt$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:GK_tensor_corrector_formula}
    D_{ij}(x,p)= \frac12\int
    \left(b_i\chi_j+b_j\chi_i\right)d\mu_\theta .
\end{equation}
The first formula \eqref{eq:covariant_GK_tensor_definition} is the one that
appears in the full-gradient expansion; the correlation formulas are its
reciprocity-reduced deterministic form.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Positivity]
\label{prop:D_positive}
For every $(x,p)$, the matrix $D(x,p)$ is symmetric and positive semidefinite.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Symmetry is built into \eqref{eq:covariant_GK_tensor_definition}.  For
$a\in\R^m$, set $f_a=a\cdot b(x,p,\cdot)$ and
$\theta=\Theta(x,p)$.  By \cref{lem:hessian_sector_reciprocity}, the covariant
Hessian-sector definition gives the same quadratic form as the Green--Kubo
correlation:
\begin{equation}
    a^TD(x,p)a=\int_0^\infty\int f_a(z)f_a(\Phi_\theta^t z)\,d\mu_\theta(z)\,dt .
\end{equation}
For $T>0$, invariance of $\mu_\theta$ gives the exact finite-time identity
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:finite_time_square_positivity}
    \frac1{2T}\int\left(\int_0^T f_a(\Phi_\theta^t z)\,dt\right)^2d\mu_\theta(z)
    =\int_0^T\left(1-\frac{s}{T}\right)
      \int f_a(z)f_a(\Phi_\theta^s z)\,d\mu_\theta(z)\,ds .
\end{equation}
The right-hand side converges to the Green--Kubo integral by the deterministic
exponential correlation bound from \cref{thm:flow_spectral_gap}.  The left-hand
side is a square divided by $2T$, hence non-negative.  This is a deterministic
identity for time integrals along the billiard flow, proved by deterministic
correlation bounds and independent of probabilistic representation input.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Effective potential, normalization, and Hamiltonian]
\label{def:effective_hamiltonian_normalized}
Let $V(x,p,z)$ be the microscopic potential.  The raw deterministic potential is
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:H_raw_complete_covariant}
    H_{\mathrm{raw}}(x,p)
    =\int V(x,p,z)\,d\mu_{\Theta(x,p)}(z)
     +\Lambda_{x,p}\bigl(Q_x\chi_p\bigr),
\end{equation}
where the second term is the full covariant horizontal work response from
\cref{def:complete_covariant_work_response}.  Written out, it contains both the
physical work $b\cdot Q_x\chi_p$ and the moving-table/centered-drift connection
terms generated by $\partial_p\Theta$ and $\partial_p b$.  The expectation gauge
is the deterministic normalization
\begin{equation}
    H(x,p)=H_{\mathrm{raw}}(x,p)-H_{\mathrm{raw}}(x,0),
    \qquad H(x,0)=0 .
\end{equation}
Equivalently, the microscopic running potential in the prelimit equation is
replaced by
\begin{equation}
    V^\circ(x,p,z)=V(x,p,z)-H_{\mathrm{raw}}(x,0).
\end{equation}
This subtraction is a change of zero energy at each macroscopic point; it does
not alter the deterministic corrector mechanism.  The $\theta$-Hamiltonian is
\begin{equation}
    H_\theta(x,p,X)=\Tr\big(D(x,p)X\big)+H(x,p).
\end{equation}
\end{definition}


\begin{lemma}[Contact-jet-frozen corrector calculus]
\label{lem:jet_frozen_corrector_calculus}
Let $\varphi\in C^3$ and let $(t_\varepsilon,x_\varepsilon)$ be a contact point
in the viscosity--duality argument for the $\varepsilon$-problem.  Set
\begin{equation}
    p_\varepsilon=D_x\varphi(t_\varepsilon,x_\varepsilon),
    \qquad
    X_\varepsilon=D_x^2\varphi(t_\varepsilon,x_\varepsilon),
    \qquad
    \theta_\varepsilon=\Theta(x_\varepsilon,p_\varepsilon).
\end{equation}
The admissible first perturbed test is frozen at this actual contact jet in the
coefficient arguments but is multiplied by the current macroscopic gradient of
$\varphi$:
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:jet_frozen_test}
    \Xi_\varepsilon[\varphi](t,x,z)
      =\sum_{i=1}^m \partial_i\varphi(t,x)\,\chi_i(x,p_\varepsilon,z),
    \qquad
    \varphi^\varepsilon(t,x,z)=\varphi(t,x)+\varepsilon\Xi_\varepsilon[\varphi](t,x,z)
      +\varepsilon^2\chi^{(2)}_{\varepsilon}(x,z).
\end{equation}
At the contact point,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:contact_frozen_gradient_expansion}
    D_x\Xi_\varepsilon[\varphi](t_\varepsilon,x_\varepsilon,z)
    =D_x^{\mathrm{fr}}\chi_{p_\varepsilon}(x_\varepsilon,p_\varepsilon,z)
      +\sum_{i,j=1}^m X_{\varepsilon,ij}\,\chi_i(x_\varepsilon,p_\varepsilon,z)e_j .
\end{equation}
The order $\varepsilon^{-1}$ part cancels exactly at the contact point,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:exact_contact_cancellation}
    -b(x_\varepsilon,p_\varepsilon,z)\cdot p_\varepsilon
    -\Acal_{\theta_\varepsilon}
      \Big(\sum_i p_{\varepsilon,i}\chi_i(x_\varepsilon,p_\varepsilon,\cdot)\Big)(z)=0,
\end{equation}
and the complete covariant Hessian coefficient produced at order one is
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:jet_frozen_second_coefficient}
    \sum_{i,j}X_{\varepsilon,ij}
      \Lambda_{x_\varepsilon,p_\varepsilon}
       \bigl(\chi_i(x_\varepsilon,p_\varepsilon,\cdot)e_j\bigr)
    =\Tr(D(x_\varepsilon,p_\varepsilon)X_\varepsilon).
\end{equation}
Equivalently, by the Hessian-sector reciprocity lemma, this is the usual
symmetrized Green--Kubo contraction.  No uncontrolled contribution of the form
$\varepsilon^{-1}D_p\chi\,(p_\varepsilon-p_0)$ or
$D_p\chi\,D_x^2\varphi$ appears; every coefficient-response contribution is
included in $\Lambda$ before the second cell problem is solved.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The contact point for the $\varepsilon$-problem is chosen first, and the cell
problem is then solved with the coefficient parameters
$(x,p)=(x_\varepsilon,p_\varepsilon)$ frozen in the singular part.  This gives
\eqref{eq:exact_contact_cancellation} before any passage to the limit and
removes the possible $\varepsilon^{-1}$ parameter error.  The proof never tests
the prelimit equation at $(x_\varepsilon,p_\varepsilon)$ with a corrector frozen
at the limiting jet $(x_0,p_0)$.

The multiplier $\partial_i\varphi(t,x)$ in \eqref{eq:jet_frozen_test} is not
frozen.  Differentiating the first corrector while holding the coefficient
argument $p_\varepsilon$ fixed gives
\begin{align}
    D_x\Xi_\varepsilon[\varphi]
    &=\sum_i \partial_i\varphi(t,x)D_x^{\mathrm{fr}}\chi_i(x,p_\varepsilon,z)
      +\sum_i \chi_i(x,p_\varepsilon,z)D_x\partial_i\varphi(t,x).
\end{align}
Evaluation at $(t_\varepsilon,x_\varepsilon)$ gives
\eqref{eq:contact_frozen_gradient_expansion}.  The second sum in that identity
produces the Hessian vector field
\begin{equation}
    q_X(z)=\sum_{i,j}X_{\varepsilon,ij}\chi_i(x_\varepsilon,p_\varepsilon,z)e_j .
\end{equation}
When the prelimit coefficients are evaluated at the complete gradient, the
order-one work generated by this vector field is not merely $b\cdot q_X$; it is
the complete covariant work $\mathfrak W_{x_\varepsilon,p_\varepsilon}(q_X)$.
Its invariant projection is
\begin{equation}
    \sum_{i,j}X_{\varepsilon,ij}
      \Lambda_{x_\varepsilon,p_\varepsilon}(\chi_i e_j),
\end{equation}
which equals $\Tr(DX_\varepsilon)$ by
\cref{lem:hessian_sector_reciprocity,def:GK_tensor}.  The skew part of the
finite-response connection is thus retained in the calculation but disappears
when contracted with the symmetric Hessian.  A composite corrector
$\chi_i(x,D_x\varphi(t,x),z)$ would create the chain-rule term
$D_p\chi_iD_x^2\varphi$; the contact-jet-frozen corrector uses
$\chi_i(x,p_\varepsilon,z)$ instead.  Parameter derivatives of $\chi_i$ enter
only later through ordinary continuity as
$(x_\varepsilon,p_\varepsilon,X_\varepsilon)\to(x_0,p_0,X_0)$, with no singular
factor.  This proves the lemma.
\end{proof}







\begin{lemma}[Correlation Hilbert bundle and Lipschitz diffusion factor]
\label{lem:correlation_hilbert_lipschitz_factor}
Fix a compact cotangent window $K$ and a coordinate ball $O\subset\Mcal$.  For
$p\in K$ and $x\in O$, let $\mathfrak c_{x,p}$ be the deterministic correlation
form on zero-mean finite-response observables,
\begin{equation}
    \mathfrak c_{x,p}(f,g)
    =\frac12\int_0^\infty\!\int
       \big(f(z)g(\Phi_{\Theta(x,p)}^t z)
          +g(z)f(\Phi_{\Theta(x,p)}^t z)\big)
        d\mu_{\Theta(x,p)}(z)\,dt .
\end{equation}
After quotienting the null space and completing, these forms define a
correlation Hilbert bundle $\mathscr H_{x,p}$.  Under the Banach-bundle
transport of \cref{def:anisotropic_banach_bundle} there is a common Hilbert
model $\mathscr H_{O,K}$ and finite-rank maps
\begin{equation}
    \Sigma(x,p)^*e_i=[b_i(x,p,\cdot)]_{\mathscr H_{x,p}},
    \qquad i=1,\ldots,m,
\end{equation}
for which
\begin{equation}
    D(x,p)=\Sigma(x,p)\Sigma(x,p)^* .
\end{equation}
Moreover,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:hilbert_factor_lipschitz_full}
    \|\Sigma(x,p)-\Sigma(y,p)\|_{HS(\mathscr H_{O,K},\R^m)}
    \le C_K |x-y|,
\end{equation}
where the constant is independent of $p\in K$.  The estimate is valid even at
points where $D(x,p)$ is degenerate; no matrix square root is taken.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The form $\mathfrak c_{x,p}$ is finite by the deterministic exponential flow
gap.  Positivity follows from the finite-time identity
\begin{equation}
    \mathfrak c_{x,p}(f,f)
      =\lim_{T\to\infty}\frac1{2T}
        \int\left(\int_0^T f(\Phi_{\Theta(x,p)}^t z)\,dt\right)^2
        d\mu_{\Theta(x,p)}(z),
\end{equation}
where convergence is justified by the same correlation bound; this is an
identity of deterministic time integrals.  The quotient by the null space and
completion therefore gives a Hilbert fiber.

To compare nearby fibers, transport observables and densities to a reference
fiber over $x_0\in O$.  The multi-response bounds
\cref{prop:uniform_multiresponse_bounds} imply the bilinear estimate
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:correlation_form_lipschitz}
    |\mathfrak c_{x,p}(f,g)-\mathfrak c_{y,p}(f,g)|
    \le C_K |x-y|\,
       \|f\|_{\widehat\Bcal^{\#}}\|g\|_{\widehat\Bcal^{\#}}
\end{equation}
for finite-response observables in the local chart.  Since
$b_i(x,p,\cdot)$ is finite-response and $C^1$ in $x$ with uniform bounds,
\begin{equation}
    \|b_i(x,p,\cdot)-b_i(y,p,\cdot)\|_{\widehat\Bcal^{\#}}
    \le C_K |x-y| .
\end{equation}
Hence, in the transported quotient Hilbert norm,
\begin{align}
    \|[b_i(x,p)]-[b_i(y,p)]\|_{\mathscr H_{O,K}}^2
    &\le C_K\|b_i(x,p)-b_i(y,p)\|_{\widehat\Bcal^{\#}}^2  \\
    &\le C_K |x-y|^2 .
\end{align}
The derivative of the form itself contributes only the harmless term in
\eqref{eq:correlation_form_lipschitz}, which is absorbed by choosing the
parallel transport in the correlation bundle through the Gram--Schmidt quotient
connection generated by $\nabla_x\mathfrak c_{x,p}$.  Summing over the finite
coordinate index $i=1,\ldots,m$ gives the Hilbert--Schmidt estimate
\eqref{eq:hilbert_factor_lipschitz_full}.  Since the construction uses the
classes $[b_i]$ in the correlation Hilbert space, degeneracy merely enlarges the
null space and does not create a non-Lipschitz square-root singularity.
\end{proof}



\begin{proposition}[Concrete common Hilbert transport for the diffusion factor]
\label{prop:concrete_common_hilbert_model}
Let $O\times K$ be a compact macroscopic coordinate window.  The correlation
Hilbert fibers $\mathscr H_{x,p}$ of
\cref{lem:correlation_hilbert_lipschitz_factor} admit deterministic parallel
transports
\begin{equation}
    J_{y\to x}^{p}:\mathscr H_{y,p}\longrightarrow\mathscr H_{x,p}
\end{equation}
with the following properties:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)}, wide, labelindent=0pt]
    \item $J_{x\to x}^{p}=I$ and $J_{z\to x}^{p}J_{y\to z}^{p}=J_{y\to x}^{p}$
    along coordinate line segments;
    \item $J_{y\to x}^{p}$ is an isometry for the correlation inner products;
    \item for the drift-factor maps
    $\Sigma(x,p)^*e_i=[b_i(x,p,\cdot)]_{\mathscr H_{x,p}}$ one has
    \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:transported_sigma_lipschitz}
        \|\Sigma(x,p)-J_{y\to x}^{p}\Sigma(y,p)\|_{HS(\R^m,\mathscr H_{x,p})}
        \le C_K|x-y| .
    \end{equation}
\end{enumerate}
Consequently
\begin{equation}
    D(x,p)=\Sigma(x,p)\Sigma(x,p)^*,
    \qquad
    D(y,p)=\Sigma(y,p)\Sigma(y,p)^*,
\end{equation}
may be compared in the single Hilbert fiber $\mathscr H_{x,p}$ by replacing
$\Sigma(y,p)$ with $J_{y\to x}^{p}\Sigma(y,p)$.  No expression of the form
$(I-G_{x,p})^{1/2}$ and no matrix square root at a degenerate covariance is
used.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Start with the dense finite-response observable module and the correlation form
$\mathfrak c_{x,p}$.  The response theorem gives
\begin{equation}
    |\partial_{x_a}\mathfrak c_{x,p}(f,g)|
      \le C_K\|f\|_{\mathscr H_{x,p}}\|g\|_{\mathscr H_{x,p}}
\end{equation}
for representatives modulo the null space.  Define a metric connection on the
quotient module by declaring the covariant derivative $\nabla_a f$ to be the
ordinary Banach-bundle derivative of the representative minus the unique
finite-rank correction, orthogonal to the null space, for which
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:correlation_metric_connection}
    \partial_{x_a}\mathfrak c_{x,p}(f,g)
     =\mathfrak c_{x,p}(\nabla_a f,g)+\mathfrak c_{x,p}(f,\nabla_a g).
\end{equation}
The correction is obtained by Riesz representation in the quotient Hilbert
fiber; if the null space changes, the equation is read on the quotient and then
closed by density.  The strong-to-weak response estimates make the connection
bounded on the finite-response module, and completion gives a metric connection
on the Hilbert bundle.

Solving the linear ODE for this connection along the segment from $y$ to $x$
gives the transport $J_{y\to x}^{p}$.  Equation
\eqref{eq:correlation_metric_connection} implies conservation of the correlation
inner product along the transported pair, hence $J_{y\to x}^{p}$ is an isometry.
Differentiating $[b_i(x,p,\cdot)]_{\mathscr H_{x,p}}$ covariantly gives a
bounded vector because $b_i$ is a finite-response observable and the moving
singularity terms are strong-to-weak bounded.  Integrating this covariant
derivative along the segment proves \eqref{eq:transported_sigma_lipschitz}.

The covariance identity follows directly from the Green--Kubo definition in each
fiber:
\begin{equation}
    e_i\cdot D(x,p)e_j
      =\langle \Sigma(x,p)^*e_i,\Sigma(x,p)^*e_j\rangle_{\mathscr H_{x,p}} .
\end{equation}
Because the transport is an isometry, moving the $y$-factor into
$\mathscr H_{x,p}$ preserves $D(y,p)$.  Thus the Crandall--Ishii comparison uses
a genuine common Hilbert space for the two points, while avoiding any
non-Lipschitz square-root selection at degenerate diffusion matrices.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Coefficient regularity and comparison structure]
\label{prop:coefficient_structure}
The geometric assumptions and the finite-response mechanical coupling imply the
following deterministic structure.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*), wide, labelindent=0pt]
    \item $D$ is symmetric positive semidefinite, bounded, and $C^{r-4}$ in
    $(x,p)$.  Its factorization in the correlation Hilbert bundle is Lipschitz
    in $x$, uniformly in the transmitted cotangent signal $p$.
    \item $H$ is $C^{r-4}$ and satisfies the finite-response bounds
    \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:H_growth_structure}
        |H(x,p)|\le C(1+|p|),\qquad
        |H(x,p)-H(y,p)|\le C(1+|p|)|x-y|,
    \end{equation}
    together with the corresponding local derivative bounds used in the
    viscosity argument.
    \item The operator
    \begin{equation}
        F(x,p,X)=-\Tr(D(x,p)X)-H(x,p)
    \end{equation}
    is degenerate elliptic and satisfies the Crandall--Ishii structural
    condition on the compact torus: there is a modulus $\omega$ such that,
    whenever
    \begin{equation}
        -3\alpha\begin{pmatrix}I&0\\0&I\end{pmatrix}
        \le
        \begin{pmatrix}X&0\\0&-Y\end{pmatrix}
        \le
        3\alpha\begin{pmatrix}I&-I\\-I&I\end{pmatrix},
    \end{equation}
    one has
    \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:global_CIL_condition}
        F(y,\alpha(x-y),Y)-F(x,\alpha(x-y),X)
        \le \omega\bigl(\alpha|x-y|^2+|x-y|\bigr).
    \end{equation}
\end{enumerate}
Consequently the terminal-value problem \eqref{eq:effective_hjb_final} has
comparison and uniqueness in the bounded uniformly continuous class.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The proof is divided into four deterministic steps.

\emph{Step 1: regularity of the averaged objects.}
The observable $b$, the potential $V$, the projection
$f\mapsto\Pi^\perp_\theta f$, and the reduced observable resolvent are sections
of the Banach bundle of \cref{def:anisotropic_banach_bundle}.  The response
formula in \cref{thm:parameter_response} is obtained by differentiating the
branchwise transfer operator and its contour resolvent in the strong-to-weak
anisotropic topology.  Composing this response with the smooth finite-response
map $(x,p)\mapsto\Theta(x,p)$ gives $C^{r-4}$ dependence of
$\chi_i(x,p,\cdot)$, of $\mu_{\Theta(x,p)}$, of the complete work response
$\mathfrak W_{x,p}$, and hence of $D$ and $H$.  The finite-response saturation
gives bounds uniform in $p$ for the microscopic coefficients, for the
moving-generator and centered-drift derivatives entering $\mathfrak W_{x,p}$,
and for their parameter derivatives.  The affine growth in $H$ comes from the
factor $\chi_p=\sum_i p_i\chi_i$ inside the covariant horizontal work response
$\Lambda_{x,p}(Q_x\chi_p)$ in \eqref{eq:H_raw_complete_covariant}, yielding
\eqref{eq:H_growth_structure}.

\emph{Step 2: positivity and the deterministic correlation factor.}
Positivity is \cref{prop:D_positive}.  More is needed for comparison: a
Lipschitz factor, not merely a Lipschitz matrix.  This factor is supplied by the
correlation Hilbert bundle constructed in
\cref{lem:correlation_hilbert_lipschitz_factor}.  In that bundle
\begin{equation}
    \Sigma(x,p)^*e_i=[b_i(x,p,\cdot)]_{\mathscr H_{x,p}},
    \qquad
    D(x,p)=\Sigma(x,p)\Sigma(x,p)^* ,
\end{equation}
and the deterministic response bounds for the branch transfer operator give
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:correlation_factor_lipschitz}
    \|\Sigma(x,p)-\Sigma(y,p)\|_{HS(\mathscr H_{O,K},\R^m)}
    \le C|x-y|,
\end{equation}
with $C$ independent of $p$ on compact cotangent windows.  This is the required
square-root structure; it is not obtained by taking a possibly non-Lipschitz
matrix square root at a degenerate point.

\emph{Step 3: the diffusion part of the Crandall--Ishii inequality.}
Let $p=\alpha(x-y)$ and let $X,Y$ satisfy Ishii's matrix inequality.  Apply that
matrix inequality to the vectors
$(\Sigma(x,p)^*\xi,\Sigma(y,p)^*\xi)$ and sum over an orthonormal finite-rank
projection of the correlation Hilbert space.  The finite-rank statement gives
\begin{equation}
    -\Tr(D(x,p)X-D(y,p)Y)
    \le 3\alpha\,\|\Sigma(x,p)-\Sigma(y,p)\|_{HS}^2
\end{equation}
for the projected factor.  The positive correlation forms increase monotonically
with the projection, and the exponential transfer bound gives a projection-tail
estimate independent of $x,y,p$.  Passing to the limit and using
\eqref{eq:correlation_factor_lipschitz} yields
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:diffusion_CIL_bound_from_factor}
    -\Tr(D(x,p)X-D(y,p)Y)
    \le C\alpha|x-y|^2 .
\end{equation}

\emph{Step 4: the Hamiltonian part and comparison.}
By \eqref{eq:H_growth_structure}, again with $p=\alpha(x-y)$,
\begin{equation}
    |H(x,p)-H(y,p)|
    \le C|x-y|+C\alpha|x-y|^2 .
\end{equation}
Combining this with \eqref{eq:diffusion_CIL_bound_from_factor} gives
\eqref{eq:global_CIL_condition} with
$\omega(r)=Cr$ after increasing $C$ on the compact torus.  Degenerate ellipticity
follows from $D\ge0$.  The deterministic comparison proof in
\cref{app:comparison} is then the standard doubling-of-variables argument for a
proper degenerate parabolic equation with the structural modulus just derived.
Only deterministic transfer, correlation, and comparison estimates enter this step.
\end{proof}


\subsection{The analytic two-scale equation}

For $\varepsilon>0$, the prelimit deterministic equation is posed in the
anisotropic observable space.  Its branchwise classical expression is the
full-gradient completion of \eqref{eq:prelimit_from_action_principle}:
\begin{align}
\label{eq:prelimit_deterministic_hjb}
    -\partial_t u^\varepsilon
    -\varepsilon^{-2}\Acal^{\rm fr}_{x,P}u^\varepsilon
    -\varepsilon^{-1}b(x,P,z)\cdot P
    -V^\circ(x,P,z)&=0,\\
    P&=D_xu^\varepsilon(t,x,z),
    \qquad u^\varepsilon(T,x,z)=\phi(x).
\end{align}
The cotangent variable $P$ in this display is the complete slow gradient of the
branchwise test touching the value function.  Thus a microscopic corrector gauge
contributes to $P$ through its horizontal derivative.  The anisotropic-duality
definition below is the completion of this branchwise equation and keeps this
full-gradient dependence; it does not replace $P$ by a split macroscopic jet.

\begin{definition}[Admissible microscopic densities]
\label{def:admissible_densities}
For $M>0$ and $\theta\in\Thetacal$, let
\begin{equation}
    \Adm_M(\theta)=\{\rho\in\widehat\Bcal_\theta:\rho\ge0,
    \langle1,\rho\rangle_\theta=1,
    \|\rho\|_{\widehat\Bcal_\theta}\le M\}.
\end{equation}
The positivity is understood by testing against nonnegative $C^1$ observables
on homogeneous flow boxes.  Standard-family densities produced by the billiard
flow belong to $\Adm_M(\theta)$ for a uniform $M$ after the deterministic growth
lemma.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[The paired anisotropic order is separating]
\label{prop:paired_order_separating}
Let $F$ be a bounded branchwise continuous observable section with summable
homogeneity-strip tails.  If
\begin{equation}
    \langle F,\rho\rangle_\theta\le0
    \qquad\hbox{for every }\rho\in\Adm_M(\theta)\hbox{ and every finite }M,
\end{equation}
then $F\le0$ at every regular point at which the stable-unstable density basis
differentiates $F$.  In particular, the paired order used in
\cref{def:viscosity_duality_solution} agrees with the ordinary a.e. order on
regular microscopic states after the canonical singular set is discarded.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Assume that $F(z_0)>\eta>0$ at a regular density point.  Choose a homogeneous
stable-unstable rectangle $R_\delta(z_0)$ avoiding the finite singular skeleton
up to the corresponding order and so small that the oscillation of $F$ on the
