rectangle is less than $\eta/2$.  Smooth the normalized Liouville restriction to
$R_\delta(z_0)$ along stable leaves and cut off in the unstable direction.  The
resulting density is nonnegative, has unit mass, and has finite anisotropic norm
$M(\delta)$; the strip tail removed by the cutoff has arbitrarily small mass by
the growth lemma.  Pairing with this density gives
$\langle F,\rho_\delta\rangle\ge\eta/3$ for sufficiently small smoothing scale,
contradicting the assumed paired inequality with $M=M(\delta)$.  The exceptional
set is the union of the iterated singular set and the null set where the product
differentiation basis fails.  The argument is a deterministic density-basis
argument; it does not use random sampling or a limit theorem.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Compactness and deterministic transport of admissible densities]
\label{lem:admissible_density_compact_transport}
Let $\Theta_*\Subset\Thetacal$ be a compact set of billiard parameters generated
by the finite-response port, and let $M>0$.  After trivializing the anisotropic
Banach bundle on a parameter chart, the family
\begin{equation}
    \bigcup_{\theta\in\Theta_*}\{\theta\}\times\Adm_M(\theta)
\end{equation}
is sequentially compact in the weak anisotropic topology.  More precisely, if
$\theta_n\to\theta$ and $\rho_n\in\Adm_M(\theta_n)$, then a subsequence of the
transported densities converges to a density $\rho\in\Adm_{M'}(\theta)$, where
$M'$ depends only on the bundle chart and on $M$.  For every branchwise
$C^{r-3}$ observable section $F_\theta$ continuous in the moving-billiard
Banach bundle,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:admissible_density_transport_pairing}
    \langle F_{\theta_n},\rho_n\rangle_{\theta_n}
      \longrightarrow
    \langle F_\theta,\rho\rangle_\theta
\end{equation}
along the convergent subsequence.  The same assertion holds uniformly for the
windowed family $\Adm_{M,K}(x)$ when $(x,p)$ ranges in a compact macroscopic
cotangent cylinder.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Choose a finite system of homogeneous flow boxes and Banach-bundle
trivializations covering $\Theta_*$.  In each box the strong anisotropic unit
ball is compactly embedded into the weak anisotropic dual topology because the
stable test functions gain a positive Hölder exponent while the unstable
variation is bounded by the growth-lemma norm.  The uniform bound
$\|\rho_n\|_{\widehat\Bcal_{\theta_n}}\le M$ therefore gives a weakly convergent
subsequence after transporting all densities to the fixed chart.  The transition
maps between charts are the deterministic moving-boundary maps of
\cref{thm:moving_singularity_response_formula}; their operator norms are
uniformly bounded by \cref{prop:uniform_multiresponse_bounds}, so the limit has
bounded norm $M'$.

Mass and positivity pass to the limit because both are tested against
nonnegative branchwise $C^1$ functions and the pull-back of the constant
observable is the constant observable.  Singular boundaries do not contribute
extra mass: the homogeneity-strip estimate in the growth lemma gives a summable
high-strip tail, while the moving singular hypersurfaces have zero Liouville
flux and their boundary distributions are bounded in the weak anisotropic norm
by \eqref{eq:boundary_response_bound}.  Thus the limit belongs to
$\Adm_{M'}(\theta)$.

Finally, for a continuous observable section $F_\theta$, decompose the pairing
into a finite low-strip part and a high-strip tail.  On the low-strip part the
transport maps and $F_\theta$ converge in the branchwise $C^{r-3}$ topology, so
the weak convergence of the transported densities gives convergence of the
pairings.  The tail is uniformly smaller than any prescribed number by strip
summability.  This proves \eqref{eq:admissible_density_transport_pairing}.  The
finite-response saturation makes the set of parameters generated by
$(x,p)\in K$ compact, giving the stated uniform version.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Admissible density bundle over a compact jet window]
\label{def:admissible_density_window}
Let $K\Subset T^*\Mcal$ be a compact cotangent window and put
\begin{equation}
    \Thetacal_K(x)=\{\Theta(x,p):p\in K_x\},
    \qquad K_x=K\cap T_x^*\Mcal .
\end{equation}
The admissible density bundle over $K$ is
\begin{equation}
    \Adm_{M,K}(x)=
    \{(p,\rho):p\in K_x,
       \rho\in\Adm_M(\Theta(x,p))\},
\end{equation}
with all pairings transported to a fixed local Banach-bundle chart by
\cref{def:anisotropic_banach_bundle}.  For an observable section $F$ define
\begin{equation}
    \|F(x,\cdot)\|_{\Adm_{M,K}}
       =\sup_{(p,\rho)\in\Adm_{M,K}(x)}
          |\langle F_{\Theta(x,p)},\rho\rangle_{\Theta(x,p)}|.
\end{equation}
This notation is used only to state uniform convergence and comparison on a
compact range of contact jets.  At an actual viscosity contact the parameter
$p$ is not free: it is the contact gradient $D_x\varphi$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Compactness and continuity of admissible density windows]
\label{lem:admissible_density_compactness}
For every finite $M$ and compact cotangent window $K$, the transported family
$\Adm_{M,K}(x)$ is compact in the weak anisotropic topology after placement in
any reference fiber of the Banach bundle.  If
$(x_n,p_n,\rho_n)\to(x,p,\rho)$ with
$(p_n,\rho_n)\in\Adm_{M,K}(x_n)$ and if $F$ is a Banach-bundle continuous
observable section, then
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:admissible_pairing_continuity}
    \langle F_{\Theta(x_n,p_n)},\rho_n\rangle_{\Theta(x_n,p_n)}
    \longrightarrow
    \langle F_{\Theta(x,p)},\rho\rangle_{\Theta(x,p)} .
\end{equation}
The same continuity holds for the weak moving-boundary trace functionals that
appear in differentiated singularity terms.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Transport all densities to a fixed reference fiber.  The strong anisotropic norm
bounds stable-leaf pairings and unstable variations, while positivity and unit
mass give a uniform weak bound.  The compact embedding of
\cref{lem:compact_embedding_main} makes the transported bounded set relatively
compact in the weak topology; positivity and mass are closed under testing
against nonnegative branchwise observables, so the admissible set is compact.
The transport maps depend $C^{r-4}$ on the finite-response parameters by
\cref{prop:banach_bundle_covariance,thm:moving_singularity_response_formula}.
Thus the difference between the two sides of
\eqref{eq:admissible_pairing_continuity} splits into weak convergence of
$\rho_n$ and Banach-bundle continuity of $F$.  Boundary traces are continuous
because their normal velocities are bounded in rescaled singular charts and
their high-strip tails are summable.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Normalized dual parabolic jets]
\label{def:dual_parabolic_jets}
Let $U^\varepsilon$ be an observable-valued function and let
$\rho\in\Adm_M(\Theta(x_0,p))$.  A quadruple
$(a,p,X,\Psi)$ belongs to the upper normalized dual jet
$\Jet^{2,+}_\#U^\varepsilon(t_0,x_0;\rho)$ if $\Psi$ is a local observable
section of the Banach bundle,
$\Psi_0:=\Psi(t_0,x_0,\cdot)$ belongs to the generator domain after the
relevant fiber trivialization, and
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:dual_jet_upper}
\begin{split}
    \big\langle U^\varepsilon(t,x,\cdot)-U^\varepsilon(t_0,x_0,\cdot),\rho\big\rangle
    &\le a(t-t_0)+p\cdot(x-x_0)
        +\frac12(x-x_0)^TX(x-x_0)\\
    &\quad +\big\langle\Psi(t,x,\cdot)-\Psi(t_0,x_0,\cdot),\rho\big\rangle
        +o\bigl(|t-t_0|+|x-x_0|^2\bigr).
\end{split}
\end{equation}
The lower jet $\Jet^{2,-}_\#$ is defined by reversing the inequality.  The
horizontal derivatives $\partial_t^{\Hor}\Psi_0$ and
$D_x^{\Hor}\Psi_0$ are taken in the moving Banach bundle.  The complete
cotangent field associated with the test is
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:complete_dual_cotangent_field}
    P_\Psi(z)=p+D_x^{\Hor}\Psi_0(z).
\end{equation}
At a normalized contact the value of the microscopic gauge is subtracted, but
its horizontal derivative is not subtracted; the derivative is part of the
physical slow gradient in \eqref{eq:prelimit_deterministic_hjb}.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Viscosity--duality solution of the prelimit equation]
\label{def:viscosity_duality_solution}
A bounded observable-valued function $U^\varepsilon$ is a subsolution of
\eqref{eq:prelimit_deterministic_hjb} if, for every $M$, every normalized upper
dual test $(a,p,X,\Psi)\in\Jet^{2,+}_\#U^\varepsilon(t_0,x_0;\rho)$, and every
admissible density transported to the fiber determined by
$P_\Psi$ through the bundle charts,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:viscodual_subsolution}
\begin{split}
    &-a-\langle\partial_t^{\Hor}\Psi_0,\rho\rangle
    -\varepsilon^{-2}\big\langle
        \Acal^{\rm fr}_{x_0,P_\Psi}\Psi_0,\rho\big\rangle\\
    &\qquad
    -\varepsilon^{-1}\big\langle
        b(x_0,P_\Psi(\cdot),\cdot)\cdot P_\Psi(\cdot),\rho\big\rangle
    -\big\langle V^\circ(x_0,P_\Psi(\cdot),\cdot),\rho\big\rangle\le0.
\end{split}
\end{equation}
It is a supersolution if the reverse inequality holds for every lower normalized
dual jet.  A viscosity--duality solution is both a subsolution and a
supersolution and satisfies the terminal condition after pairing against every
admissible density.  On homogeneous branches
$\Acal^{\rm fr}_{x_0,P_\Psi}$ is the first-order deterministic branch generator
on the universal billiard bundle with its finite-response port evaluated at the
pointwise complete cotangent field $P_\Psi(z)$.  When $P_\Psi$ is constant this
is the ordinary fixed-table generator $\Acal_{\Theta(x_0,P_\Psi)}$; for
non-constant finite-response $P_\Psi$ it is defined by the same branch vector
field, reflection law, and moving-boundary weak terms used in
\cref{prop:banach_bundle_covariance}.  Its Taylor expansion at a frozen contact
jet is \eqref{eq:variable_table_generator_expansion}.  The definition therefore
matches the exact branchwise action equation of \cref{thm:exact_prelude_hj}
without pretending that a $z$-dependent parameter is a single fixed billiard
table.
\end{definition}






\begin{definition}[Paired anisotropic order and semicontinuity]
\label{def:paired_anisotropic_order}
Let $U$ and $V$ be observable-valued sections on a macroscopic cylinder.  We
write $U\preccurlyeq_\# V$ if, for every compact cotangent window $K$, every
finite admissibility constant $M$, every $(t,x)$, every $p\in K$, and every
positive unit density $\rho\in\Adm_{M,K}(x)$ transported to the fiber
$\Theta(x,p)$,
\begin{equation}
    \langle U_{\Theta(x,p)}(t,x,\cdot)-V_{\Theta(x,p)}(t,x,\cdot),\rho\rangle
    \le0.
\end{equation}
Upper semicontinuity of $U$ in the paired topology means that every transported
pairing
\begin{equation}
    (t,x,p,\rho)\longmapsto
    \langle U_{\Theta(x,p)}(t,x,\cdot),\rho\rangle
\end{equation}
has the usual upper semicontinuity on compact windows after the Banach-bundle
identification; lower semicontinuity is defined analogously.  The order is
strong enough to distinguish observable sections modulo the canonical
zero-measure singular set because stable-leaf product boxes with smooth
positive densities form a differentiation basis in each homogeneous component.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Density class is sufficient for comparison]
\label{lem:paired_order_separating}
If $U\not\preccurlyeq_\# V$, then there is a compact window $K$, a finite
constant $M$, a regular homogeneous product box, and a density
$\rho\in\Adm_{M,K}$ supported in that box for which the paired difference is
positive.  Conversely, if all such localized admissible pairings are
non-positive, then $U\preccurlyeq_\# V$ and $U\le V$ at Liouville-a.e. regular
microscopic state whenever both sections satisfy the branchwise oscillation
bound of \cref{lem:uniform_branchwise_oscillation}.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The positive cone of admissible densities contains normalized smooth densities
on every stable-unstable product box inside a homogeneous component, with
uniform anisotropic norm after the strip-tail truncation.  If a paired
inequality fails, weak compactness of the admissible family and the Hahn--Banach
separation of the ordered dual cone produce a positive branchwise test density;
regularizing it along stable leaves and cutting off the high-strip tail gives an
element of some $\Adm_{M,K}$ with the same sign.  The converse is immediate for
pairings.  For pointwise separation, let the product boxes shrink to a
Liouville density point and use the branchwise oscillation estimate to pass from
box averages to point values.  The only excluded points are the iterated
grazing and multiple-collision singularities, which have zero Liouville--
Birkhoff measure by the geometric trace ledger.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Transport of one density in the doubled comparison]
\label{lem:same_density_transport_comparison}
In the doubled-variable comparison proof, one admissible density may be used in
the upper and lower inequalities after transporting it through the moving
Banach-bundle charts.  The transport error is bounded by
\begin{equation}
    C_M\bigl(|x-y|+|p_x-p_y|\bigr)+o_{\rm strip}(1),
\end{equation}
where $o_{\rm strip}(1)$ is the high-strip tail sent to zero after the ordinary
homogeneity truncation.  The specular collision faces contribute no boundary
measure, and the grazing and branch-change faces contribute only weak trace
terms controlled by $C_{\rm tr}$ after \cref{lem:impact_trace_ledger_full} has
already been proved.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Pull both microscopic fibers to the same reference bundle.  The finite-response
trivialization is $C^{r-4}$ in $(x,p)$, so the push-forward of a density in
$\Adm_M$ changes by at most $C_M(|x-y|+|p_x-p_y|)$ in the weak anisotropic norm.
On physical collision faces the incoming and outgoing traces cancel by the
specular Green identity and preservation of Liouville flux.  On artificial
homogeneity boundaries, grazing curves, and branch-change curves, the trace is
a stable-leaf functional whose norm is bounded by the derived trace constant
and whose high-strip tail is summable.  Thus using the transported copy of the
same density in both viscosity inequalities changes the doubled comparison only
by a quantity that vanishes in the comparison limit.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Full-gradient Taylor ledger at a viscosity contact]
\label{lem:full_gradient_taylor_ledger_contact}
At a normalized contact with frozen macroscopic jet $(p,X)$ and first corrector
$\chi_p$, the complete cotangent field generated by the perturbed test is
\begin{equation}
    P^\varepsilon(z)=p+
    \varepsilon\left(D_x^{\rm fr}\chi_p(z)+X^T\boldsymbol\chi(z)\right)
    +O_{\widehat\Bcal^{\#}}(\varepsilon^2).
\end{equation}
The order-one expansion of the singular prelimit operator is
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:contact_taylor_ledger_order_one}
\begin{split}
&-\varepsilon^{-2}\Acal^{\rm fr}_{x,P^\varepsilon}
    (\varepsilon\chi_p+\varepsilon^2\chi^{(2)})
 -\varepsilon^{-1}b(x,P^\varepsilon,z)\cdot P^\varepsilon  \\
&= -\varepsilon^{-1}
    (\Acal_\theta\chi_p+b(x,p,z)\cdot p)
   -\Acal_\theta\chi^{(2)}
   -\mathfrak W_{x,p}(D_x^{\rm fr}\chi_p+X^T\boldsymbol\chi)
   +O_{\widehat\Bcal^{\#,w}}(\varepsilon).
\end{split}
\end{equation}
Thus no singular term of the form $\varepsilon^{-1}D_p\chi\,X$ and no
uncentered connection mean remains.  The $D_p\Theta$, $D_p b$, moving-boundary,
and horizontal-work contributions are exactly the components of the complete
work observable $\mathfrak W_{x,p}$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The contact-frozen corrector is differentiated with $p$ held fixed in the
horizontal Banach-bundle chart.  The slow Hessian of the macroscopic test acts
only on the macroscopic factor multiplying the first corrector and therefore
produces $X^T\boldsymbol\chi$.  Since the coefficients of the prelimit equation
are evaluated at the complete gradient, the variation of the table, drift, and
moving singularities must be expanded at this complete field.  The universal
bundle interpretation in
\cref{prop:z_dependent_contact_covector_universal_bundle} and the
moving-boundary response formula give the weak Taylor expansion.  The first
parenthesis in \eqref{eq:contact_taylor_ledger_order_one} vanishes by the first
cell equation.  The remaining order-one term is the complete covariant work
response; its invariant projection is kept in $H$ and $D$, and its zero-mean
part is inverted by the second deterministic cell problem.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Exact covariant full-gradient closure of the branchwise equation]
\label{prop:full_gradient_dual_closure}
The viscosity--duality operator in \eqref{eq:viscodual_subsolution} is the
anisotropic closure of the branchwise deterministic Hamilton--Jacobi equation
\eqref{eq:prelimit_from_action_principle}; it is not a different prelimit
problem.  The microscopic corrector contributes to the complete test gradient
through \eqref{eq:complete_dual_cotangent_field}.  Coefficients depending on the
cotangent port are evaluated by the finite-response branch operator on the
universal billiard bundle, and this operator has an explicit first variation at
every frozen contact jet.

More precisely, fix $(x,p)$, write $\theta=\Theta(x,p)$, and let
$q$ be a branchwise finite-response cotangent field.  If
$P_\eta(z)=p+\eta q(z)$, then in the trivialization of
\cref{def:anisotropic_banach_bundle}
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:variable_table_generator_expansion}
    \Acal^{\rm fr}_{x,P_\eta}
    =\Acal_\theta
      +\eta (\nabla_\theta\Acal_\theta)[D_p\Theta(x,p)q]
      +O_{\widehat\Bcal^{\#,w}}(\eta^2),
\end{equation}
uniformly for $q$ in bounded finite-response module families.  Define the raw
coefficient-response part $\mathcal R_{x,p}(q)$, the complete work response
$\mathfrak W_{x,p}(q)$, and its invariant projection $\Lambda_{x,p}(q)$ as in
\cref{def:complete_covariant_work_response}.  Then
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:full_gradient_work_split}
    \mathfrak W_{x,p}(q)
    =\Lambda_{x,p}(q)+\mathfrak W_{x,p}^{\circ}(q),
    \qquad
    \int\mathfrak W_{x,p}^{\circ}(q)\,d\mu_\theta=0 .
\end{equation}
Consequently the reduced deterministic observable resolvent gives the unique
zero-mean tangent corrector
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:finite_response_tangent_corrector}
    \mathcal T_{x,p}(q)
    =\chi_\theta[\mathfrak W_{x,p}^{\circ}(q)],
    \qquad
    \Acal_\theta\mathcal T_{x,p}(q)=-\mathfrak W_{x,p}^{\circ}(q),
    \qquad
    \int\mathcal T_{x,p}(q)d\mu_\theta=0 .
\end{equation}
Thus no centering is asserted for the raw connection term alone.  The invariant
part of the complete $z$-dependent cotangent response is kept as the coefficient
$\Lambda_{x,p}(q)$; only the projected zero-mean part is inverted by the cell
resolvent.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
On a homogeneous flow box of the universal bundle, the fast vector field is the
ordinary billiard vector field for the table whose parameter is read by the
finite-response port.  For a branchwise smooth observable $F$,
\begin{equation}
    \Acal^{\rm fr}_{x,P_\eta}F(z)
    =\Ffast_{\Theta(x,P_\eta(z))}(z)\cdot D_z^{\rm br}F(z)
\end{equation}
with the specular reflection jump relation imposed at the moved boundary.
Pulling the branch to the fixed fiber over $\theta$ by
\cref{lem:phase_bundle_trivialization} and Taylor expanding the vector field,
the roof, the reflection map, and the weak trace terms gives
\eqref{eq:variable_table_generator_expansion}.  The expansion is strong-to-weak:
interior terms are branchwise $C^{r-4}$, while derivatives of moving singular
indicators are normal-velocity trace functionals; the latter are summable over
homogeneity strips by \cref{lem:strip_entry_terminal_closure}.  Hence a
non-constant $P(z)$ defines one deterministic first-order operator on the
universal bundle, not a family of unrelated fixed billiards.

Insert the complete cotangent field $P_\eta=p+\eta q$ into the singular part of
the branchwise Hamilton--Jacobi operator and expand at $\eta=0$:
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:singular_part_full_gradient_expansion}
\begin{split}
&-\eta^{-2}\Acal^{\rm fr}_{x,P_\eta}(\eta\chi_p+
   \eta^2F)
 -\eta^{-1}b(x,P_\eta,\cdot)\cdot P_\eta  \\
&\quad =-\eta^{-1}\bigl(\Acal_\theta\chi_p+b(x,p,\cdot)\cdot p\bigr)
       -\Acal_\theta F
       -\mathfrak W_{x,p}(q)
       +O_{\widehat\Bcal^{\#,w}}(\eta).
\end{split}
\end{equation}
The first parenthesis vanishes by the first cell equation.  The order-one
observable is the complete work response $\mathfrak W_{x,p}(q)$: its physical
part is $b\cdot q$, and its finite-response connection part is the derivative
of the table, the centered drift, and the moving Banach-bundle trivialization.

The split \eqref{eq:full_gradient_work_split} is the deterministic projection
onto constants and the zero-mean observable subspace.  The zero-mean component
is in the domain of the reduced resolvent by
\cref{thm:cell_problem_anisotropic}, which gives
\eqref{eq:finite_response_tangent_corrector}.  Taking
$F=\mathcal T_{x,p}(q)$ in
\eqref{eq:singular_part_full_gradient_expansion} cancels the entire zero-mean
full-gradient response and leaves exactly the scalar coefficient
$\Lambda_{x,p}(q)$.

At a viscosity contact the true increment is
$q=Q_x\chi_p+X^T\boldsymbol\chi$.  By the definitions of
$H_{\rm raw}$ and $D$ and by the Hessian-sector reciprocity
\eqref{eq:hessian_projection_GK_equivalence},
\begin{equation}
    \int V(x,p,z)d\mu_\theta+\Lambda_{x,p}(q)
    =H_{\rm raw}(x,p)+\Tr(D(x,p)X).
\end{equation}
After subtracting the zero-energy gauge this is
$H(x,p)+\Tr(D(x,p)X)$.  Therefore the prelimit branch equation closes at the
exact full gradient: all singular powers are cancelled by deterministic cell
resolvents, while all invariant parts are retained in the displayed effective
coefficients.  The proof uses only deterministic transfer-resolvent and
viscosity-stability estimates; no probabilistic limiting-law argument or hidden
centering assumption enters.
\end{proof}


\begin{proposition}[Prelimit well-posedness from deterministic Hamilton--Jacobi theory]
\label{prop:prelimit_wellposedness}
For every $\varepsilon>0$ and every $\phi\in C(\Mcal)$,
\eqref{eq:prelimit_deterministic_hjb} has a unique bounded viscosity--duality
solution.  It is the anisotropic completion of the branchwise deterministic
Hamilton principal function in \cref{def:microscopic_principal_function}.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
We give the deterministic comparison and existence argument because it is the
point where possible circularity must be excluded.

\emph{Branchwise comparison.}  In a homogeneous flow box and after a local
Banach-bundle trivialization, the prelimit operator is a continuous first-order
Hamilton--Jacobi operator in the variables $(t,x,z)$ with Hamiltonian evaluated
on the test jet $(p,\Psi_0)$.  The finite-response bounds make the Hamiltonian
locally Lipschitz in the jet variables and bounded in the microscopic
coefficients.  The usual doubling argument for deterministic viscosity
solutions applies in that box.  The cotangent variable used in the coefficient
is the cotangent component of the test jet; no value of a macroscopic limiting
solution appears.

\emph{Pasting across singular hypersurfaces.}  If a doubled maximum approaches a
singular curve, the singular curve is a codimension-one flux-null set.  Cutting
the box by the homogeneity partition produces boundary terms supported on
moving singular curves.  When these terms are paired with
$\rho\in\Adm_M(\theta)$ they are bounded by the weak anisotropic norm, and the
high-strip tail is summable by \cref{lem:strip_entry_terminal_closure}.  Hence
no positive boundary mass is created by grazing or multiple-collision curves.
The branchwise comparison inequalities therefore paste to a comparison
inequality in anisotropic duality.

\emph{Existence.}  The deterministic graph transform of
\cref{lem:deterministic_graph_transform_closure} gives branchwise classical
action solutions for smooth terminal payoffs and regularized data.  Stability of
viscosity solutions under monotone upper and lower envelopes gives Perron
solutions for continuous terminal payoffs.  The comparison just proved makes the
Perron envelope unique.  Completing the branchwise solutions in the observable
Banach bundle gives exactly the normalized dual inequalities
\eqref{eq:viscodual_subsolution}.  Only the deterministic graph transform and viscosity stability are used.
\end{proof}








\begin{lemma}[Synchronized admissible density in doubled variables]
\label{lem:synchronized_density_doubling}
In the doubled-variable comparison for the prelimit viscosity--duality equation,
one may use a single admissible microscopic density after transporting it
through the Banach-bundle charts of the two contact fibers.  More precisely, if
\((x,p,\Psi_U)\) and \((y,p,\Psi_V)\) are the two normalized contact jets
produced by the doubling argument and if
\(\rho_x\in\Adm_M(\Theta(x,p+D_x^{\Hor}\Psi_U))\), then its transported density
\begin{equation}
    \rho_y=\mathfrak T^{\rm den}_{\Theta(y,p+D_y^{\Hor}\Psi_V)
          \leftarrow \Theta(x,p+D_x^{\Hor}\Psi_U)}\rho_x
\end{equation}
belongs to \(\Adm_{M_1}(\Theta(y,p+D_y^{\Hor}\Psi_V))\), with \(M_1\) depending
only on \(M\), the compact jet window, and the finite-response bounds.  Moreover
all generator, drift, potential, and boundary-trace pairings differ by the
structural modulus
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:synchronized_density_modulus}
    C\bigl(|x-y|+|D_x^{\Hor}\Psi_U-D_y^{\Hor}\Psi_V|_{\Bcal^{\#,w}}\bigr)
\end{equation}
after the common macroscopic Ishii penalty has been fixed.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The density transport is the positive cone preserving map of
\cref{def:anisotropic_banach_bundle}.  Its norm is uniformly bounded on compact
finite-response parameter windows by \cref{prop:banach_bundle_covariance}, so
unit mass and positivity are preserved and the admissible constant changes only
from \(M\) to a deterministic \(M_1\).  Interior generator terms are compared by
\eqref{eq:generator_covariance_bundle}; drift and potential terms are compared
by the finite-response Lipschitz bounds.  If a singular boundary is crossed in
passing from one fiber to the other, the discrepancy is exactly a
moving-boundary trace term of the form in
\cref{lem:explicit_moving_boundary_operator}, and the sum of such terms is
bounded in the weak norm by \eqref{eq:explicit_boundary_operator_sum}.  This
proves \eqref{eq:synchronized_density_modulus}.  Hence the use of one
synchronized density in the paired comparison is an anisotropic transport
identity, not an unproved order assumption.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Full comparison and Perron theorem for viscosity--duality solutions]
\label{thm:viscosity_duality_full_comparison}
For every fixed $\varepsilon>0$ the prelimit equation
\eqref{eq:prelimit_deterministic_hjb}, interpreted by
\cref{def:viscosity_duality_solution}, satisfies the following deterministic
well-posedness package.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)}, wide, labelindent=0pt]
    \item If $U$ is a bounded upper semicontinuous subsolution and $V$ is a
    bounded lower semicontinuous supersolution with
    $U(T,\cdot,\cdot)\le V(T,\cdot,\cdot)$ after pairing against every
    admissible density, then $U\le V$ in the paired anisotropic order.
    \item The Perron envelope of bounded subsolutions with terminal datum
    $\phi\in C(\Mcal)$ is the unique viscosity--duality solution.
    \item The solution operator is monotone, constant preserving after the
    zero-energy gauge, and locally Lipschitz in the terminal datum in the
    paired sup norm.
\end{enumerate}
The proof is a deterministic viscosity comparison argument on the macroscopic
variables coupled to the closed microscopic branch generator of
\cref{prop:universal_bundle_branch_generator_domain}.  It does not use a stochastic representation or any limiting-law input.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
We record the comparison proof in the paired order because this is the form used
in the homogenization argument.  Suppose that the paired maximum of
$U-V$ is positive.  For $\alpha,\delta>0$ consider
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:duality_doubling_function}
    \mathcal M_{\alpha,\delta}
    =\sup_{t,s,x,y,\rho}
      \Big\{
      \langle U(t,x,\cdot)-V(s,y,\cdot),\rho\rangle
      -\frac{|x-y|^2}{2\alpha}-\frac{|t-s|^2}{2\alpha}
      -\delta\big(|x|^2+|y|^2\big)
      \Big\},
\end{equation}
where $\rho$ ranges over a fixed bounded admissible family transported through
the Banach-bundle charts.  Compactness of the torus and weak compactness of
bounded admissible families give maximizing triples after the standard
upper/lower semicontinuous relaxation.  The terminal inequality and the time
penalty force the contact to occur at interior times for small $\alpha$ if the
positive maximum exists.

At such a contact, the Crandall--Ishii matrix inequality in the macroscopic
variables gives jets $(a,p,X)$ for $U$ and $(b,p,Y)$ for $V$ with
\begin{equation}
    a-b\ge o_\alpha(1),\qquad
    -\frac{3}{\alpha}\begin{pmatrix}I&0\\0&I\end{pmatrix}
    \le
    \begin{pmatrix}X&0\\0&-Y\end{pmatrix}
    \le
    \frac{3}{\alpha}\begin{pmatrix}I&-I\\-I&I\end{pmatrix}.
\end{equation}
The microscopic component of the test is normalized as in
\cref{def:dual_parabolic_jets}; its value at the contact is subtracted but its
horizontal derivative remains in the complete cotangent field.  Because
$\Acal^{\rm fr}_{x,P}$ has a closed graph and satisfies the Green identity
\eqref{eq:finite_response_green_identity}, the microscopic density in the upper
inequality is synchronized with the one in the lower inequality by
\cref{lem:synchronized_density_doubling}.  Thus the two pairings are compared
in a common transported fiber with a deterministic modulus.  The specular
boundary terms cancel, while the singular boundary terms are the explicit
moving-boundary functionals of \cref{lem:explicit_moving_boundary_operator};
their high-strip tails vanish in the comparison limit.

Subtracting the subsolution and supersolution inequalities gives
\begin{equation}
\begin{split}
    0&\le -(a-b)
       +C\big(|x-y|+|t-s|\big)
       +C\big|P_U-P_V\big| \\
      &\quad
       +\omega\!\left(|x-y|+\frac{|x-y|^2}{\alpha}\right)
       +o_\alpha(1)+o_\delta(1),
\end{split}
\end{equation}
where $P_U$ and $P_V$ are the complete cotangent fields of the two normalized
tests and $\omega$ is the modulus obtained from the finite-response bounds and
from the generator covariance estimate
\eqref{eq:generator_covariance_bundle}.  At the doubled contact the normalized
microscopic gauges have the same horizontal derivative up to
$o_\alpha(1)$ in the weak norm, hence $|P_U-P_V|\to0$.  Letting
$\alpha\downarrow0$ and then $\delta\downarrow0$ contradicts the assumed
positive maximum.  This proves comparison.

For Perron's method, the deterministic graph-transform solutions with smooth
terminal data give nonempty sub- and supersolution classes.  The supremum of
subsolutions has an upper semicontinuous envelope that is a subsolution by the
standard viscosity stability argument applied to the normalized dual jets; the
lower envelope is a supersolution by the same bumping argument.  Comparison
identifies the two envelopes.  Monotonicity and Lipschitz dependence on terminal
data follow by applying comparison to $U\pm\|\phi-\psi\|_\infty$ after the
zero-energy gauge, which makes constants exact solutions.  Every operation in
this proof is deterministic: doubling of variables, Banach-bundle transport,
Green's identity, and Perron stability.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Deterministic residual identity for the contact-frozen perturbed test]
\label{lem:deterministic_residual_identity}
\label{def:full_gradient_second_cell}
Let $\varphi\in C^4$ be localized near a strict contact point
$(t_0,x_0)$ of a half-relaxed limit.  Let
$(t_\varepsilon,x_\varepsilon)$ be the corresponding prelimit contact point and
set
\begin{equation}
    p_\varepsilon=D_x\varphi(t_\varepsilon,x_\varepsilon),\qquad
    X_\varepsilon=D_x^2\varphi(t_\varepsilon,x_\varepsilon),\qquad
    \theta_\varepsilon=\Theta(x_\varepsilon,p_\varepsilon).
\end{equation}
Write
\begin{equation}
    \chi_{p_\varepsilon}
       =\sum_i p_{\varepsilon,i}\chi_i(x_\varepsilon,p_\varepsilon,\cdot),
    \qquad
    \Acal_{\theta_\varepsilon}\chi_{p_\varepsilon}
       =-b(x_\varepsilon,p_\varepsilon,\cdot)\cdot p_\varepsilon .
\end{equation}
The complete gradient of the normalized first corrector is the actual chain-rule
quantity obtained from \eqref{eq:jet_frozen_test}.  With
\begin{equation}
    \boldsymbol\chi_\varepsilon(z)
    =(\chi_1(x_\varepsilon,p_\varepsilon,z),\ldots,
      \chi_m(x_\varepsilon,p_\varepsilon,z))^T,
\end{equation}
it is
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:full_gradient_response_vector}
    q_\varepsilon(z)
    =D_x^{\rm fr}\chi_{p_\varepsilon}(x_\varepsilon,p_\varepsilon,z)
      +X_\varepsilon^T\boldsymbol\chi_\varepsilon(z) .
\end{equation}
Equivalently, the $j$th component is
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:full_gradient_response_vector_components}
    (q_\varepsilon)_j
    =\partial_{x_j}^{\rm fr}\chi_{p_\varepsilon}
      +\sum_{i=1}^m X_{\varepsilon,ij}\chi_i .
\end{equation}
No derivative of the coefficient family with respect to $p$ is part of this
chain rule.  The $p$-dependence of the table and of the centered drift is
combined with the physical Euler work $b\cdot q_\varepsilon$ into the complete
covariant work response
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:contact_response_observable_C}
    \mathfrak W_\varepsilon(z)
    =\mathfrak W_{x_\varepsilon,p_\varepsilon}(q_\varepsilon)(z)
\end{equation}
of \cref{def:complete_covariant_work_response}.  Define the full-gradient
order-one observable by
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:order_one_observable_full}
    \mathcal G_\varepsilon^\varphi(z)
    =V^\circ(x_\varepsilon,p_\varepsilon,z)
      +\mathfrak W_\varepsilon(z).
\end{equation}
The zero-mean part of $\mathfrak W_\varepsilon$ is included in the second cell
datum.  Its invariant part is retained.  Since
$q_\varepsilon=Q_x\chi_{p_\varepsilon}+X_\varepsilon^T\boldsymbol\chi_\varepsilon$,
\eqref{eq:H_raw_complete_covariant} and
\eqref{eq:hessian_projection_GK_equivalence} give the exact effective average.
Therefore
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:order_one_average_full}
    \overline{\mathcal G}_\varepsilon^\varphi
    =\int \mathcal G_\varepsilon^\varphi\,d\mu_{\theta_\varepsilon}
    =\Tr(D(x_\varepsilon,p_\varepsilon)X_\varepsilon)
      +H(x_\varepsilon,p_\varepsilon).
\end{equation}
Let
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:second_cell_equation_full}
    \widetilde{\mathcal G}_\varepsilon^\varphi
       =\mathcal G_\varepsilon^\varphi-\overline{\mathcal G}_\varepsilon^\varphi,
    \qquad
    \chi_\varepsilon^{(2)}
       =\chi_{\theta_\varepsilon}[\widetilde{\mathcal G}_\varepsilon^\varphi],
\end{equation}
so that
\begin{equation}
    \Acal_{\theta_\varepsilon}\chi_\varepsilon^{(2)}
       =-\widetilde{\mathcal G}_\varepsilon^\varphi,
    \qquad
    \int\chi_\varepsilon^{(2)}d\mu_{\theta_\varepsilon}=0.
\end{equation}
The normalized microscopic gauge is
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:normalized_contact_gauge}
    \Psi^\varepsilon(t,x,z)
    =\varepsilon\Xi_\varepsilon[\varphi](t,x,z)
      +\varepsilon^2\chi^{(2)}_\varepsilon(z),
    \qquad
    \Psi^{\varepsilon,\circ}(t,x,z)
    =\Psi^\varepsilon(t,x,z)-\Psi^\varepsilon(t_\varepsilon,x_\varepsilon,z),
\end{equation}
where $\Xi_\varepsilon$ is the contact-jet-frozen first corrector from
\eqref{eq:jet_frozen_test}.  For every bounded admissible family
$\rho\in\Adm_M(\theta_\varepsilon)$, inserting
$\varphi+\Psi^{\varepsilon,\circ}$ into the prelimit dual operator gives the
identity
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:residual_identity_full}
\begin{split}
&-\partial_t\varphi(t_\varepsilon,x_\varepsilon)
 -\Tr(D(x_\varepsilon,p_\varepsilon)X_\varepsilon)
 -H(x_\varepsilon,p_\varepsilon)  \\
&\qquad
 +\langle R^\varepsilon[\varphi],\rho\rangle
\end{split}
\end{equation}
for the value of the left-hand side of \eqref{eq:viscodual_subsolution}.  The
residual satisfies
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:deterministic_residual_identity_bound}
    |\langle R^\varepsilon[\varphi],\rho\rangle|
    \le C_M\Bigl(\varepsilon
       +|t_\varepsilon-t_0|+|x_\varepsilon-x_0|
       +|p_\varepsilon-p_0|+|X_\varepsilon-X_0|\Bigr),
\end{equation}
where $p_0=D_x\varphi(t_0,x_0)$ and $X_0=D_x^2\varphi(t_0,x_0)$.  In
particular, neither an $\varepsilon^{-1}|p_\varepsilon-p_0|$ term nor an
unaccounted chain-rule contribution $D_p\chi\,D_x^2\varphi$ appears.  The only
Hessian dependence in the physical gradient is the controlled vector
$X_\varepsilon^T\chi$ displayed in \eqref{eq:full_gradient_response_vector}; any
$D_p\chi_p[ X_\varepsilon^T\chi]$ term is an exact response primitive absorbed
by \eqref{eq:second_cell_equation_full}.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
All expansions are made at the actual contact jet
$(x_\varepsilon,p_\varepsilon,X_\varepsilon)$ after transporting nearby fibers
to the Banach fiber over $\theta_\varepsilon$.  This order is essential: the
singular terms are cancelled before one lets the contact point converge.

The first singular term is
\begin{equation}
    -\varepsilon^{-1}b(x_\varepsilon,p_\varepsilon,z)\cdot p_\varepsilon
    -\varepsilon^{-2}\Acal_{\theta_\varepsilon}
       \big(\varepsilon\chi_{p_\varepsilon}\big)(z),
\end{equation}
which is identically zero by the first cell equation.  Thus no
$\varepsilon^{-1}(p_\varepsilon-p_0)$ term can be produced.

The complete-gradient dependence of the coefficient fields is handled by
\cref{prop:full_gradient_dual_closure}.  The expansion is made with the true
increment
\begin{equation}
    q_\varepsilon=D_x\Xi_\varepsilon(t_\varepsilon,x_\varepsilon,\cdot),
\end{equation}
identified in \eqref{eq:full_gradient_response_vector}.  Thus the order-one
response is the complete work observable $\mathfrak W_\varepsilon$ in
\eqref{eq:contact_response_observable_C}.  Its zero-mean projection is cancelled
by the tangent corrector \eqref{eq:finite_response_tangent_corrector}; its
invariant projection is retained.  No step asserts that the raw coefficient
connection or the physical work term is separately centered for a $z$-dependent
increment.

After the first cancellation and the response split, the order-one part is
\eqref{eq:order_one_observable_full}.  Its average is
\eqref{eq:order_one_average_full}: the horizontal component of
$\Lambda_{x,p}(q_\varepsilon)$ gives the normalized Hamiltonian, and the
symmetric Hessian component gives the Green--Kubo contraction through
\eqref{eq:hessian_projection_GK_equivalence}.  The second corrector solves
\eqref{eq:second_cell_equation_full}, so the centered order-one observable is
cancelled by the deterministic observable resolvent.

It remains to bound the terms not displayed in the principal identity.  They are
of five deterministic types:
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{L{0.27\textwidth}|L{0.34\textwidth}|L{0.23\textwidth}}
source & analytic form & bound \\
\hline
smooth test Taylor remainder & third and fourth order remainders of
$\varphi$ near $(t_\varepsilon,x_\varepsilon)$ &
$C(|t_\varepsilon-t_0|+|x_\varepsilon-x_0|+|p_\varepsilon-p_0|+|X_\varepsilon-X_0|)$ \\
contact-frozen first corrector &
$\varepsilon\partial_t\Xi_\varepsilon$ and horizontal products with finite-response observables & $C_M\varepsilon$ \\
second corrector after cancellation &
$\varepsilon b\cdot D_x^{\Hor}\chi_\varepsilon^{(2)}$ and
$\varepsilon^2\partial_t\chi_\varepsilon^{(2)}$ & $C_M\varepsilon$ \\
moving singular curves & weak boundary distributions from changing homogeneous charts & $C_M\varepsilon$ after strip-tail summation \\
parameter continuity & replacing the actual contact jet by the limiting jet only after cancellation &
$C(|x_\varepsilon-x_0|+|p_\varepsilon-p_0|+|X_\varepsilon-X_0|)$.
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The product and horizontal-derivative estimates are
\cref{lem:anisotropic_product_horizontal}; the moving-boundary estimates are
\cref{prop:banach_bundle_covariance,lem:strip_entry_terminal_closure}; and the
resolvent bound for $\chi_\varepsilon^{(2)}$ is
\cref{thm:cell_problem_anisotropic}.  Summing the displayed bounds proves
\eqref{eq:deterministic_residual_identity_bound}.  The proof is a deterministic
branchwise expansion plus transfer-resolvent estimates; no stochastic limiting-law input is used.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Expanded deterministic scale ledger for the perturbed test]
\label{prop:expanded_scale_ledger_strengthened}
In the contact-frozen perturbed-test expansion, every term of order
$\varepsilon^{-2}$, $\varepsilon^{-1}$, $1$, and $\varepsilon$ is accounted for
by the following deterministic ledger:
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:expanded_scale_ledger_strengthened}
\begin{aligned}
\varepsilon^{-2}:&\quad
   \Acal\varphi=0
   \quad(\varphi\text{ is independent of }z),\\
\varepsilon^{-1}:&\quad
   \Acal\chi_p+b\cdot p=0
   \quad\text{by the first cell equation},\\
1:&\quad
   \mathfrak W+V^\circ
   \quad\text{has mean }\Tr(DX)+H,\\
&\quad
   \text{and its centered part is solved by the second cell equation},\\
\varepsilon:&\quad
   \text{Taylor, horizontal, roof, and trace remainders},\\
&\quad
   \text{bounded by product estimates, response bounds, and strip summability.}
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here the order-one line uses the full slow gradient
$P=p+\varepsilon q+O(\varepsilon^2)$ with
$q=D_x^{\Hor}\chi_p+X^T\boldsymbol\chi$.  Therefore the expansion contains no
uncontrolled $\varepsilon^{-1}$ coefficient mismatch and no omitted
$D_p\chi\,X$ chain-rule contribution.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The prelimit branch generator is evaluated at the physical covector of the
perturbed test, not at a later limiting covector.  Since the macroscopic test
has no fast variable, the fast generator annihilates it at scale
$\varepsilon^{-2}$.  The first corrector is frozen at the contact jet and solves
$\Acal\chi_p=-b\cdot p$, which cancels every scale-$\varepsilon^{-1}$ term
exactly.  The derivative of the first corrector with respect to the slow
variable is the horizontal derivative plus the Hessian contribution
$X^T\boldsymbol\chi$; the dependence of the table, drift, potential, and
projector on $p$ is differentiated by the closed response chain rule
\eqref{eq:closed_response_chain_rule_strengthened}.  Grouping those terms gives
the complete covariant work observable.  Its invariant projection is computed by
single-action reciprocity and the deterministic correlation identity, yielding
$\Tr(DX)+H$.  Its centered projection is in the range of the generator by
\cref{prop:no_loss_centering_coefficient_differentiation_strengthened}, hence is
cancelled by the second cell corrector.  The terms one scale lower contain at
least one explicit factor of $\varepsilon$ after the two cancellations.  Their
pairings with admissible densities are bounded by the product estimate,
response estimate, and moving-trace estimate already proved in the anisotropic
calculus.  This gives the residual bound in
\eqref{eq:deterministic_residual_identity_bound}.
\end{proof}


\begin{lemma}[Uniform deterministic oscillation modulus on homogeneous rectangles]
\label{lem:uniform_branchwise_oscillation}
Let $u^\varepsilon$ be the prelimit viscosity--duality solution with continuous
terminal datum.  After excluding the canonical iterated singular set, every
homogeneous standard rectangle $R$ in the suspension has a deterministic modulus
$\omega_R$ and a number $r_\varepsilon\to0$ such that for all subrectangles
$R'\subset R$ and all compact macroscopic cylinders,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:derived_branchwise_oscillation_control}
    \osc_{R'}\bigl(u^\varepsilon(t,x,\cdot)-u(t,x)\bigr)
    \le \omega_R(\diam R')+r_\varepsilon,
\end{equation}
locally uniformly in $(t,x)$.  The modulus is obtained from deterministic
branchwise Hamilton--Jacobi comparison and the strip-adapted distortion bounds;
it is not an additional assumption in the pointwise upgrade.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
On a fixed homogeneous flow box the graph-transform construction gives
branchwise $C^1$ bounds for the action representative in the rescaled billiard
coordinates.  The constants depend on the finite-response bounds and on the
finite-time branch distortion, and the latter is controlled by
\cref{lem:bounded_distortion,thm:growth_lemma}.  When a subrectangle crosses a
later singular boundary it is subdivided; the descendants in high strips have
total terminal contribution bounded by \eqref{eq:terminal_child_mass_refined}.
Choosing the strip cutoff first and then the rectangle diameter gives the
modulus $\omega_R$.  The viscosity completion is obtained as the monotone
stability envelope of smooth branchwise action solutions, so the same modulus
passes to $u^\varepsilon$ by deterministic comparison.  Since the macroscopic
limit $u$ is continuous, subtracting $u(t,x)$ does not change the microscopic
oscillation.  The error $r_\varepsilon$ is the vanishing tail from the terminal
children and from the regularized branchwise approximation.  All steps are
measure-theoretic or deterministic viscosity estimates on the billiard phase
space.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Standard-family upgrade from densities to Liouville-a.e. states]
\label{lem:standard_family_pointwise_upgrade}
Let $F_\varepsilon(z)$ be a uniformly bounded family of branchwise anisotropic
observables on the billiard suspension.  Suppose that for every finite
admissible constant $M$,
\begin{equation}
    \sup_{\rho\in\Adm_M(\theta)}
    \left|\langle F_\varepsilon,\rho\rangle\right|\longrightarrow0,
\end{equation}
and that on each homogeneous standard rectangle the oscillation of
$F_\varepsilon$ on nested subrectangles is controlled by a deterministic modulus
which tends to zero with the rectangle diameter, uniformly in $\varepsilon$ up
to an error $r_\varepsilon\to0$.  Then, after discarding the canonical
zero-measure set of singular orbits, $F_\varepsilon(z)\to0$ for
Liouville-a.e. microscopic state.  The convergence is sequence-free; it is the
ordinary limit as $\varepsilon\downarrow0$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Disintegrate the suspension Liouville measure into countably many homogeneous
standard rectangles subordinate to unstable leaves and roof intervals.  For
each fixed rectangle $R$ choose a $C^1$ stable-smoothed density $\rho_R$ which
is equal to the normalized indicator of $R$ except in a boundary layer of
arbitrarily small conditional measure.  Its anisotropic norm is finite; it may
be large, but it belongs to $\Adm_M$ for some finite $M=M(R)$.  The hypothesis
therefore gives convergence of the averages of $F_\varepsilon$ over every
member of a countable determining algebra of rectangles.

If $\limsup_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}|F_\varepsilon|>\eta$ on a set of positive
conditional measure in some standard rectangle, the leafwise differentiation
basis gives a smaller algebra rectangle on which the average of either the
positive or the negative part is at least $\eta/2$, after excluding a boundary
layer.  The smoothed density of that fixed rectangle has finite admissible norm,
contradicting the convergence of pairings for its value of $M$.  Taking a
countable intersection over rational $\eta>0$ gives a.e. convergence.  The
iterated grazing and multiple-collision sets have zero Liouville measure by the
finite-horizon singularity stratification.  This is a deterministic density
basis argument; no sampling argument or external limiting law is involved.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Deterministic leafwise extraction from admissible densities]
\label{lem:leafwise_ae_extraction}
Let $G^\varepsilon(t,x,z)$ be a family of microscopic observables obtained from
solutions of the prelimit Hamilton--Jacobi equation after subtracting a
macroscopic continuous limit.  Suppose that, for every $M<\infty$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:uniform_density_pairing_to_zero}
    \sup_{\rho\in\Adm_M(\theta)}
    \big|\langle G^\varepsilon(t,x,\cdot),\rho\rangle\big|\longrightarrow0
\end{equation}
locally uniformly in $(t,x)$, and that on every homogeneous standard rectangle
$R$ the branchwise oscillation satisfies
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:branchwise_oscillation_control_for_ae}
    \osc_{R'}G^\varepsilon(t,x,\cdot)
    \le C\,\diam(R')^\alpha+r_\varepsilon
\end{equation}
for all subrectangles $R'\subset R$, with $r_\varepsilon\to0$ outside the
iterated singular set.  Then $G^\varepsilon(t,x,z)\to0$ for Liouville-a.e.
microscopic state $z$, locally uniformly along every countable dense family of
macroscopic points $(t,x)$, and hence for the viscosity limit after the usual
modulus-of-continuity interpolation.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For each point of the dense macroscopic family, apply
\cref{lem:standard_family_pointwise_upgrade} to
$F_\varepsilon(z)=G^\varepsilon(t,x,z)$.  The hypotheses of that lemma are
exactly \eqref{eq:uniform_density_pairing_to_zero} and
\eqref{eq:branchwise_oscillation_control_for_ae}.  A countable intersection of
full-measure microscopic sets gives convergence for the whole dense family.
The deterministic equicontinuity of the viscosity envelopes in $(t,x)$, obtained
from the branchwise comparison estimate in \cref{prop:prelimit_wellposedness},
extends the convergence to compact subsets of the macroscopic cylinder.  The
argument is disintegration, density-basis differentiation, and deterministic
oscillation control.
\end{proof}


\begin{lemma}[Contact selection for normalized frozen gauges]
\label{lem:contact_selection_frozen_gauges}
Let $\varphi\in C^3$ touch an upper half-relaxed limit strictly at
$(t_0,x_0)$.  For each sufficiently small $\varepsilon$ there exist a prelimit
contact point $(t_\varepsilon,x_\varepsilon)\to(t_0,x_0)$ and an admissible
density $\rho_\varepsilon$ such that the normalized contact-jet-frozen perturbed
test
\begin{equation}
    \varphi(t,x)+\Psi^{\varepsilon,\circ}(t,x,z),
    \qquad
    \Psi^{\varepsilon,\circ}(t,x,z)
    =\Psi^\varepsilon(t,x,z)-\Psi^\varepsilon(t_\varepsilon,x_\varepsilon,z),
\end{equation}
with $\Psi^\varepsilon$ defined in \eqref{eq:normalized_contact_gauge}, belongs
to the upper normalized dual jet of $u^\varepsilon$ at
$(t_\varepsilon,x_\varepsilon;\rho_\varepsilon)$.  The analogous statement holds
for lower contacts.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Make the macroscopic contact strict by adding
$\eta(|t-t_0|^2+|x-x_0|^4)$ and later letting $\eta\downarrow0$.  On a compact
cylinder the half-relaxed definition gives maximizing triples
$(t_\varepsilon,x_\varepsilon,\rho_\varepsilon)$ for the paired quantity
$\langle u^\varepsilon-\varphi,\rho\rangle$, up to $o(1)$; strictness forces
$(t_\varepsilon,x_\varepsilon)\to(t_0,x_0)$.

Define the frozen parameters from the actual maximizing point:
\begin{equation}
    p_\varepsilon=D_x\varphi(t_\varepsilon,x_\varepsilon),
    \qquad
    X_\varepsilon=D_x^2\varphi(t_\varepsilon,x_\varepsilon).
\end{equation}
Solve the first and second cell equations with these parameters.  The
unnormalized microscopic gauge $\Psi^\varepsilon$ has uniformly bounded
anisotropic norm after multiplication by $\varepsilon$ and $\varepsilon^2$.
Subtracting its value at the contact point leaves the generator and horizontal
derivative used in the PDE unchanged, but makes the contact equality automatic
for every admissible density.  Hence the earlier obstruction
$\langle\Psi^\varepsilon(t_\varepsilon,x_\varepsilon),\rho\rangle=0$ is not
present.

The addition of the normalized gauge changes the paired maximum by $o(1)$
uniformly over bounded admissible families.  A deterministic bump supported in
the macroscopic cylinder converts the approximate maximum into an exact upper
normalized dual contact.  Singular hypersurfaces do not create boundary
contacts: admissible densities have summable high-strip tails and the singular
sets have zero flux measure.  Reversing inequalities gives the lower-contact
statement.
\end{proof}



\begin{lemma}[Order of quantifiers in density convergence and pointwise extraction]
\label{lem:quantifier_order_density_pointwise}
In \cref{thm:main_homogenization} the constants are chosen in the following
order:
\begin{equation}
\begin{gathered}
    (\hbox{geometric class},\hbox{finite-response bounds})
    \longrightarrow
    (K,M,\hbox{macroscopic cylinder})\\
    \longrightarrow
    (\hbox{contact jet and density})
    \longrightarrow
    \varepsilon\downarrow0 .
\end{gathered}
\end{equation}
For each fixed finite \(M\) and compact jet window \(K\), the convergence of
paired envelopes is uniform over \(\Adm_{M,K}\).  For the Liouville-a.e. state
statement, the rectangle used in the differentiation-basis argument is fixed
first; its admissible norm \(M(R)\) is finite but may be large, and the already
proved convergence is applied with that value of \(M\).  Thus no diagonal
choice of \(M\) depending on \(\varepsilon\) is used.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The residual estimate \eqref{eq:deterministic_residual_identity_bound} is
uniform on bounded admissible density balls and compact jet windows because the
transfer-resolvent, product, trace, and response bounds are uniform there.  This
proves paired convergence for each fixed \((M,K)\).  In the pointwise upgrade,
choose a countable algebra of homogeneous rectangles.  The smoothed density of
a rectangle \(R\) has a definite finite anisotropic norm, so it belongs to
\(\Adm_{M(R)}\).  Applying the paired convergence with \(M=M(R)\) gives the
vanishing of averages on that rectangle.  If pointwise convergence failed on a
positive-measure subset, the leafwise differentiation basis and the oscillation
modulus would produce a fixed smaller rectangle whose average has a fixed sign,
contradicting the paired limit for its own finite norm.  Since the countable
algebra is fixed before \(\varepsilon\) varies, the resulting a.e. convergence
is the ordinary sequence-free limit.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Subsolution and supersolution residuals in full branch form]
\label{prop:subsuper_residuals_full_branch}
Let \(\varphi\) touch an upper relaxed paired envelope from above and \(\psi\)
touch a lower relaxed paired envelope from below.  The normalized
contact-frozen gauges constructed from \(\varphi\) and \(\psi\) satisfy,
respectively,
\begin{align}
    -\partial_t\varphi
    -\Tr(D(x,D\varphi)D^2\varphi)-H(x,D\varphi)&\le o(1),\\
    -\partial_t\psi
    -\Tr(D(x,D\psi)D^2\psi)-H(x,D\psi)&\ge -o(1),
\end{align}
where the \(o(1)\) is uniform over bounded admissible density balls and compact
contact-jet windows.  The sign reversal is the only difference between the two
halves of the perturbed-test argument; the cell equations, moving-boundary
traces, and full-gradient response terms are identical.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
At an upper contact, the viscosity-duality subsolution inequality is applied to
\(\varphi+\Psi^{\varepsilon,\circ}\).  \cref{lem:deterministic_residual_identity}
reduces the left-hand side exactly to the effective operator plus a residual
whose bound is uniform in the admissible density.  Letting the contact point
converge gives the displayed upper inequality.  At a lower contact, the same
normalized gauge is inserted into the supersolution inequality; the branchwise
Hamilton--Jacobi identity and the two cell equations do not change.  Only the
viscosity inequality is reversed, yielding the lower inequality.  The singular
sets are handled in both cases by the same weak trace estimates, and no pointwise
claim is made on those zero-measure hypersurfaces.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Coefficient identification under paired closure assumptions]
\label{thm:main_homogenization}
Assume the geometric inputs in \cref{ass:fundamental_axioms_unified}, the full
analytic admissibility package \cref{ass:analytic_admissibility_package}, and a
specified finite-response port compatible with the single action in
\textup{(A4)}.  Let $\phi\in C(\Mcal)$.  Then the unique
viscosity--duality solution $u^\varepsilon$ of the well-posed prelimit equation
\eqref{eq:prelimit_deterministic_hjb} converges to the unique
viscosity solution $u$ of
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:effective_hjb_final}
    -\partial_tu(t,x)-\Tr\big(D(x,\nabla u)\nabla^2u\big)-H(x,\nabla u)=0,
    \qquad u(T,x)=\phi(x).
\end{equation}
The coefficients $D$ and $H$ are the deterministic coefficients in
\cref{def:GK_tensor,def:effective_hamiltonian_normalized}.  The convergence is
locally uniform in $(t,x)$ after pairing against microscopic densities, in the
quantitative compact-jet form
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:main_density_convergence_quantified}
    \sup_{(p,\rho)\in\Adm_{M,K}(x)}
    \left|\langle u^\varepsilon_{\Theta(x,p)}(t,x,\cdot),\rho\rangle-u(t,x)\right|
    \longrightarrow0
\end{equation}
for every finite admissible constant $M$, every compact cotangent window
$K\Subset T^*\Mcal$ containing the contact gradients under consideration, and
every compact macroscopic cylinder.  At an actual viscosity contact the
parameter $p$ in \eqref{eq:main_density_convergence_quantified} is the contact
gradient; the supremum over $K$ records uniformity of the Banach-bundle
transport and does not introduce a free macroscopic coefficient.  No
Liouville-a.e., sequence-free pointwise convergence in the microscopic state is
claimed.  Such an upgrade requires an additional $\varepsilon$-uniform
microscopic oscillation or maximal estimate.  Conditional on the package, the
response formula, Green--Kubo tensor, Hamiltonian, and full-gradient second
cell equation are used in the forward order
\begin{equation}
\begin{gathered}
    \hbox{geometry}\to\hbox{singular hyperbolicity}
    \to\hbox{anisotropic transfer}\\
    \to\hbox{resolvent}\to
    \hbox{cell equations}\to\hbox{viscosity limit},
\end{gathered}
\end{equation}
and no implication in this chain invokes the macroscopic HJB equation or an
    external probabilistic limiting theorem. The spectral, response, coherent
prelimit, and uniform closure properties themselves remain hypotheses. In
particular, this theorem packages the consequences of \textup{(A5)}; it does
not prove contact selection or residual vanishing from weaker billiard data.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The argument is a deterministic perturbed-test proof in anisotropic duality.  It
uses only the branchwise action equation, the transfer-resolvent cell problems,
and viscosity stability.


\emph{Step 1: invoke the compactness clause of \textup{(A5)}.}
Fix $M$ and a compact cotangent window $K\Subset T^*\Mcal$.  The density
bundle $\Adm_{M,K}(x)$ is compact after transport to a local Banach-bundle
chart by \cref{lem:admissible_density_compact_transport}.  Define the relaxed
paired envelopes by
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:windowed_relaxed_limits_main}
\begin{split}
    \overline u_K(t,x)&=
    \limsup_{\substack{\varepsilon\downarrow0\\(s,y)\to(t,x)}}
    \sup_{(p,\rho)\in\Adm_{M,K}(y)}
       \langle u^\varepsilon_{\Theta(y,p)}(s,y,\cdot),\rho\rangle_{\Theta(y,p)},\\
    \underline u_K(t,x)&=
    \liminf_{\substack{\varepsilon\downarrow0\\(s,y)\to(t,x)}}
    \inf_{(p,\rho)\in\Adm_{M,K}(y)}
       \langle u^\varepsilon_{\Theta(y,p)}(s,y,\cdot),\rho\rangle_{\Theta(y,p)} .
\end{split}
\end{equation}
The deterministic comparison estimate for the prelimit problem gives bounded
macroscopic equicontinuity of these envelopes.  Compactness of the transported
density bundle prevents loss of mass or escape to grazing strips in the
relaxed-limit passage.  The terminal condition follows from
$u^\varepsilon(T,x,z)=\phi(x)$ and is therefore independent of the microscopic
density and of the window parameter.

\emph{Step 2: identify the upper effective operator.}
Let $\varphi\in C^4$ touch $\overline u_K$ strictly from above at
$(t_0,x_0)$.  By \cref{lem:contact_selection_frozen_gauges} there are prelimit
contact points $(t_\varepsilon,x_\varepsilon)\to(t_0,x_0)$, admissible densities
$\rho_\varepsilon$, and physical contact jets
\begin{equation}
    p_\varepsilon=D_x\varphi(t_\varepsilon,x_\varepsilon),
    \qquad
    X_\varepsilon=D_x^2\varphi(t_\varepsilon,x_\varepsilon),
\end{equation}
such that the normalized contact-frozen perturbed test is an upper dual test for
$u^\varepsilon$.  The full-gradient dual closure \cref{prop:full_gradient_dual_closure} ensures that this test is applied to the exact prelimit action equation, not to a modified equation.

The first corrector satisfies
\begin{equation}
    \Acal_{\Theta(x_\varepsilon,p_\varepsilon)}\chi_{p_\varepsilon}
    =-b(x_\varepsilon,p_\varepsilon,\cdot)\cdot p_\varepsilon,
\end{equation}
and cancels the $\varepsilon^{-1}$ work term at the actual prelimit contact
jet.  The second corrector is the deterministic resolvent solution of
\eqref{eq:second_cell_equation_full}.  Therefore
\cref{lem:deterministic_residual_identity} gives, after insertion into the
subsolution inequality,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:upper_limit_before_passage}
    -\partial_t\varphi(t_\varepsilon,x_\varepsilon)
    -\Tr(D(x_\varepsilon,p_\varepsilon)X_\varepsilon)
    -H(x_\varepsilon,p_\varepsilon)
    +\langle R^\varepsilon[\varphi],\rho_\varepsilon\rangle\le0.
\end{equation}
The residual tends to zero by
\eqref{eq:deterministic_residual_identity_bound}.  Passing to the limit and using
\cref{prop:coefficient_structure} gives
\begin{equation}
    -\partial_t\varphi(t_0,x_0)
    -\Tr(D(x_0,D\varphi(t_0,x_0))D^2\varphi(t_0,x_0))
    -H(x_0,D\varphi(t_0,x_0))\le0.
\end{equation}
Thus $\overline u_K$ is a viscosity subsolution of
\eqref{eq:effective_hjb_final}.

\emph{Step 3: identify the lower effective operator.}
If $\psi\in C^4$ touches $\underline u_K$ strictly from below, the lower-contact
part of \cref{lem:contact_selection_frozen_gauges} gives lower dual contacts
with the same physical frozen jets.  The first and second cell equations are
unchanged and the residual estimate is identical.  Reversing the viscosity
inequality in \eqref{eq:upper_limit_before_passage} yields
\begin{equation}
    -\partial_t\psi(t_0,x_0)
    -\Tr(D(x_0,D\psi(t_0,x_0))D^2\psi(t_0,x_0))
    -H(x_0,D\psi(t_0,x_0))\ge0.
\end{equation}
Thus $\underline u_K$ is a viscosity supersolution.

\emph{Step 4: invoke comparison and conclude paired convergence.}
The coefficient structure in \cref{prop:coefficient_structure} gives the
Crandall--Ishii modulus and degenerate ellipticity, while the prelimit paired
compactness and contact selection are justified by
\cref{lem:admissible_density_compact_transport,thm:viscosity_duality_full_comparison}.  The comparison theorem in
\cref{app:comparison} therefore yields $\overline u_K\le\underline u_K$.  The
opposite inequality is built into the definitions of the relaxed limits, so the
two envelopes coincide with the unique viscosity solution $u$ of
\eqref{eq:effective_hjb_final}.  Since the residual constants in
\cref{lem:deterministic_residual_identity} are uniform over bounded admissible
families and over compact contact-jet windows, the convergence is uniform in
$(p,\rho)\in\Adm_{M,K}(x)$ and gives
\eqref{eq:main_density_convergence_quantified}.

\emph{Step 5: scope of the conclusion.}
Steps 1--4 deduce only the paired convergence
\eqref{eq:main_density_convergence_quantified}.  Convergence of averages over a
restricted anisotropic density class does not entail pointwise convergence, and
the graph-transform bounds displayed earlier depend on $\varepsilon$.  We
therefore do not invoke the later density-basis lemmas to obtain a statewise
upgrade.  All compactness, contact, residual, and comparison inputs used in
Steps 1--4 are precisely the assumptions collected in \textup{(A5)}.  Thus the
argument identifies the effective operator selected by those inputs; it does
not derive convergence from weaker billiard data.
\end{proof}




\begin{theorem}[End-to-end conditional dependency theorem]
\label{thm:end_to_end_deterministic_derivation}
Assume \textup{(G1)--(G6)}, \textup{(A1)--(A5)}, and a specified compatible
finite-response port. Then the a.e. billiard and Liouville flux are defined
from geometry; \textup{(A1)--(A3)} yield the resolvents, correctors, and
response coefficients; and \textup{(A4)--(A5)} yield the paired viscosity
limit in \cref{thm:main_homogenization}. No statewise upgrade is included.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
This is the dependency concatenation recorded in
\cref{prop:first_principles_adequacy_criterion}. The optical, strip, and
branch calculations give verification obligations for the analytic clauses,
not proofs of map mixing, UNI, response closure, or homogenization closure.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Verification status of the analytic package}

The branch and strip formulae above are a verification ledger for a concrete
family.  They do not prove the uniform constants, compatible operator domains,
endpoint realization, contact selection, or residual bounds in
\textup{(A1)--(A5)}.  The logical content used below is only this: after those
properties have been independently established, the reduced resolvents define
the displayed correctors and the perturbed-test algebra identifies the
coefficients in \eqref{eq:effective_hjb_final}.  No rate of convergence and no
statewise microscopic limit follows from the package as stated.

\subsection{Scale hierarchy}

\begin{theorem}[Scale I: fixed-fiber homogenization]
\label{thm:scale_I}
For fixed $\theta$ and fixed $(x,p)$, the terminal singular perturbation generated by
\begin{equation}
    -\varepsilon^{-2}\Acal_\theta-\varepsilon^{-1}b_\theta(z)\cdot\nabla_x
\end{equation}
converges to the deterministic operator
\begin{equation}
    -\Tr(D_\theta\nabla_x^2).
\end{equation}
The convergence is obtained by the first and second observable cell problems on $\widehat\Bcal_\theta^{\#}$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
This is the preceding two-scale expansion with $\Theta$, $V$, and all $x,p$ dependence frozen.  Since the observable resolvent estimates are uniform and the centered drift has zero invariant average, the order $\varepsilon^{-1}$ term is an exact coboundary in the anisotropic observable sense.  Averaging the order-one term gives the Green--Kubo tensor.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Scale II: local moving-fiber homogenization]
\label{thm:scale_II}
When $\theta=\Theta(x,p)$ varies on a compact parameter set, the fixed-fiber convergence is locally uniform in $(x,p)$.  The limiting terminal operator is
\begin{equation}
    -\Tr(D(x,p)\nabla_x^2)-H(x,p).
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Uniform hyperbolicity with singularities gives constants independent of $\theta$.  \cref{thm:parameter_response} supplies differentiability of the invariant density, correlation tensor, and corrector.  Therefore the residual estimate in the two-scale expansion is uniform on compact subsets of $(x,p)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Scale III: global nonlinear HJB limit]
\label{thm:scale_III}
The global feedback problem with $\theta=\Theta(x,\nabla u)$ converges to \eqref{eq:effective_hjb_final}.  The Hamiltonian has the rigid form
\begin{equation}
    H_\theta(x,p,X)=\Tr(D(x,p)X)+H(x,p),
    \qquad H(x,0)=0.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Combine Scale II with viscosity stability.  At a contact point the parameter is evaluated at the test gradient $p=\nabla\varphi$, not at a nonexistent classical gradient of $u^\varepsilon$.  The prelimit feedback is therefore non-circular, and the limiting equation follows from the subsolution and supersolution inequalities proved in \cref{thm:main_homogenization}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Scale IV: deterministic fluctuation equation}

Let
\begin{equation}
    u^\varepsilon=u+\varepsilon w^\varepsilon.
\end{equation}
Expanding the already derived effective Hamiltonian around $(x,\nabla u,\nabla^2u)$ gives the deterministic linearized operator
\begin{equation}
    \mathfrak L_u w
    =-\partial_t w
     -\Tr(D(x,\nabla u)\nabla^2w)
     -\partial_pH(x,\nabla u)\cdot\nabla w
     -\sum_{i,j}\bigl(\partial_pD_{ij}(x,\nabla u)\cdot\nabla w\bigr)\partial_{ij}u .
\end{equation}
At the next order, the quadratic part of the Hamiltonian produces the deterministic viscous Burgers hierarchy
\begin{align}
\label{eq:pde_scale_IV_burgers_hierarchy}
    \mathfrak L_u w
    &-\sum_{i,j}\bigl(\partial_pD_{ij}(x,\nabla u)\cdot\nabla w\bigr)\partial_{ij}w \\
    &-\frac12\sum_{i,j}\bigl(\nabla w^T\partial_{pp}^2D_{ij}(x,\nabla u)\nabla w\bigr)\partial_{ij}u
    -\frac12\nabla w^T\partial_{pp}^2H(x,\nabla u)\nabla w=0 .
\end{align}
This equation is deterministic and is obtained by Taylor expansion of the derived HJB operator.


\section{The microscopic origin of macroscopic non-convexity and non-subadditivity}
\label{sec:nonconvexity}

This section separates two algebraic facts that are often conflated.  A negative Hessian direction proves local non-convexity of $p\mapsto H(x,p)$.  Failure of subadditivity of the induced nonlinear expectation is instead detected by the direct Hamiltonian inequality
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:hamiltonian_superadditive_defect}
    H(x,p+q)>H(x,p)+H(x,q)
\end{equation}
for some $x,p,q$.  The deterministic billiard construction supplies both effects by explicit microscopic potentials, but the second is not inferred merely from the first.

\subsection{Perturbation of the invariant density}

Let $p\mapsto\theta(p)$ be a smooth feedback curve and let the normalized potential contribution be defined by \cref{def:effective_hamiltonian_normalized}.  By \cref{thm:parameter_response}, $H$ is twice differentiable whenever the table and $V$ are sufficiently smooth.

\begin{lemma}[Second derivative formula]
\label{lem:Hessian_formula}
Assume near $p=p_*$ that
\begin{equation}
    V(p,z)=V_0(z)+\ell(z)\cdot(p-p_*)+\frac12(p-p_*)^TA(z)(p-p_*)+O(|p-p_*|^3),
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
    \theta(p)=\theta_*+\Theta_1(p-p_*)+\frac12\Theta_2[p-p_*,p-p_*]+O(|p-p_*|^3).
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:Hessian_formula_corrected}
    \nabla_p^2H(p_*)
    =\langle A\rangle_*
      +\mathcal R_{\mu}(V_0,\ell,\Theta_1,\Theta_2)
      +\nabla_p^2H_{\rm work}(p_*),
\end{equation}
where $\mathcal R_{\mu}$ is the deterministic invariant-density response obtained by differentiating the reduced transfer resolvent, and $H_{\rm work}(x,p):=\Lambda_{x,p}(Q_x\chi_p)$ is the complete covariant horizontal work response in \cref{def:effective_hamiltonian_normalized}.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Differentiate the normalized expression for $H$ twice.  The explicit second derivative of $V$ gives $\langle A\rangle_*$.  Differentiating the invariant density gives the two response terms: one linear in the second parameter derivative $\Theta_2$ acting on $V_0$, and one quadratic in the first parameter derivative $\Theta_1$ acting on the first and second reduced-resolvent derivatives.  These are precisely the terms collected in $\mathcal R_\mu$.  The corrector contribution is differentiated by the identity
\begin{equation}
    \partial_p(-\Acal_{\Theta(x,p)})^{-1}
    =(-\Acal)^{-1}(\partial_p\Acal)(-\Acal)^{-1}
\end{equation}
on the moving zero-mean bundle, together with the derivatives of the centered projection.  The normalization subtracts $H_{\rm raw}(x,0)$ and therefore contributes no Hessian at $p_*$ unless $p_*=0$ and the chosen gauge is moved; in the fixed gauge used here it is a deterministic additive reference.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Directional non-convexity criterion]
\label{thm:non_convexity_proven}
If there exist $x$, $p_*$ and $v\in\R^m$ such that
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:correct_nonconvexity_condition}
    v^T\nabla_p^2H(x,p_*)v<0,
\end{equation}
then $p\mapsto H(x,p)$ is not convex on any neighborhood of $p_*$ containing the segment $p_*+[-\delta,\delta]v$ for sufficiently small $\delta>0$.  In terms of \eqref{eq:Hessian_formula_corrected}, condition \eqref{eq:correct_nonconvexity_condition} is
\begin{equation}
    v^T\langle A\rangle_*v
    +v^T\mathcal R_{\mu}(V_0,\ell,\Theta_1,\Theta_2)v
    +v^T\nabla_p^2H_{\rm work}(p_*)v<0 .
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For $s$ small, Taylor's formula gives
\begin{equation}
    H(x,p_*+sv)+H(x,p_*-sv)-2H(x,p_*)
    =s^2 v^T\nabla_p^2H(x,p_*)v+o(s^2).
\end{equation}
If the displayed quadratic coefficient is negative, Jensen convexity along this line segment fails for all sufficiently small nonzero $s$.  This proves local non-convexity.  The formula in terms of microscopic data follows from \cref{lem:Hessian_formula}.
\end{proof}



\begin{assumption}[Verified non-trivial response direction for the example]
\label{lem:nontrivial_geometric_response_direction}
For the particular admissible billiard/port family used in the example, assume
there is a $C^r$ one-parameter normal deformation $\theta_s$ remaining in the
admissible parameter window and a branchwise $C^{r-4}$ finite-response
observable $W$ such that
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:first_response_nonzero_W}
    L(W):=\left.\frac{d}{ds}\right|_{s=0}\int W\,d\mu_{\theta_s}\ne0.
\end{equation}
Assume also that this derivative is the normalized invariant-suspension
response supplied by \textup{(A3)}, including roof, dynamics, normalization,
and moving-singularity contributions. This is not asserted for every table.
\end{assumption}


\begin{proposition}[Conditional response-generated non-convexity and non-subadditivity]
\label{thm:constructive_nonconvex_example}
Assume \textup{(A1)--(A5)}, the verified response direction in
\cref{lem:nontrivial_geometric_response_direction}, and a mechanically
compatible port that realizes the bounded feedback curve used below. For any
unit vector $e\in\R^m$, if that port admits the cubic--quartic scalar profile,
then the normalized Hamiltonian is non-convex and satisfies the direct defect
\eqref{eq:hamiltonian_superadditive_defect}. More precisely, along $p=re$ one
obtains
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:response_generated_cubic_quartic_profile}
    H(x,re)=\alpha r^3-\kappa r^4+O(r^6),
    \qquad \alpha>0,
    \qquad \kappa>0,
\end{equation}
where both coefficients are deterministic invariant-density response
coefficients of the billiard table under a finite-response feedback.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Let $\theta_s$ and $W$ be given by
\cref{lem:nontrivial_geometric_response_direction}, and replace $W$ by $-W$ if
necessary so that $L(W)>0$.  Choose functions
$Q_3,Q_4\in C_b^\infty(\R)$ with bounded derivatives of all orders and with
\begin{equation}
    Q_3(r)=r^3,
    \qquad
    Q_4(r)=r^4
    \qquad (|r|\le R),
    \qquad
    Q_3(0)=Q_4(0)=0 .
\end{equation}
For $r=p\cdot e$ define the table feedback by
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:odd_even_response_feedback_curve}
    \Theta(x,p)=\theta_{\eta_3Q_3(r)-\eta_4Q_4(r)},
\end{equation}
where $\eta_3,\eta_4>0$ are chosen small enough that the entire curve of tables
stays in the admissible parameter window. This feedback is finite-response
because the $Q_j$ are bounded with bounded derivatives and because its
realization is part of the specified compatible-port hypothesis; no arbitrary
finite-jet realization theorem is invoked.
Choose a centered drift $b_0(z)$ independent of $(x,p)$ in the working chart and
with no $x$-dependence, so $Q_x\chi_p=0$ and the complete horizontal work response in $H$ vanishes
along the line $p=re$.  The Green--Kubo tensor is irrelevant for the direct
Hamiltonian defect because the terminal test functions used in
\cref{cor:non_subadditivity} have zero Hessian at the chosen point.

Take the microscopic potential to be
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:odd_even_nonconvex_potential}
    V(x,p,z)=W(z)
\end{equation}
on the working chart, with the harmless global finite-response saturation of
\cref{def:finite_response_coupling}.  Thus the potential has no explicit
cotangent dependence near the jet interval; all displayed powers of $r$ below
come from the feedback of the deterministic billiard table.

By \textup{(A3)} applied to the independently verified geometric deformation,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:response_taylor_first_order_constructive}
    \int W\,d\mu_{\theta_s}
    =\int W\,d\mu_{\theta_0}+L(W)s+O(s^2),
\end{equation}
with the remainder controlled by the differentiated transfer resolvent.  Using
\eqref{eq:odd_even_response_feedback_curve} and the normalization $H(x,0)=0$,
for $|r|\le R$ we obtain
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:constructive_H_expansion_oddeven}
    H(x,re)
    =L(W)\eta_3 r^3-L(W)\eta_4 r^4
      +O\bigl((\eta_3r^3-\eta_4r^4)^2\bigr).
\end{equation}
Put $\alpha=L(W)\eta_3$ and choose $\eta_4$ so that
$\kappa=L(W)\eta_4$ is positive and the deformation remains geometric.  Then
choose $0<\eta_3\ll\eta_4$ so that
$r_*:=3\alpha/(4\kappa)$ lies in $(0,R/2)$ and the $O(r^6)$ remainder in
\eqref{eq:constructive_H_expansion_oddeven}, together with its second derivative,
is dominated on $[0,R]$ by the displayed cubic--quartic terms.  At
$r=r_*$,
\begin{equation}
    \frac{d^2}{dr^2}\bigl(\alpha r^3-\kappa r^4\bigr)\Big|_{r=r_*}
    =-\frac{9}{4}\frac{\alpha^2}{\kappa}<0,
\end{equation}
and therefore \cref{thm:non_convexity_proven} gives local non-convexity.

For the direct Hamiltonian defect, take $a>0$ with $2a\le R$ and
$a<\alpha/(4\kappa)$, after reducing $a$ further so that the $O(a^6)$ term is
smaller than $\alpha a^3$.  Then
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:response_driven_superadditivity_defect}
    H(x,2ae)-2H(x,ae)
    =6\alpha a^3-14\kappa a^4+O(a^6)>0 .
\end{equation}
Thus \eqref{eq:hamiltonian_superadditive_defect} holds.  The leading defect is
the response-generated cubic term, not an explicit quadratic term in the
microscopic potential and not an effective Hamiltonian coefficient inserted by
hand.  The construction uses bounded finite-response microscopic data fixed
before the transfer operator, the cell problem, and the effective HJB equation.
\end{proof}


\begin{proposition}[Algebraic status of the conditional example]
\label{prop:triangular_bump_no_effective_encoding}
Under the realization and response hypotheses of
\cref{thm:constructive_nonconvex_example}, the coefficients
\(\alpha\) and \(\kappa\) in
\eqref{eq:response_generated_cubic_quartic_profile} are not chosen as effective
Hamiltonian data; they are the first response coefficients
\begin{equation}
    \alpha=L(W)\eta_3,
    \qquad
    \kappa=L(W)\eta_4
\end{equation}
produced by the verified normalized invariant response. This proposition does
not assert that every billiard admits such a port.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For the specified port, map the scalar feedback
\(s=\eta_3Q_3(p\cdot e)-\eta_4Q_4(p\cdot e)\) to this normal deformation.  This
is a mechanically compatible finite-dimensional prescription by hypothesis.
The observable \(W\) is supported in the same rectangle and is fixed before the
invariant density is differentiated.  The value \(L(W)\) is then computed by
the moving-singularity response formula; it is not part of the input.  The
triangular choice of \(Q_3,Q_4\) only separates the odd and even powers in the
cotangent feedback so that the Taylor expansion has a cubic term creating the
subadditivity defect and a quartic term creating a negative second derivative
at an interior point. Thus the algebra does not insert the effective
coefficients by hand, while existence of the response direction and compatible
realization remains an explicit hypothesis.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Non-subadditivity of the induced expectation]
\label{cor:non_subadditivity}
If the derived Hamiltonian satisfies \eqref{eq:hamiltonian_superadditive_defect} at some $x,p,q$, then the nonlinear semigroup associated with \eqref{eq:effective_hjb_final} is not subadditive. Hence the resulting expectation lies outside the sublinear $G$-expectation class. Under the additional hypotheses of \cref{thm:constructive_nonconvex_example}, its algebraic profile satisfies this condition.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Assume, for contradiction, that the semigroup is subadditive for all smooth terminal data.  Work in a coordinate chart around $x$ and choose smooth periodic terminal functions $\phi_p,\phi_q$ whose gradients at $x$ are $p$ and $q$ and whose Hessians vanish at $x$.  The short-time expansion of the viscosity semigroup, obtained directly from the deterministic generator, gives
\begin{align}
    \Ecal_{T-\tau,T}^\theta[\phi_p](x)&=\phi_p(x)+\tau H(x,p)+o(\tau),\\
    \Ecal_{T-\tau,T}^\theta[\phi_q](x)&=\phi_q(x)+\tau H(x,q)+o(\tau),\\
