    \Ecal_{T-\tau,T}^\theta[\phi_p+\phi_q](x)&=\phi_p(x)+\phi_q(x)+\tau H(x,p+q)+o(\tau).
\end{align}
Subadditivity would imply $H(x,p+q)\le H(x,p)+H(x,q)$ after division by $\tau$ and letting $\tau\downarrow0$, contradicting \eqref{eq:hamiltonian_superadditive_defect}.  Therefore the semigroup is not subadditive.
\end{proof}




\section{Acyclic deterministic derivation and dependency closure}
\label{sec:acyclic_deterministic_closure}

This section records the final logical closure of the first part as part of
the proof.  The chain is forward directed:
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:acyclic_chain_summary}
\begin{gathered}
\hbox{geometry}
\Longrightarrow
\hbox{singular hyperbolicity}\\
\xrightarrow{\textup{(A1)--(A2)}}
\hbox{anisotropic transfer gap}
\Longrightarrow
\hbox{observable resolvent}\\
\xrightarrow{\textup{(A3)--(A5)}}
\hbox{cell correctors}
\Longrightarrow
\hbox{viscosity homogenization}.
\end{gathered}
\end{equation}
The macroscopic equation appears only at the final arrow.  It is not used in the
construction of the prelimit graph, the spectral gap, the cell problem, or the
coefficient response.

\begin{theorem}[No circular dependence in the deterministic proof]
\label{thm:no_circular_dependence_deterministic}
Every object used in \cref{thm:main_homogenization} is obtained before the
homogenized solution $u$ is introduced.  More precisely:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*), wide, labelindent=0pt]
    \item the billiard flow, singular sets, homogeneity strips, invariant
    measure, and Banach bundle are constructed from
    \cref{ass:fundamental_axioms_unified};
    \item the prelimit solution $u^\varepsilon$ is the viscosity-duality
    completion of the deterministic jet graph of
    \cref{lem:deterministic_graph_transform_closure};
    \item the spectral gap and flow resolvent are supplied by
    \textup{(A1)--(A2)} in the forms recorded in
    \cref{thm:spectral_gap_geometry,lem:uniform_nonintegrability_curvature,lem:dolgopyat_contraction_deterministic};
    \item the correctors $\chi_i$ and $\chi^{(2)}$ are obtained by applying the
    reduced observable resolvent to centered deterministic observables;
    \item the effective coefficients $D$ and $H$ are invariant-measure pairings
    of those deterministic correctors;
    \item the homogenized function $u$ is introduced only as the unique
    viscosity solution selected by comparison after the subsolution and
    supersolution inequalities have been proved.
\end{enumerate}
No step in this list assumes the conclusion of a later step.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Items (i) and (ii) are \cref{sec:micro_framework,sec:first_principles_prelimit}.
Their inputs are scatterer geometry, specular reflection, and finite-response
mechanical coupling.  Items (iii) and (iv) are the transfer and cell-problem
  sections: the hypotheses act on densities and observables in the anisotropic
bundle and are independent of the value function.  Item (v) is the algebraic
averaging in \cref{def:GK_tensor,def:effective_hamiltonian_normalized}.  Item
(vi) follows from \cref{prop:coefficient_structure} and the perturbed-test proof
of \cref{thm:main_homogenization}.  The only occurrence of a gradient of a
solution before the final HJB equation is the prelimit cotangent jet in the
branchwise deterministic graph transform and, in viscosity form, the cotangent
component of a test jet.  Neither object is the gradient of the unknown
homogenized solution.  The dependency graph \eqref{eq:acyclic_chain_summary} is
therefore acyclic.
\end{proof}



\begin{theorem}[Conditional support of the main theorem]
\label{thm:maximal_deterministic_support_main}
The convergence theorem \cref{thm:main_homogenization} is supported by the
geometric construction together with \textup{(A1)--(A5)}.  The following display
is a dependency chain, not a claim that every arrow follows from ordinary
curvature and finite horizon:
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:maximal_deterministic_support_chain}
\begin{gathered}
\text{ordinary dispersing billiard geometry}
\Longrightarrow
\text{a.e. singular collision map with branch geometry}\\
\xrightarrow{\textup{(A1)}}
\text{anisotropic spectral gap}\\
\xrightarrow{\textup{(A2)}}
\text{Dolgopyat suspension resolvent}
\xrightarrow{\textup{(A3)}}
\text{moving-singularity response}\\
\Longrightarrow
\text{reduced observable correctors}
\xrightarrow{\textup{(A4)--(A5)}}
\text{full-gradient residual identity}\\
\Longrightarrow
\text{viscosity--duality comparison}
\Longrightarrow
\text{effective HJB and nonlinear semigroup.}
\end{gathered}
\end{equation}
Each arrow is either a geometric construction or an explicitly identified
analytic hypothesis.  The
finite-response port supplies only compact deterministic microscopic action data
and bounded cotangent read-out; it does not supply an effective coefficient, a
macroscopic value function, or a representation of the limiting semigroup.  The
uncertainty tensor $D$, the nonlinear Hamiltonian $H$, and the failure of
subadditivity are therefore conditional outputs of the billiard
transfer-resolvent calculation.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Geometry constructs the a.e. branch map.  The successive analytic arrows are,
respectively, \textup{(A1)}, \textup{(A2)}, \textup{(A3)}, and
\textup{(A4)--(A5)}.  Once those inputs hold, the reduced-resolvent definitions
and coefficient pairings occur in the displayed forward order.

The compact finite-response port appears only in the primitive input layer: it
moves the table and supplies branchwise action read-outs through a fixed
finite-dimensional Hamiltonian mechanism.  All its Taylor coefficients are fixed
before transfer operators are defined.  Since no object produced after the
transfer-resolvent stage is used to define the port, and since the limiting
semigroup is introduced only after the HJB comparison input, the dependency
chain is acyclic.  This acyclicity does not prove any clause of
\textup{(A1)--(A5)}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Closure of the conditional deterministic implication]
\label{cor:no_probability_in_first_part}
The derivation of \cref{eq:effective_hjb_final} uses only deterministic microscopic trajectories, anisotropic transfer estimates, and viscosity stability.  The positive tensor $D$ is a deterministic
Green--Kubo bilinear form represented by the transfer resolvent.  The conclusion
is paired convergence; no Liouville-a.e. microscopic statement is included.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The limiting operations used in the conclusion are Banach-space limits of
transfer resolvents, contour-resolvent limits for the suspension semigroup, and
viscosity half-relaxed limits, under \textup{(A1)--(A5)}.  The representation language introduced in
Part II is a post-derivation representation of the already derived nonlinear
semigroup and is not used in proving \cref{thm:main_homogenization}.
\end{proof}


\part{The Probabilistic Representation}
\label{part:ii}

\section{The \texorpdfstring{\ThetaExpectation{}}{theta-Expectation} as a nonlinear semigroup}
\label{sec:theta_expectation_corrected}

The probabilistic representation begins only after the deterministic derivation is complete.  The primitive object in this part is the normalized nonlinear viscosity semigroup generated by the derived HJB equation.  The normalization $H(x,0)=0$ was obtained in \cref{def:effective_hamiltonian_normalized}; it is the deterministic zero-energy gauge that makes constants genuine constants.

\begin{definition}[The $\theta$-expectation semigroup]
For terminal payoff $\phi\in C(\Mcal)$, let $u$ be the unique viscosity solution of
\begin{equation}
    -\partial_tu-\Tr(D(x,\nabla u)\nabla^2u)-H(x,\nabla u)=0,
    \qquad u(T,x)=\phi(x).
\end{equation}
For $0\le t\le T$, define
\begin{equation}
    \Ecal_{t,T}^\theta[\phi](x)=u(t,x).
\end{equation}
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Foundational properties]
\label{thm:theta_expectation_properties}
The family $\Ecal_{t,T}^\theta$ is constant preserving, cash additive, monotone, and time consistent:
\begin{align}
    \Ecal_{t,T}^\theta[c]&=c,\\
    \Ecal_{t,T}^\theta[\phi+c]&=\Ecal_{t,T}^\theta[\phi]+c,\\
    \phi\le\psi&\Longrightarrow \Ecal_{t,T}^\theta[\phi]\le\Ecal_{t,T}^\theta[\psi],\\
    \Ecal_{s,T}^\theta[\phi]&=\Ecal_{s,t}^\theta[\Ecal_{t,T}^\theta[\phi]].
\end{align}
It is generally nonlinear and, whenever the direct Hamiltonian defect in \cref{cor:non_subadditivity} holds, not subadditive.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
If $u\equiv c$, then $\nabla u=0$, $\nabla^2u=0$, and the PDE reduces to $-H(x,0)=0$, which holds by the deterministic normalization in \cref{def:effective_hamiltonian_normalized}.  The same observation gives cash additivity, because adding a constant to a solution changes neither its gradient nor its Hessian.  Monotonicity and time consistency are consequences of the comparison and uniqueness theorem proved in \cref{prop:coefficient_structure,app:comparison}.  Nonlinearity and failure of subadditivity follow from the Hamiltonian's dependence on $\nabla u$ and from \cref{cor:non_subadditivity}.
\end{proof}

\section{Why a nonlinear martingale problem is not the primitive object}
\label{sec:no_nonlinear_mp}

\begin{theorem}[Linearity obstruction]
\label{thm:linearity_obstruction}
Let $\mathcal G$ be an operator on smooth test functions.  Suppose there exists a single probability law under which
\begin{equation}
    f(t,X_t)-f(0,X_0)-\int_0^t\mathcal G f(s,X_s)\,ds
\end{equation}
is a martingale for every smooth $f$.  Then $\mathcal G$ is additive on this class of tests.  In particular, a genuinely nonlinear Hamiltonian
\begin{equation}
    \mathcal G f=\partial_tf+\Tr(D(x,\nabla f)\nabla^2f)+H(x,\nabla f)
\end{equation}
cannot be the generator of a single classical martingale problem.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
If the displayed process is a martingale for $f$, $g$, and $f+g$, then the difference
\begin{equation}
    \int_0^t\left(\mathcal G(f+g)-\mathcal Gf-\mathcal Gg\right)(s,X_s)\,ds
\end{equation}
is a finite-variation martingale.  It is therefore constant.  Differentiating in $t$ gives additivity along the support of the law.  Varying the initial point gives additivity on the test class.  A nonlinear dependence on $\nabla f$ or $\nabla^2f$ violates this unless the Hamiltonian is accidentally linear.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
This theorem does not enter the deterministic derivation.  It identifies the correct representation layer after the HJB equation has already been obtained: the nonlinear semigroup is primitive, while classical probability laws appear only after fixing and linearizing a payoff.
\end{remark}

\section{Linearization, carr\'e du champ, and It\^o calculus}
\label{sec:linearization_calculus}

Let $u$ solve the effective HJB equation for a fixed terminal payoff.  Define
\begin{equation}
    a^u(t,x)=2D(x,\nabla u(t,x)),
\end{equation}
and, when the coefficients are differentiable,
\begin{equation}
    b^u(t,x)=\partial_pH(x,\nabla u(t,x))
    +\sum_{i,j}\partial_{p}D_{ij}(x,\nabla u(t,x))\partial_{ij}u(t,x).
\end{equation}
The linearized generator acting on a smooth test $f$ is
\begin{equation}
    L^u_tf(x)=\frac12\Tr(a^u(t,x)\nabla^2f(x))+b^u(t,x)\cdot\nabla f(x).
\end{equation}
The carr\'e du champ is
\begin{equation}
    \Gamma^u_t(f,g)=\frac12\left(L^u_t(fg)-fL^u_tg-gL^u_tf\right)
    =\nabla f^Ta^u\nabla g/2 .
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Linearized representation for a fixed payoff]
\label{thm:linearized_representation}
For a fixed payoff $\phi$ and its decoupling field $u$, the increment of the nonlinear semigroup along perturbations of the payoff is represented by the linear parabolic equation generated by $L^u$.  Equivalently, any classical diffusion representation uses the generator $L^u$, which depends on $u$ and therefore on $\phi$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Perturb the terminal condition to $\phi+\delta\eta$ and write $u^\delta=u+\delta v+o(\delta)$.  Expanding the HJB equation to first order in $\delta$ gives
\begin{equation}
    -\partial_tv-L^u_tv=0,
    \qquad v(T)=\eta .
\end{equation}
Thus the derivative of the nonlinear semigroup is represented by the linearized generator.  Since $u$ depends on $\phi$, the representing law is payoff-dependent.
\end{proof}


\begin{theorem}[Degenerate calibrated diffusion representation by deterministic viscosity regularization]
\label{thm:degenerate_calibrated_representation_regularization}
After the deterministic HJB equation has been derived, fix a terminal payoff
whose viscosity solution is the locally uniform limit of smooth solutions
\(u^\delta\) to the uniformly parabolic regularizations
\begin{equation}
    -\partial_t u^\delta
    -\Tr\big((D(x,\nabla u^\delta)+\delta I)\nabla^2u^\delta\big)
    -H(x,\nabla u^\delta)=0,
    \qquad u^\delta(T,\cdot)=\phi^\delta .
\end{equation}
For each \(\delta>0\), the payoff-calibrated linearization has an ordinary
Markov diffusion representation.  As \(\delta\downarrow0\), the corresponding
linear semigroups converge locally uniformly to the nonlinear viscosity
semigroup calibrated by the derived solution \(u\).  This representation is a
post-derivation analytic identity and is never used in Part~\ref{part:i}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For fixed \(\delta>0\), the matrix \(D+\delta I\) is uniformly positive and the
smooth solution \(u^\delta\) freezes the coefficients
\begin{equation}
    a^\delta(x,t)=D(x,\nabla u^\delta(t,x))+\delta I,
    \qquad
    b^\delta(x,t)=\partial_pH(x,\nabla u^\delta)
       +\sum_{i,j}\partial_pD_{ij}(x,\nabla u^\delta)\partial_{ij}u^\delta .
\end{equation}
The resulting operator is linear in the test function, so the standard
parabolic representation applies.  Stability of viscosity solutions and the
Crandall--Ishii modulus already derived in \cref{prop:coefficient_structure}
identify the locally uniform limit with the original degenerate HJB semigroup.
The construction starts only after \(D\) and \(H\) have been obtained from the
billiard transfer calculus; it is therefore not a microscopic probabilistic
proof of homogenization.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Downstream character of all probabilistic representations]
\label{thm:downstream_probabilistic_representations}
Every probabilistic object in Part~\ref{part:ii} is determined by the
deterministic semigroup of \cref{thm:main_homogenization}.  Fixing a terminal
payoff and a regular decoupling field $u$ determines the linearized coefficients
$a^u$ and $b^u$; any diffusion, FBSDE, or Girsanov formula below represents this
already-derived linearized parabolic problem.  If two deterministic microscopic
billiard models produce the same coefficients $(D,H)$ on the relevant jet
range, then all representation-layer objects in this part coincide on that
range.  Changing the payoff changes $u$ and therefore changes the representing
linear law, while the underlying nonlinear semigroup remains the same
deterministic HJB semigroup.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The nonlinear semigroup is the solution operator of the effective HJB equation
whose coefficients were constructed in Part~\ref{part:i}.  Linearizing this
semigroup at a payoff is ordinary differentiation of a deterministic parabolic
equation and gives the operator $L^u$ in
\cref{thm:linearized_representation}.  The diffusion representation, when the
coefficient factorization is nondegenerate on the visited region, is a
representation theorem for this linear operator.  The BSDE identity is It\^o's
formula applied to the same smooth decoupling field, and the Girsanov identity
is an algebraic Hamiltonian shift of the same deterministic generator.  None of
these constructions is used in the proof of \cref{thm:main_homogenization};
they depend only on the derived pair $(D,H)$ and on the selected payoff.
\end{proof}

\section{The \texorpdfstring{$\theta$}{theta}-BSDE representation}
\label{sec:theta_bsde_corrected}

Assume in this representation section that the derived matrix $D$ is uniformly positive definite on the region visited by the decoupling field, and write
\begin{equation}
    \sigma(x,p)\sigma(x,p)^T=2D(x,p).
\end{equation}
The BSDE control variable is
\begin{equation}
    Z_t=\sigma(X_t,\nabla u(t,X_t))^T\nabla u(t,X_t),
\end{equation}
not the gradient itself unless $\sigma=I$.

\begin{theorem}[Decoupled FBSDE for a smooth solution]
\label{thm:fbsde_corrected}
Let $u$ be a smooth solution of the effective HJB equation and set $p(t,x)=\nabla u(t,x)$.  Let $X$ solve
\begin{equation}
    dX_t=b^u(t,X_t)\,dt+\sigma(X_t,p(t,X_t))\,dW_t.
\end{equation}
Define
\begin{equation}
    Y_t=u(t,X_t),\qquad
    Z_t=\sigma(X_t,p(t,X_t))^Tp(t,X_t).
\end{equation}
Then $(X,Y,Z)$ satisfies
\begin{equation}
    dY_t=-g(t,X_t,Z_t)\,dt+Z_t\,dW_t,
    \qquad Y_T=\phi(X_T),
\end{equation}
where, with $p=(\sigma^T)^{-1}Z$,
\begin{equation}
    g(t,x,Z)=H(x,p)-b^u(t,x)\cdot p .
\end{equation}
Conversely, a sufficiently regular Markovian solution of this FBSDE recovers the viscosity solution of the HJB equation.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Apply It\^o's formula to $Y_t=u(t,X_t)$.  The martingale coefficient is $Z_t=\sigma^T\nabla u$.  The drift is
\begin{equation}
    \partial_tu+b^u\cdot\nabla u+\frac12\Tr(\sigma\sigma^T\nabla^2u)
    =\partial_tu+b^u\cdot p+\Tr(D\nabla^2u).
\end{equation}
Using the HJB equation,
\begin{equation}
    \partial_tu+\Tr(D\nabla^2u)=-H(x,p),
\end{equation}
so the drift equals $b^u\cdot p-H(x,p)=-g$.  The converse follows by reversing the calculation and using the Markovian identification $Y_t=u(t,X_t)$.
\end{proof}

\section{The \texorpdfstring{$\theta$}{theta}-Girsanov transformation}
\label{sec:girsanov_corrected}

Girsanov transformations are classical transformations of the linearized law associated with a fixed payoff.  At the nonlinear semigroup level they are encoded by a deterministic Hamiltonian shift and by the corresponding generator defect.

\begin{definition}[Hamiltonian shift and deterministic defect]
For a deterministic drift shift $h(t,x)$, define
\begin{equation}
    H^h(x,p)=H(x,p+h)-H(x,h),
    \qquad
    D^h(x,p)=D(x,p+h).
\end{equation}
The shifted semigroup is generated by
\begin{equation}
    -\partial_tu-\Tr(D^h(x,\nabla u)\nabla^2u)-H^h(x,\nabla u)=0.
\end{equation}
Relative to the unshifted generator, the deterministic defect is
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:Lambda_h_def}
    \Lambda_h(t,x,p,X)
    =\Tr\big((D(x,p+h(t,x))-D(x,p))X\big)
     +H(x,p+h(t,x))-H(x,h(t,x))-H(x,p).
\end{equation}
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Nonlinear Cameron--Martin formula at semigroup level]
\label{thm:nonlinear_cm}
Let $u_h$ be the shifted solution.  Then $u_h$ is equivalently the solution of the unshifted HJB equation with deterministic source $\Lambda_h$ evaluated along its own jet:
\begin{equation}
    -\partial_tu_h-\Tr(D(x,\nabla u_h)\nabla^2u_h)-H(x,\nabla u_h)
    -\Lambda_h(t,x,\nabla u_h,\nabla^2u_h)=0.
\end{equation}
Consequently the shifted expectation is the unshifted nonlinear semigroup with this deterministic generator defect.  When $D$ is independent of $p$ and $H$ is linear-quadratic, \eqref{eq:Lambda_h_def} reduces after linearization to the classical Cameron--Martin--Girsanov drift term.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Substitute the definitions of $D^h$ and $H^h$ into the shifted PDE and add and subtract the unshifted terms $\Tr(D(x,\nabla u_h)\nabla^2u_h)+H(x,\nabla u_h)$.  The remainder is exactly \eqref{eq:Lambda_h_def}.  If $D$ is independent of $p$ and $H(x,p)=\frac12p^Ta(x)p+\ell(x)\cdot p$, then
\begin{equation}
    H(x,p+h)-H(x,h)-H(x,p)=p^Ta(x)h,
\end{equation}
which is the drift-tilt term appearing in the payoff-dependent linearized diffusion.  The exponential density is therefore a representation of this already-derived linearized identity, not an input to the deterministic homogenization.
\end{proof}


\section{Post-derivation representation hierarchy and universality}
\label{sec:prob_hierarchy_corrected}

The four deterministic scales have corresponding representation layers.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*), wide, labelindent=0pt]
    \item Scale I is represented by a linear diffusion with deterministic Green--Kubo covariance.
    \item Scale II is represented by an inhomogeneous diffusion whose coefficients are deterministic functions of $(x,p)$.
    \item Scale III is represented by the nonlinear HJB semigroup and, after fixing a payoff, by the corresponding decoupled FBSDE.
    \item Scale IV is represented by the deterministic viscous Burgers hierarchy \eqref{eq:pde_scale_IV_burgers_hierarchy}; probabilistic formulae are optional linearized representations, not inputs to the derivation.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Computational falsification and reproducibility protocol}
\label{sec:computational_falsification}

The results above are analytic.  This section states a prospective numerical
protocol for testing whether finite, explicitly simulated billiards display the
four computable consequences on which the proposed interpretation relies.  It
reports no experiment and no fitted quantity.  In particular, the thresholds
below are fixed before simulation and are not additional assumptions in any
proof.  A failed threshold falsifies the corresponding \emph{computational
claim} for the preregistered configurations; it does not by itself identify
which analytic hypothesis, discretization, or implementation component failed.

\subsection{Claims, estimands, and decision rules}

Let the collision observable be centered with respect to the numerically
approximated invariant collision measure, and let $D$ and $H$ denote the
coefficient functionals defined in \cref{def:GK_tensor,def:effective_hamiltonian_normalized}.
The tests are fixed as follows.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{C\arabic*}, wide, labelindent=0pt]
    \item \textbf{Correlation and positivity.}  Compare exponential and
    power-law fits to the centered correlation tail by AIC; estimate $D$ both
    by the truncated Green--Kubo sum and by block displacements.  Pass only if
    the exponential fit has smaller AIC, the minimum eigenvalue is at least
    minus three bootstrap standard errors, and the two estimates differ by
    less than $10\%$ in relative Frobenius norm.
    \item \textbf{Coefficient response.}  For regular table and port
    perturbations, compare centered finite differences of $D$ and $H$ at
    $h,h/2,h/4$ with tangent/Ulam reduced-resolvent derivatives.  Pass only if
    the discrepancy decreases at every refinement and the final relative error
    is below $10\%$ (with an absolute-error report when the reference derivative
    is numerically zero).
    \item \textbf{Billiard-to-HJB convergence.}  At
    $\varepsilon\in\{1/4,1/8,1/16,1/32\}$, compare the deterministic prelimit
    value and the monotone-scheme HJB solution on the same space--time grid.
    Report $L^\infty$, relative $L^2$, and the log--log slope.  Pass only if both
    errors decrease over the last three levels and the finest relative $L^2$
    error is below $5\%$.
    \item \textbf{Nonconvexity and non-subadditivity.}  At points fixed in the
    run manifest, estimate the smallest eigenvalue of $D_p^2H$ and
    $\Delta(p,q)=H(p+q)-H(p)-H(q)$.  Pass only if a preregistered Hessian point
    has a bootstrap confidence interval wholly below zero and a preregistered
    pair $(p,q)$ has a confidence interval for $\Delta$ wholly above zero.
\end{enumerate}
All relative errors must state their denominator and the regularization used
near zero.  Confidence levels, block lengths, correlation-fit windows, Ulam
partition sequences, HJB meshes, and the set of $(p,q)$ points are part of the
manifest and may not be chosen after inspecting outcomes.

\subsection{Simulation design and numerical safeguards}

The design uses three periodic finite-horizon tables: a symmetric geometry, an
asymmetric geometry, and a near-grazing stress geometry.  Each is evaluated at
three regular port amplitudes.  Every configuration receives $10^7$
post-burn-in collisions for each of the fixed seeds
$1103,2207,3301,4409,5501$; seeds choose initial conditions and bootstrap
resamples, not the deterministic dynamics.  The event-driven integrator solves
the next-impact equation with grazing-safe bracketing and applies the specular
reflection map.  Each run records energy drift, impact-equation residual,
empirical lower and upper flight-time bounds, homogeneity-strip index, discarded
singular events, burn-in, and effective sample size.

Computation stops after all five seeds reach the collision budget and the finest
two discretizations, or earlier for numerical invalidity: energy drift above
$10^{-8}$, collision residual above $10^{-10}$, effective sample size below
$500$, or Ulam mass-conservation error above $10^{-10}$.  Invalid runs are
reported rather than replaced.  One rerun is permitted only for a documented
implementation bug, with both the original manifest and the patch retained.
Time-shuffled observables, the frozen-table/no-port system, block displacement
versus Green--Kubo estimation, the linear heat equation, and the HJB equation
with $H=0$ are baselines.  The planned ablations remove, one at a time,
moving-boundary response, full-gradient work terms, the second corrector, and
high homogeneity strips; they diagnose sensitivity and never replace the
primary model.

\subsection{Artifact contract and independent rerun}

Reproducibility requires more than a seed list.  A conforming release contains
(i) immutable geometry and port parameters with units and admissibility checks;
(ii) source revision, compiler/interpreter and dependency lockfiles, platform
metadata, and the complete command line; (iii) raw initial conditions,
trajectories or deterministic checkpoints, collision logs, and derived arrays;
(iv) the manifest fixing all choices listed above; and (v) a machine-readable
report mapping each claim to its estimand, uncertainty interval, threshold, and
pass/fail/invalid status.  Every artifact is indexed by a cryptographic hash,
and reports are generated only from hashed inputs.  Raw and derived data remain
separate; preprocessing is deterministic and records parent hashes.

Before long runs, unit tests must verify specular energy conservation,
time-reversal away from singularities, observable centering, Ulam mass
conservation, and convergence of the monotone solver on constant-coefficient
heat and manufactured HJB solutions.  An independent rerun consists of
validating hashes and the environment, executing the unit tests, regenerating
one short trajectory from its initial condition, reproducing one coefficient
estimate from a stored checkpoint, and rebuilding all tables from the raw
artifacts.  This protocol makes numerical evidence auditable without making it
a premise of the analytical argument.

\section{Scope and limitations}
\label{sec:limitations}

The theory is conditional on both the geometric class in
\cref{ass:fundamental_axioms_unified} and the independent analytic package
\cref{ass:analytic_admissibility_package}.  No claim is made here that every
periodic finite-horizon dispersing billiard satisfies that package.  Infinite horizon, cusps, focusing or
flat components, noncompact energy shells, topology-changing table
perturbations, and perturbations that destroy the uniform response constants
are outside the theorem.  Near-grazing trajectories are retained through
homogeneity strips, but the estimates are not uniform under degeneration of the
primitive curvature, horizon, or transversality margins.

The effective equation is a finite-horizon terminal-value statement on the
compact slow manifold.  It does not establish rates of homogenization unless a
rate is stated separately, interchange of infinite-time and homogenization
limits, pathwise convergence for every initial condition, or robustness to
measurement noise.  Positive semidefiniteness of $D$ allows degeneracy; the
FBSDE and Girsanov representations therefore require the additional smoothness
and nondegeneracy assumptions stated in Part~\ref{part:ii}.  Nonconvexity and
non-subadditivity are conditional on verifying the indicated response
coefficients for a mechanically compatible port; they are not consequences of
ordinary billiard geometry.

Finally, the numerical protocol has finite-sample and discretization error.
Bootstrap intervals can be misleading under poorly resolved long correlations;
Ulam derivatives can be biased by a partition that does not resolve moving
singularities; and a monotone HJB solver can converge too slowly for the chosen
grid hierarchy.  The invalidity rules, estimator comparisons, stress geometry,
and ablations expose some of these failures but cannot prove the analytic
theorems.  Conversely, passing all four tests corroborates only the registered
finite configurations and scales, not the entire admissible class.

\section{Conclusion}
\label{sec:conclusion}

We treat the billiard map as a global a.e. collision map with singularities.
Grazing collisions form the singular set around which homogeneous charts are
organized.  The geometric construction defines this dynamics; the spectral
gap, suspension UNI, moving-singularity response scale, coherent prelimit
mechanics, and uniform homogenization closure are assumed in
\textup{(A1)--(A5)}.

The resulting Hamiltonian is affine in the Hessian and may be non-convex in the gradient when the chosen admissible port has the required response coefficients.  The probabilistic part is formulated through nonlinear semigroups and payoff-dependent linearizations.  Within the revised scope in \cref{sec:limitations}, the paper gives a conditional $\theta$-expectation framework: the uncertainty tensor, nonlinear Hamiltonian, and downstream representation calculus follow once the independently stated spectral, response, coherent-prelimit, and homogenization hypotheses \textup{(A1)--(A5)} have been verified for a concrete billiard/port family.  Establishing those hypotheses from ordinary curvature and finite horizon remains outside the present theorem.

\appendix
\renewcommand{\thesection}{A.\arabic{section}}
\setcounter{section}{0}

\section{Analytic verification ledger and effective comparison}
\label{app:comparison}

This appendix replaces the former repeated branch/strip proof templates with a
single dependency ledger.  For any proposed billiard/port family, the following
items must be proved independently, with spaces, domains, and constants fixed
uniformly on the parameter window:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(A\arabic*)}, wide, labelindent=0pt]
    \item quasi-compact map transfer operators with simple invariant
    eigenvalue and a uniform gap on the zero-mean space;
    \item a quantitative non-lattice/UNI estimate and the resulting suspension
    resolvent strip;
    \item closable moving-boundary derivatives, summable singular traces, and
    all intermediate strong-to-weak bounds needed for differentiated resolvent
    words;
    \item one globally coherent prelimit action or generator satisfying the
    reflection, collision-face, reciprocity, and comparison requirements;
    \item uniform paired compactness, contact selection, corrector residual
    convergence, and effective comparison.
\end{enumerate}
Optical identities, homogeneity coordinates, and formal resolvent identities
are useful in such a verification, but none substitutes for these five items.
In particular, positive curvature and finite horizon alone are not used here to
deduce mixing, UNI, response closure, or homogenization closure.

\begin{theorem}[Effective comparison under the closure hypothesis]
Let $D,H$ be the coefficients in
\cref{def:GK_tensor,def:effective_hamiltonian_normalized}.  Under
\textup{(A5)}, if $u$ is a bounded upper-semicontinuous subsolution and $v$ a
bounded lower-semicontinuous supersolution of
\eqref{eq:effective_hjb_final}, with $u(T,\cdot)\le v(T,\cdot)$, then
$u\le v$ on $[0,T]\times\Mcal$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
This is the effective-comparison clause of \textup{(A5)}.  The coefficient
calculation identifies $D$ and $H$, but does not derive the global
Crandall--Ishii modulus from ordinary billiard geometry.  A concrete application
must verify that modulus (or another valid comparison criterion) before invoking
the theorem.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Scope.}
The main theorem is therefore an assumption-to-coefficient-and-limit reduction.
Its falsifiable content is the explicit port compatibility, corrector equations,
Green--Kubo coefficient formula, and full-gradient residual identity to be
checked against a proposed model.  It is not a new unconditional spectral or
homogenization theorem for finite-horizon billiards, and the nonconvexity example
requires the additional verified response and rank hypotheses stated in
\cref{lem:nontrivial_geometric_response_direction,thm:constructive_nonconvex_example}.
